Intercollegiate MRCS Part A Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Galactography to identify the duct and any intraluminal lesion): Spontaneous bloody discharge from one duct can come from a papilloma or another intraductal lesion. Galactography can outline the discharging duct and show filling defects when the source is not otherwise clear. Cytology of discharge has low sensitivity and does not map the duct, while bilateral magnetic resonance imaging is not the specific initial investigation for this focused problem. Delayed mammography and sentinel node biopsy do not identify the bleeding duct.
  2. The correct answer is B (Warm the patient, intravenous fluids, and exposed body surfaces): Hypothermia impairs clotting, slows drug metabolism, increases infection risk, and stresses the heart. Active warming should begin during surgery, including warmed fluids and covered surfaces. Several other wrong options worsen heat loss or mask its effects. Another wrong option delays treatment during the period when complications are developing.
  3. The correct answer is A (Tension pneumothorax causing trapped air and impaired venous return): Severe respiratory distress with absent unilateral breath sounds, distended neck veins, and shock points to trapped pleural air obstructing venous return. Treatment is immediate chest decompression followed by a chest drain, without waiting for a radiograph in this unstable patient. A simple fracture and flail chest do not explain shock by themselves. Haemothorax and tamponade are alternatives that usually lack this precise combination of unilateral absent breath sounds and an expanding chest.
  4. The correct answer is E (Central venous pressure is a pressure measurement that does not directly measure volume): Pressure reflects venous return, ventricular compliance, right heart function and intrathoracic pressure as well as blood volume. A single value therefore cannot prove that volume is normal or predict a response to fluid. Changes in volume can occur with little pressure change, while stiff ventricles or high airway pressure can raise pressure without congestion. Central venous pressure does not directly quantify oxygen delivery, and both compliance and respiratory pressure influence the reading.
  5. The correct answer is E (Hyperechoic structure producing a posterior shadow on ultrasound): Hyperechoic means brighter than surrounding tissue, and a gallstone can block sound to create a dark shadow. This pattern supports a gallstone rather than simple liquid. Several other wrong options use the wrong echo brightness or shadow pairing for the bright object. Several other wrong options incorrectly describe the expected posterior acoustic effect of a dense reflecting structure.
  6. The correct answer is E (Hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis caused by large saline infusion): Normal saline contains a high chloride load, which can lower bicarbonate and produce a metabolic acidosis with a normal anion gap. The history of several litres infused is the key clue. Several other wrong options describe the wrong acid base direction. Another wrong option would require impaired perfusion or another lactate source.
  7. The correct answer is B (Lumbar vessels pass posteriorly and can retract into the muscular spinal region): These segmental vessels lie near the vertebral column, and avulsion can cause major bleeding because the cut end retracts deeply. One wrong option confuses renal arteries with segmental lumbar vessels. Another wrong option wrongly sends intercostal veins into the aorta. Several other wrong options reverse venous drainage and invent a pathway through the spinal canal.
  8. The correct answer is B (Urinary retention caused by an obstructed urinary catheter): A palpable bladder with lower abdominal discomfort and no drainage strongly suggests obstruction at the catheter or outlet. Normal blood pressure and unchanged creatinine make a major perfusion or kidney injury less likely. Tubular injury and prerenal injury would not usually produce a distended palpable bladder, while glomerulonephritis is unrelated to this postoperative picture. Antidiuretic hormone excess can concentrate urine but does not cause bladder distension.
  9. The correct answer is A (The ovarian vessels cross the pelvic brim close to the ureter): The ovarian vessels enter the pelvis at the infundibulopelvic ligament region and cross close to the ureter near the pelvic brim. That proximity makes the vessels a hazard when the ureter is mobilised or a broad ligament pedicle is secured. The obturator canal is tempting because it is also a pelvic side wall landmark, but it contains the obturator nerve and vessels. Identifying both structures before clamping reduces the risk of ureteric injury and major bleeding.
  10. The correct answer is B (Heparin-induced thrombocytopenia causing thrombosis with low platelets and antibody activation): Heparin and platelet factor 4 form complexes that antibodies recognise. Platelets become activated and are consumed, so the count falls while thrombosis risk rises. Heparin should be stopped and a nonheparin anticoagulant used, usually guided by local protocols. Heparin overdosage causes bleeding through anticoagulation, not this antibody-mediated prothrombotic state.
  11. The correct answer is B (Hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis caused by gastric acid and chloride loss): Gastric fluid is rich in hydrogen and chloride, so repeated vomiting raises bicarbonate and lowers chloride. Potassium is also lost, and correction usually requires chloride containing fluid with potassium replacement. Small bowel loss tends toward bicarbonate loss and acidosis, while saline load can cause hyperchloraemic acidosis. Ketones and lactate raise the anion gap, and pain related hyperventilation does not explain the high bicarbonate and low chloride.
  12. The correct answer is C (The right coronary artery in the majority of individuals): It supplies much of the right ventricle and commonly provides the artery to the sinoatrial node. One wrong option describes a vessel directed mainly to the anterior left ventricle and septum. Another wrong option usually supplies the lateral and posterior left ventricle rather than the right ventricular mass. Several other wrong options describe a short trunk or a distal branch rather than the main vessel with this typical territory.
  13. The correct answer is B (A smaller tidal volume reduces effective alveolar ventilation relative to dead space): Part of every breath remains in the conducting airways, so shallow breaths waste a larger share of each tidal volume. Minute ventilation can remain unchanged while alveolar ventilation falls and carbon dioxide rises. A faster rate does not guarantee better carbon dioxide clearance when each breath is small, and dead space does not deliver extra fresh gas to alveoli. Shallow breathing reduces rather than increases exchange surface exposure.
  14. The correct answer is E (Resuscitate, give treatment antibiotics, and arrange urgent operative source control): Perforation with established contamination is a dirty operative field that requires treatment and urgent control of the leak. Antibiotics support resuscitation but cannot replace closure, repair, or resection of the perforated bowel. Several other wrong options confuse prophylaxis with treatment of established infection. Several other wrong options delay urgent action and therefore risk worsening shock.
  15. The correct answer is B (The right recurrent laryngeal nerve loops below the right subclavian artery): The right nerve leaves the vagus at this loop before ascending toward the larynx, making it vulnerable in right apical dissection. One wrong option assigns the right subclavian loop to the left nerve. Another wrong option puts the right recurrent nerve around the aortic arch, while a further wrong option places the left nerve around the bronchus. Yet another wrong option substitutes the pulmonary trunk for the subclavian artery.
  16. The correct answer is D (Proceed with prompt incision and drainage of the anorectal abscess): A fluctuant, tender perianal swelling with fever is an anorectal abscess and requires prompt surgical drainage. Antibiotics alone rarely cure a formed collection and may allow it to expand or track. A seton is used for a fistula rather than as the primary treatment of a simple abscess. Manometry and magnetic resonance imaging do not replace urgent drainage when the abscess is clinically evident.
  17. The correct answer is D (The cords are named by their position around the second arterial part): The lateral, medial, and posterior cords surround the second part of the axillary artery and take their names from those positions. This relationship lets the surgeon anticipate cord branches during proximal vascular control. The first part relation is tempting because the plexus is nearby, but the cords are defined around the second part. Careful proximal and distal control with nerve identification reduces the risk of combined vascular and neural injury.
  18. The correct answer is C (Ultrasound guided fine needle aspiration of the nodule): A solid hypoechoic nodule with irregular margins has suspicious ultrasound features and requires cytological assessment. Ultrasound guidance improves sampling of the correct part of the nodule. Routine observation could delay diagnosis of thyroid cancer, while surgery before cytology may be unnecessary. Radioiodine treats functional thyroid disease rather than providing a tissue diagnosis, and calcitonin alone does not assess this nodule adequately.
  19. The correct answer is E (Hold empagliflozin before surgery and monitor ketones if unwell): Sodium glucose cotransporter 2 inhibitors can cause ketoacidosis with only modest glucose elevation around surgery. Local policy should guide the exact stop time, and illness should trigger ketone assessment. Several other wrong options continue medicines without addressing the specific surgical risk. Several other wrong options make broad changes that may cause poor glucose control instead of a targeted plan.
  20. The correct answer is A (Laparoscopic cholecystectomy during the same admission by a fit patient): Early cholecystectomy after mild gallstone pancreatitis reduces the risk of another gallstone related attack while the patient is already in hospital. A fit patient without ongoing severe illness is suitable for this approach. Routine delay for six weeks increases the interval during which pancreatitis or cholangitis can recur. Duct instrumentation, percutaneous drainage and discharge without follow up do not remove the gallbladder or address the underlying stone risk.
  21. The correct answer is C (Thoracodorsal nerve running with subscapular vessels toward latissimus dorsi): The thoracodorsal nerve descends along the posterior axillary wall toward latissimus dorsi and often travels with the thoracodorsal vessels. Preserving it maintains shoulder adduction and internal rotation, which matter for overhead and pulling tasks. The long thoracic nerve is tempting because it is another axillary danger nerve, but it lies on serratus anterior and causes scapular winging. The thoracodorsal bundle should be traced and protected when oncologic clearance permits.
  22. The correct answer is B (Malignancy causing a distal ileal transition point and weight loss): A virgin abdomen makes simple adhesive obstruction less likely, while weight loss and a change in bowel habit raise concern for a tumour. The distal ileal transition point requires assessment for small bowel or caecal malignancy and may need surgical management. A reduced hernia could cause obstruction, but persistent symptoms and a fixed transition point point more strongly toward a mass. Crohn disease and gallstone ileus can obstruct the bowel, but the prolonged constitutional symptoms favor malignancy.
  23. The correct answer is B (Paediatric assessment triangle with poor appearance as priority): Appearance, work of breathing, and circulation can be observed before detailed examination and give an early severity signal. Poor interaction, weak tone, grunting, and cool hands are concerning. One wrong option misattributes illness to age, and another wrong option delays breathing and circulation checks. Several other wrong options rely on late or irrelevant testing before urgent treatment.
  24. The correct answer is D (Give cefazolin within the hour before incision and repeat during long surgery): Prophylaxis must produce adequate tissue levels when the incision opens and while the wound remains exposed. Prolonged procedures can allow drug levels to fall, so local policy may require another dose. Several other wrong options use wrong timing and unnecessary postoperative continuation. Several other wrong options either omit needed cover or use an inappropriate prolonged oral regimen.
  25. The correct answer is A (Increase fibre and fluids, use stool softeners and topical anaesthetic): A superficial anal fissure often heals when stool is softened and pain is controlled so that normal defecation resumes. Fibre, fluids, stool softeners and a topical agent are appropriate first line measures. Surgery or botulinum toxin is considered when a fissure persists despite conservative care. Antibiotics, bed rest and magnetic resonance imaging are not indicated for a simple tear without infection or another complication.
  26. The correct answer is C (Local anaesthetic systemic toxicity with early neurological features): Taste change, tinnitus, and perioral numbness are classic early neurological warning signs of local anaesthetic systemic toxicity. Treatment requires stopping injection, calling for help, supporting airway and circulation, and using local protocol. Several other wrong options do not explain this coherent sequence of neurological symptoms. Several other wrong options have different triggers and clinical patterns.
  27. The correct answer is D (Secondary intention with granulation tissue filling a tissue gap): Healing by secondary intention occurs when wound edges cannot be apposed and the defect must fill with granulation tissue. New capillaries, fibroblasts and inflammatory cells form this tissue before epithelial cover develops. The process takes longer and leaves a larger scar than primary healing. Keloid formation is an abnormal scar outcome, not the normal filling of a tissue defect.
  28. The correct answer is B (Delay enteral feeding until perforation and severe ischaemia have been treated): A rigid abdomen, absent motility, large aspirates and evidence of ischaemia make enteral feed unsafe because the gut cannot absorb it and perforation is possible. Feeding can increase distension, vomiting and aspiration risk in this setting. A stomach that produces fluid is not proof of useful absorption, and early feeding does not prevent every mucosal injury. Enteral nutrition is valuable when the gut works, but not when obstruction, perforation or severe ischaemia is present.
  29. The correct answer is A (Creatinine production and renal clearance change slowly, and low muscle mass reduces the baseline): Creatinine comes from muscle and is cleared by the kidney, so serum concentration may take time to reflect a new fall in filtration. Frail patients with little muscle can have lower baseline values despite reduced kidney function. Urine output can reveal injury before creatinine has reached a new steady level. Liver clearance, dietary salt and red cell haemolysis are not the main determinants.
  30. The correct answer is C (Fluid resuscitation and urgent enema reduction under appropriate care): A stable child without peritonitis or perforation can first be treated with enema reduction after resuscitation and specialist preparation. Delay increases ischaemic risk. One wrong option reserves surgery for failed reduction or concerning findings, while another wrong option discharges an evolving obstruction. Several other wrong options neither reduce the intussusception nor provide urgent control.
  31. The correct answer is C (Anastomotic strength is low early, and poor perfusion and infection impair healing): A newly formed anastomosis depends on a fragile early collagen scaffold while it gains strength during remodelling. Poor perfusion reduces oxygen and nutrition, and infection adds tissue destruction and inflammatory stress. Together these factors increase the risk of dehiscence during the first week. Wound strength is not maximal early, and epithelial cover does not make the deep collagen layer immune to failure.
  32. The correct answer is C (Retropubic venous plexus between the prostate and pubis): The space of Retzius contains a rich venous plexus between the pubis and prostate, and this area is opened during a retropubic approach. Bleeding here can collect in the pelvis and cause early postoperative shock without widespread peritoneal signs. Superficial abdominal vein bleeding is tempting after a lower abdominal incision, but it would usually appear at the wound rather than produce a deep pelvic collection. Prompt packing and direct control of the retropubic plexus are required.
  33. The correct answer is B (Apply a pelvic binder around the greater trochanters and limit movement): A suspected unstable pelvis can bleed heavily from bone and veins, so a binder placed at the greater trochanters helps close the pelvic volume and reduce movement. Keep it in place during transfers and continue blood product resuscitation when indicated. Repeated springing can worsen bleeding. Removing support for imaging, giving oral fluids, or using lavage first delays the key early intervention.
  34. The correct answer is A (Reduced myocardial contractility and impaired catecholamine responsiveness during severe acidaemia): Severe acid loads depress ventricular contraction and weaken the vasopressor response, so shock can worsen even when oxygen is supplied. Treatment therefore focuses on restoring perfusion and correcting the cause while supporting ventilation and circulation. Acidosis does not help ejection by raising resistance, and improved oxygen unloading cannot compensate for falling cardiac output. Renal bicarbonate loss would worsen acidosis rather than rapidly correct it, and direct cardiac stimulation is not the dominant effect.
  35. The correct answer is D (Major pelvic fracture bleeding from venous and bony surfaces): An unstable pelvic fracture can cause major concealed bleeding into the pelvis even when FAST shows no free intraperitoneal fluid. Falling haemoglobin and ongoing hypotension support haemorrhage from the pelvic injury, and treatment includes binder support, blood products, and definitive bleeding control. Bowel perforation and surface bruising do not cause rapid major loss. Chest injury has been excluded, and tamponade is not described.
  36. The correct answer is D (Acute cellular rejection caused by recipient T cells against the graft): Cellular rejection usually develops over days to weeks as recipient T cells recognise donor antigens. In the liver, immune attack can injure bile ducts and vessels, producing fever and abnormal liver tests. Hyperacute rejection occurs almost immediately through preformed antibodies, and chronic rejection develops over a much longer period. Graft-versus-host disease attacks host tissue after donor immune cells are transplanted.
  37. The correct answer is D (The right crus is longer and arises from the upper lumbar vertebrae): The right crus is longer and has a broad upper lumbar origin, which is useful when identifying the crura during hiatal repair. One wrong option reverses the usual length difference. Another wrong option incorrectly shortens the right crus and gives it an isolated central tendon origin. Several other wrong options incorrectly equalize or reverse the crural anatomy.
  38. The correct answer is B (Primary respiratory acidosis with renal bicarbonate retention reducing the pH change): Chronic carbon dioxide retention is the primary problem, and the kidneys slowly retain bicarbonate to limit the fall in pH. The high bicarbonate therefore fits compensation rather than a separate metabolic alkalosis. Metabolic alkalosis would raise pH and usually lower ventilation, while respiratory alkalosis would lower carbon dioxide below normal. A metabolic acidosis would lower bicarbonate and drive ventilation upward rather than produce chronic carbon dioxide retention.
  39. The correct answer is A (Ischial spine palpated through the lateral vaginal wall): The pudendal nerve crosses close to the ischial spine after leaving the greater sciatic foramen and before entering the lesser sciatic foramen. The ischial spine is therefore the key landmark for a transvaginal pudendal nerve block. The ischial tuberosity is tempting because it is nearby, but it lies farther from the main nerve trunk and is less useful for this approach. Accurate placement blocks perineal sensation and reduces pain without requiring deep blind injections.
  40. The correct answer is A (Recurrent median branch supplying the thenar muscles after passing laterally): The recurrent median branch leaves the median nerve near the distal carpal tunnel and curves toward the thenar muscles. It can be compressed by a tight transverse carpal ligament or by a separate fibrous edge. Anterior interosseous nerve compression is tempting in proximal median disorders, but it causes weak pinch without the sensory pattern described. Recognising thenar wasting matters because delayed decompression may leave persistent thumb weakness.
  41. The correct answer is B (Use an airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure sequence): A structured ABCDE review finds and treats the most immediate threats in a safe order. Airway and breathing precede circulation, and reassessment follows each intervention. One wrong option treats circulation before confirming oxygenation. Several other wrong options miss immediate threats or delay action.
  42. The correct answer is C (Arrange specialist drainage of infected necrosis through the safest feasible route): Infected necrosis and a gas-containing collection require drainage and debridement, often in a staged specialist approach. The route depends on anatomy, expertise, and local resources rather than on one universal operation. Several other wrong options leave the collection uncontrolled. Several other wrong options impose an extreme procedure or treatment change without adequate evidence.
  43. The correct answer is E (Destruction of the supporting framework beyond the capacity for regeneration): Regeneration requires surviving specialised cells and an intact scaffold to guide reconstruction. When severe infection destroys both, fibroblasts fill the defect with collagen and a fibrous scar forms. Preserved architecture and rapid regeneration would favour restoration rather than fibrosis. Infection control and restored perfusion support healing but cannot recreate a framework that has already been lost.
  44. The correct answer is C (Resuscitate at once while preparing for urgent exploratory laparotomy): Shock, confusion, abdominal distension, and free fluid after a gunshot wound indicate serious intra-abdominal injury with likely haemorrhage or contamination. Urgent laparotomy provides source control while blood products and warming continue. Computed tomography or observation risks deterioration in an unstable patient. Closing the wound or performing lavage before calling the team delays definitive care.
  45. The correct answer is C (Low haemoglobin reduces oxygen carrying capacity despite normal saturation): Saturation describes the percentage of available haemoglobin carrying oxygen, not the total amount transported each minute. Severe anaemia lowers arterial oxygen content even when saturation and cardiac output remain normal. Supplemental oxygen adds dissolved oxygen but cannot replace missing haemoglobin carrying capacity on its own. A rising lactate suggests inadequate delivery or impaired use, while normal cardiac output does not guarantee sufficient compensation.
  46. The correct answer is D (Keloid scar with growth beyond the original wound margin): A keloid extends outside the original wound and commonly recurs after removal. Excessive collagen deposition creates a firm raised lesion in predisposed patients. A hypertrophic scar can also be raised but remains within the wound and tends to improve with time. Contracture causes shortening across a joint, while normal primary healing leaves a thin scar.
  47. The correct answer is E (Low serum phosphate caused by intracellular shift and metabolism): Refeeding increases insulin release and drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium into cells, with low phosphate being a characteristic marker. Feeding should start cautiously with electrolyte monitoring and correction. Several other wrong options have the wrong direction for the main metabolic shift. Several other wrong options may occur in other states but do not define the classic presentation.
  48. The correct answer is A (Resuscitate and correct fluid and electrolyte abnormalities first): Vomiting of gastric acid causes hypochloraemic, hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis, and anaesthesia is safer after correction. Fluids and electrolytes should be rechecked during treatment. One wrong option ignores a major physiological risk, while another wrong option uses an inappropriate oral route in a vomiting infant. Several other wrong options delay definitive care or use unsafe fluid monitoring.
  49. The correct answer is D (Anterior axillary nodes along the lateral pectoral border): Most lymph from the upper outer quadrant drains first toward the axillary nodes, especially the anterior group along the pectoral border. That pathway makes axillary sentinel node biopsy the usual mapping target for this tumour. Internal thoracic nodes are tempting because part of the breast drains medially, but they are not the primary first echelon for this quadrant. Correct tracer placement maps the tumour's regional drainage rather than every potential nodal basin.
  50. The correct answer is E (The left gonadal vein draining into the left renal vein): This route can transmit increased left renal pressure into the gonadal drainage and contribute to a varicocele that persists when lying flat. One wrong option confuses the vein with an artery and gives the wrong origin. Another wrong option swaps the drainage to the right side, while a further wrong option describes the typical right gonadal vein. Yet another wrong option confuses the renal artery with the venous anatomy responsible for this pattern.
  51. The correct answer is E (Resuscitate and proceed promptly to surgical repair of the perforation): Free intraperitoneal gas with board-like rigidity after sudden severe pain indicates a perforated hollow viscus, most likely a peptic ulcer in this patient. Intravenous fluids, analgesia, broad antibiotics and early operative repair are needed to control contamination and prevent worsening sepsis. Endoscopic clipping is not a reliable treatment for a free perforation with generalised peritonitis. Observation, drug treatment alone or delay for computed tomography can worsen an immediately life threatening problem.
  52. The correct answer is D (A low ionised calcium concentration measured in the blood sample): Ionised calcium is the physiologically active fraction and remains interpretable when albumin is low. The symptoms and prolonged QT interval also suggest low extracellular calcium. Correcting total calcium can estimate the ionised fraction, but adding albumin tissue to the sodium result is not a valid calculation method. Phosphate, total calcium and magnesium provide useful context but cannot confirm the active calcium level when protein binding is abnormal.
  53. The correct answer is E (Use an independent interpreter now to support informed decision making): Valid consent needs information the patient can understand, and an independent interpreter reduces error and pressure. Urgent care can proceed when needed, but discussion should still occur with the alert patient. Several other wrong options bypass reliable communication. Several other wrong options delay or omit consent unnecessarily.
  54. The correct answer is C (Dry gangrene caused by prolonged ischaemia without infection): Severe arterial disease can mummify tissue, producing a dry, black toe with a relatively clear boundary. The absence of swelling and foul discharge supports dry gangrene rather than infection. Wet gangrene develops when ischaemic tissue becomes infected and oedematous, while gas gangrene follows toxin-producing anaerobes. Caseous and fibrinoid are necrosis patterns, not standard clinical types of gangrene.
  55. The correct answer is C (Metabolic alkalosis from vomiting with added lactic acidosis from sepsis): Repeated gastric loss raises bicarbonate, while fever and hypotension suggest sepsis with lactate accumulation. These opposing metabolic processes can leave pH near normal while both bicarbonate and the anion gap are abnormal. Pure lactic acidosis would lower bicarbonate rather than raise it, and pure respiratory disorders do not directly explain the widened gap. Bicarbonate loss would lower bicarbonate, and fever related hyperventilation alone would not create the metabolic pattern.
  56. The correct answer is D (Assess capacity for the foot decision after support, considering understanding, retention, weighing and communication): Capacity is decision-specific, so delirium does not automatically remove the ability to make every choice, and practical support must be attempted. The assessment must test understanding, retention, use or weighing, and communication for this treatment decision. Several other wrong options rely on blanket assumptions about delirium, relatives, or timing. Another wrong option treats an early statement as settled despite evidence that the man cannot weigh the decision consistently.
  57. The correct answer is A (Latissimus dorsi, pectoralis major, and a horizontal line at nipple level): The triangle is bordered by the anterior edge of latissimus dorsi, the lateral edge of pectoralis major, and a line above the nipple. One wrong option substitutes serratus anterior and trapezius, which lie outside the standard boundaries of this triangle. Another wrong option confuses the anterior chest wall muscles with pectoralis minor and the clavicle, which do not define the safe triangle. Several other wrong options use abdominal or shoulder muscles that are unrelated to the usual axillary entry zone.
  58. The correct answer is C (Parafollicular C cells producing calcitonin and related hormones): Medullary thyroid carcinoma arises from the calcitonin producing parafollicular C cells. A raised calcitonin level, cervical nodes and a family history of thyroid and adrenal tumours suggest a hereditary syndrome such as multiple endocrine neoplasia. Follicular cells produce thyroid hormone and thyroglobulin, while papillary architecture belongs to papillary carcinoma. Parathyroid cells and thyroid stromal cells do not give rise to medullary carcinoma.
  59. The correct answer is C (The intercostal neurovascular bundle within the groove of the upper rib): The bundle runs in the subcostal groove on the lower border of the rib above, so passing close to that border can tear the artery, vein, or nerve. One wrong option mistakes the muscle layer for the protected bundle and underestimates the deeper vascular risk. Several other wrong options name superficial or distant nerves that do not occupy the subcostal groove and would not be injured by this trajectory. A further wrong option describes the phrenic nerve on the mediastinal surface, which is far from a lateral intercostal skin entry.
  60. The correct answer is A (The hepatic veins drain directly into the inferior vena cava): The upper posterior liver contains large hepatic veins entering the caval junction, so injury there can cause rapid major venous bleeding. One wrong option sends portal flow to the right atrium instead of the liver sinusoids. Another wrong option invents a cystic route for all hepatic drainage. Several other wrong options reverse or misplace splenic and gastric venous anatomy.
  61. The correct answer is B (Restore physiology first and perform definitive repair after stabilisation): Damage control surgery prioritises rapid control of catastrophic bleeding and contamination, temporary closure, warming, resuscitation, and correction of clotting before planned definitive reconstruction. Complex repair during severe physiological derangement increases the risk of death. Bleeding must not be left uncontrolled while waiting for laboratory values to normalise. Temporary packing and planned reoperation are deliberate parts of this approach.
  62. The correct answer is D (Sixty-five millilitres per hour using the 4, 2, 1 rule): The first ten kilograms contribute forty, the next ten contribute twenty, and the final five contribute five, giving sixty-five millilitres hourly. This is a starting estimate that needs review against losses and clinical state. One wrong option counts only the first band, and another wrong option uses an unsupported adult threshold. Several other wrong options apply arbitrary multipliers instead of the stated rule.
  63. The correct answer is D (The spleen lying immediately beneath the left hemidiaphragm in the upper abdomen): A low left thoracic port can pass through the diaphragm into the left upper quadrant, where the spleen is at immediate risk. One wrong option names the organ expected on the opposite side, and another wrong option describes a right retroperitoneal structure. A further wrong option places the caecum far below a low left intercostal entry. Yet another wrong option crosses the abdomen but is not the organ directly beneath the left hemidiaphragm.
  64. The correct answer is D (Start intravenous antibiotics and arrange urgent biliary drainage for obstruction): Cholangitis with obstruction requires antimicrobial support and prompt drainage of infected bile. Blood cultures should be obtained when possible without delaying urgent treatment. Several other wrong options either rely on antibiotics alone or postpone definitive biliary decompression. This allows sepsis to progress despite medication.
  65. The correct answer is E (Aspiration pneumonitis requiring airway support and later imaging): Vomiting with impaired airway reflexes, noisy breathing, drowsiness, and falling oxygen saturation makes aspiration likely. Airway and oxygenation take priority, followed by reassessment and imaging as needed. One wrong option does not explain the witnessed vomiting and noisy breathing. Several other wrong options address the wrong early problem.
  66. The correct answer is A (Observe with repeated examination and immediate access to intervention): A stable patient without peritonitis or active contrast bleeding can often be managed without immediate surgery when monitoring and rapid intervention are available. Repeated examination, blood counts, and a clear escalation plan are essential because splenic bleeding can evolve. Discharge and ignoring the scan are unsafe. Immediate splenectomy and anticoagulation are not justified by the stable grade three injury described.
  67. The correct answer is B (Proceed with resuscitative thoracotomy under the local emergency protocol): Penetrating chest trauma with witnessed arrest and immediate availability of a trained surgical team can justify resuscitative thoracotomy under clear local criteria. The procedure may relieve tamponade or control intrathoracic bleeding and is time critical. Prolonged compression alone without addressing the mechanical cause is inadequate. Thrombolysis, magnetic resonance imaging, and outpatient review do not treat this immediate traumatic arrest.
  68. The correct answer is E (The left brachiocephalic vein crossing behind the thymus and in front of arteries): This large vein crosses the superior mediastinum and is vulnerable to traction during thymectomy. One wrong option describes a right posterior mediastinal vein rather than the anterior crossing vessel. Several other wrong options refer to neck veins that are not the major structure crossing behind the thymus. A further wrong option is a smaller right-sided upper thoracic vein and does not occupy the described central position.
  69. The correct answer is D (Abscess formation with a cavity of pus and surrounding fibrous wall): Persistent neutrophils and organisms can produce a localised collection of pus, especially when drainage is poor. The surrounding tissue attempts to wall off the collection, and the destroyed framework prevents orderly restoration. Complete resolution requires removal of the stimulus and preservation of tissue structure. Primary healing and metaplasia do not describe a pus-filled cavity with tissue destruction.
  70. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary embolism from a proximal lower limb vein thrombus): Sudden breathlessness, pleuritic pain, hypoxia, and tachycardia after major hip surgery are highly concerning for pulmonary embolism. Emergency assessment and treatment should not be delayed. Several other wrong options usually evolve more gradually with other respiratory signs. Several other wrong options have different exposures or absence of an appropriate trigger.
  71. The correct answer is A (Capillary leakage with loss of intravascular plasma volume): Thermal injury increases capillary permeability, so protein rich plasma enters tissue and leaves less volume inside vessels. Haemoglobin concentration can remain near normal early because plasma shifts into tissue without a proportional loss of red cells, so the concentration does not show total circulating volume. Immediate dilution by transfusion fluids would lower haemoglobin, while pump failure would usually produce pulmonary congestion rather than diffuse tissue swelling. Vasodilation and pericardial tamponade alter pressure through other mechanisms, but neither explains the rapid plasma leak into burned tissue.
  72. The correct answer is B (Approximate the right and left crura posterior to the oesophagus): Posterior crural closure narrows the defect behind the oesophagus while preserving the normal relationship of the stomach and vagal trunks. One wrong option places the repair in front and can create obstruction or distort the repair. Another wrong option fails to close the hiatal defect and changes the oesophageal attachment. Several other wrong options sacrifice function or create an excessively narrow opening.
  73. The correct answer is C (The retrocaecal position irritates the flank and nearby ureter): A retrocaecal appendix can lie against the posterior abdominal wall and irritate the ureter or nearby structures. This may shift pain toward the flank and produce urinary frequency without true urinary infection. A pelvic appendix often irritates the rectum and can cause diarrhoea or tenesmus. Anterior, subhepatic and hernial positions produce different patterns, and none matches the computed tomography finding here.
  74. The correct answer is C (Seek specialist advice and set an individual perioperative steroid plan): Long term steroid treatment can impair the stress response, but the correct cover depends on dose, duration, disease, and operation. Specialist advice allows an individual plan for supplementation and return to usual treatment. Several other wrong options risk inadequate steroid exposure. Several other wrong options use unsuitable fixed or inhaled replacement.

One wrong option describes omental anatomy, while another wrong option refers to rectus muscle intersections. A further wrong option is a groin ligament, and yet another wrong option is the linea alba. None of these provide the lateral colonic dissection plane.
  76.   78.   79. The correct answer is E (Check oxygenation, glucose, pain, urine retention, medicines, and infection): New postoperative confusion should prompt a search for treatable causes, including hypoxia, metabolic disturbance, pain, retention, drugs, and sepsis.   80. The correct answer is E (Inappropriate antidiuretic hormone release with excessive water retention): Surgery, pain and nausea can stimulate antidiuretic hormone, which concentrates urine and retains water despite low serum sodium. The absence of dehydration or oedema also fits a modest increase in total body water. Primary water excess should suppress antidiuretic hormone and produce dilute urine, while gastrointestinal sodium loss usually causes extracellular volume depletion. Heart failure and renal sodium wasting do not fit this combination of concentrated urine, normal volume status and normal endocrine screening.
  81. The correct answer is A (Call the experienced airway clinician and prepare to secure the airway): Stridor, agitation, falling oxygen saturation, and facial swelling indicate a rapidly worsening upper airway obstruction. The neck should be supported during management, but a collar must not delay airway care. Dental examination, oral fluids, and outpatient referral are dangerously slow.
  82. The correct answer is A (ASA physical status two with mild controlled systemic disease): ASA physical status two describes mild systemic disease without a major functional limitation. The score helps teams communicate risk but does not replace clinical assessment. One wrong option applies to a healthy patient without systemic disease. Several other wrong options describe progressively more severe disease and functional threat.
  83. The correct answer is D (The superior mesenteric vein joins the splenic vein behind the pancreatic neck): This junction forms the portal vein and is the key landmark for controlling the venous confluence during pancreatic surgery.   84. The correct answer is D (Begin cessation support now and continue it through the operative period): Stopping as early as possible can reduce respiratory and wound complications, even when complete cessation is difficult.   85. The correct answer is B (Apoptosis with cell shrinkage and membrane-bound fragment removal): Apoptosis is programmed cell death in which chromatin condenses and the cell forms membrane-bound bodies. These bodies are removed by phagocytes, so cell contents rarely escape and major inflammation is avoided. Necrosis generally causes swelling, membrane rupture and an inflammatory response. Caseous and fibrinoid necrosis have distinctive tissue patterns that are not described in this specimen.
  86.   89. The correct answer is E (Hyperchloraemic acidosis caused by gastrointestinal bicarbonate loss and high chloride concentration): Profuse diarrhoea removes bicarbonate, so chloride rises relative to sodium and the anion gap stays normal. Acceptable perfusion also makes lactic acidosis less likely. Lactate, ketones, urea and many toxins add unmeasured anions and usually raise the gap, while isolated bicarbonate loss leaves the gap unchanged. The stool history ties the biochemical pattern to the gastrointestinal tract.

  91.   92. The correct answer is D (Gastroduodenal artery posterior to the first part of duodenum): A posterior duodenal ulcer can erode into the gastroduodenal artery and cause brisk upper gastrointestinal bleeding. Splenic, mesenteric and inferior mesenteric branches do not occupy the posterior duodenal wall in the same clinically important way.

  94. The correct answer is E (Loop diuretics can raise urine sodium and make a low value unreliable): Loop diuretics inhibit sodium reabsorption in the thick ascending limb, so urine sodium may be high even when the kidney response is otherwise prerenal. The clinical state, fluid balance, urine output trend and creatinine need more weight than one value after diuretic exposure.   95. The correct answer is A (Basal atelectasis caused by reduced lung expansion and retained secretions): Pain, anaesthesia and supine posture reduce lung volumes after upper abdominal surgery and predispose to small airway collapse.   96. The correct answer is C (Hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy in the severely injured trauma patient): These three problems reinforce one another in severe trauma, because cold and acid impair clotting and poor clotting permits further blood loss and shock. Damage control focuses on warming, restoring perfusion, correcting calcium and clotting, and returning for definitive care after stabilisation. Hyperthermia and alkalosis oppose the usual trauma pattern. The other options list combinations that are not the lethal triad.
  97. The correct answer is E (Antidiuretic hormone inserts aquaporin channels in collecting duct cells): Antidiuretic hormone increases collecting duct water permeability by adding aquaporin channels, so water follows the medullary osmotic gradient. Inappropriate secretion continues this response despite low plasma osmolality and dilutes serum sodium. Aldosterone regulates sodium and potassium transport rather than forming the primary water channel, and atrial natriuretic peptide promotes sodium excretion. Loop diuretics reduce the medullary gradient, and glucose causes osmotic diuresis rather than concentrated urine.
  98. The correct answer is E (Give balanced isotonic crystalloid and reassess fluid status frequently): Early acute pancreatitis causes fluid shifts and reduced circulating volume, so balanced isotonic crystalloid with repeated clinical review is appropriate. Fluid therapy must be adjusted to avoid both under resuscitation and overload. Prophylactic antibiotics are not routine without infection, and hypertonic saline is not the standard fluid. Withholding all fluids, prolonged fasting or waiting for amylase to normalise does not improve early supportive care.

  100.   101. The correct answer is C (Increased atrial natriuretic peptide release promoting sodium and water excretion): Rising atrial pressure stretches the atria and releases atrial natriuretic peptide, which increases renal sodium and water loss. The temporary diuresis after a fluid load fits this response. Aldosterone conserves sodium, and antidiuretic hormone conserves free water rather than promoting sodium excretion. Decreased stretch would reduce the signal, and lower prostaglandin production would more often impair renal blood flow than explain natriuresis.
  102. The correct answer is D (Paget disease of the nipple associated with underlying breast carcinoma): Persistent eczema like nipple change that does not settle with topical treatment can be Paget disease of the nipple. It may occur without a palpable mass and is often associated with an underlying in situ or invasive breast cancer. Benign eczema can affect the nipple, but its failure to respond and the unilateral persistent change require investigation.   103. The correct answer is E (Obstructive shock caused by tension pneumothorax impairing venous return): Air trapped under pressure compresses the mediastinum and great veins, so blood cannot fill the heart effectively. Hyperexpansion, tracheal shift and absent breath sounds identify the obstruction, and decompression restores return. Hypovolaemic shock would not improve with chest decompression, and distributive shock usually presents with warm peripheries rather than unilateral chest signs. Cardiogenic shock affects pump function, while neurogenic shock follows cord injury with bradycardia and loss of sympathetic tone.
  104.   105.   112.   113. The correct answer is E (Critical care support with antithyroid drug, beta blockade, iodine and glucocorticoid): Fever, agitation, marked tachycardia, heart failure and altered mental state in untreated Graves disease indicate thyroid storm.   114.   116.   123.   124. The correct answer is D (Cardiac tamponade caused by fluid in the pericardial sac): Pericardial fluid raises filling pressure, reduces ventricular filling and produces high jugular venous pressure with soft heart sounds.   128. The correct answer is A (Loss of afferent prostaglandin dilation and efferent constriction during reduced perfusion): Prostaglandins normally dilate the afferent arteriole, and angiotensin constricts the efferent arteriole to preserve filtration pressure during low flow. A nonsteroidal drug blocks the first response, the converting enzyme inhibitor blocks the second, and dehydration plus surgery removes reserve. The result is a prerenal pattern that can progress to intrinsic injury. Increased sodium retention, water diuresis and preserved filtration pressure describe the opposite of this drug interaction.
  129.   130. The correct answer is A (Local invasion and metastatic potential rather than tumour size): Malignancy is defined by behaviour such as invasion and metastasis, not by physical dimensions. A small tumour can be malignant if it infiltrates, while a large well-circumscribed lesion can remain benign. Borders and cellular maturity provide useful clues, but no single feature replaces demonstration of invasive behaviour. Organ and age influence risk but do not directly decide whether a tumour is malignant.
  131. The correct answer is A (Branches from S3 and S4 in the sacral plexus): Levator ani receives direct sacral plexus branches, chiefly from S3 and S4 on its pelvic surface.   139. The correct answer is D (Haemorrhagic shock caused by major internal blood loss): An unstable pelvis and tender abdomen after high-energy trauma make concealed bleeding likely, and cool skin, delayed refill, tachycardia, confusion, and a narrowed pulse pressure support haemorrhagic shock.   140. The correct answer is B (Anaplastic thyroid carcinoma causing rapid invasion and recurrent nerve palsy): A rapidly enlarging hard thyroid mass in an older patient with hoarseness and invasion suggests anaplastic thyroid carcinoma. This tumour is aggressive and often spreads locally into the airway, oesophagus and recurrent laryngeal nerve. Papillary cancer often grows more slowly and tends to spread to lymph nodes, while Graves disease is an autoimmune hyperthyroid condition. Hashimoto thyroiditis and medullary cancer can cause thyroid swelling, but they do not typically produce this rapid invasive picture.
  141.   142. The correct answer is C (Reduce tidal volume further if plateau pressure remains above the recommended range): Plateau pressure estimates alveolar distending pressure and should remain within a lung protective range. A high value calls for review of tidal volume, chest wall mechanics, auto positive end expiratory pressure and secretions. Larger tidal volumes can worsen ventilator induced injury, and a higher inspired oxygen fraction is not maintained without a clinical target. Peak airway pressure includes resistive losses, and removing positive end expiratory pressure may destabilise alveoli rather than protect them.
  143. The correct answer is C (Compensatory vasoconstriction and tachycardia can preserve pressure while perfusion falls): Young patients often maintain central pressure by increasing heart rate and constricting peripheral vessels, so organ perfusion can fall before hypotension appears.   144. The correct answer is B (Ischaemia stopping nutrient delivery and allowing waste products to accumulate): Ischaemia interrupts both oxygen and glucose delivery, and retained metabolic waste worsens cell injury. Simple hypoxaemia leaves blood flow intact, so glucose can still reach cells and waste can be removed. Reperfusion can add oxidative injury rather than immediately restoring normal metabolism. Arterial oxygen content alone and venous congestion do not explain the greater injury caused by blocked perfusion.
  145.   146.   229.   230. The correct answer is A (Pulse oximetry cannot distinguish normal haemoglobin from carboxyhaemoglobin reliably): Carboxyhaemoglobin can produce a misleadingly normal or high saturation because many oximeters do not separate it from oxyhaemoglobin. Confusion and metabolic acidosis should prompt co-oximetry and assessment of oxygen delivery. Pulse oximetry does not measure arterial partial pressure or carbon dioxide, and poor peripheral perfusion can reduce accuracy rather than improve it.   231. The correct answer is B (Hyperkalaemia causing impaired ventricular membrane excitation and slowed cardiac conduction): Kidney injury reduces potassium excretion, and the sequence of tall T waves, QRS widening and bradycardia is classic for dangerous hyperkalaemia. Hypocalcaemia lengthens the QT interval, while hypomagnesaemia commonly produces torsades rather than these potassium related changes. Sodium disturbances can cause confusion or seizures, but the traced changes point specifically to potassium.

  238. The correct answer is D (Irreversible injury caused by ATP depletion and membrane pump failure): Severe trauma and hypoxia stop mitochondrial ATP production and impair the sodium-potassium pump. The benefit applies only while the ventricle remains on the useful part of its filling curve.
