Immediate clearance without histological confirmation, imaging alone or PET before tissue sampling is not the preferred sequence for these suspicious nodes.
  294. The correct answer is E (Flail chest with paradoxical movement and underlying pulmonary injury): Multiple rib fractures in more than one place create the mobile segment, which moves inward during inspiration. Oxygen, analgesia, careful fluid balance, and breathing support are central, with ventilation help when needed. Tension pneumothorax, tamponade, and an open wound do not explain the observed chest movement. A single rib fracture does not create this mobile segment or degree of respiratory compromise.
  295. The correct answer is B (It crosses anterior to the aorta before entering the inferior vena cava): The left renal vein has a longer course than the right and usually passes in front of the aorta. One wrong option places it behind the kidney and near the spleen. Another wrong option moves it behind the inferior vena cava. Several other wrong options give an intramuscular or pancreatic pathway and the wrong venous destination.
  296. The correct answer is E (Retroperitoneal haemorrhage that can remain hidden from FAST): Blood behind the peritoneum may cause flank bruising and haemorrhagic shock without producing the free intraperitoneal fluid FAST is designed to find. The computed tomography result and falling haemoglobin link the shock to this bleeding site. Tension pneumothorax, tamponade, spinal shock, and allergy do not fit the imaging and examination pattern. Continue resuscitation and arrange urgent source-specific control.
  297. The correct answer is A (Chronic inflammation with macrophage-rich granuloma formation and ongoing tissue destruction): Persistent organisms can stimulate macrophages and lymphocytes for months, and activated macrophages may fuse into giant cells. This creates granulomatous inflammation with tissue destruction and fibrosis. Acute inflammation is brief and neutrophil-rich, while the described duration and cell pattern do not fit it. The remaining options describe vascular, transfusion or haemostatic events rather than a chronic tissue reaction.
  298. The correct answer is E (Fat necrosis caused by pancreatic enzyme injury to adipose tissue): Leaked pancreatic lipases split triglycerides in fat, and the released fatty acids combine with calcium in a process called saponification. This creates chalky deposits around the pancreas and can lower serum calcium. Coagulative necrosis follows ischaemia in solid organs and does not typically produce these fat deposits. Apoptosis, abscess formation and fibrinoid vascular injury do not explain the operative appearance.
  299. The correct answer is A (Choose a guideline-approved non-beta-lactam regimen and document the reaction): Immediate swelling, wheeze, and collapse suggest a severe IgE-mediated reaction rather than a benign rash. The drug choice should follow the operation, expected organisms, and local allergy guidance, with the history clearly recorded. One wrong option wrongly assumes that all beta-lactams are safe after anaphylaxis. Several other wrong options either omit effective cover or use unsafe and unselective alternatives.
  300. The correct answer is B (Airway, breathing, circulation, disability, and exposure as the initial survey order): The primary survey follows ABCDE so that the problems most likely to kill within minutes are found and treated first. Catastrophic external bleeding is controlled at the circulation step and can require immediate action alongside airway care. Disability covers neurological status and glucose, while exposure allows full inspection without allowing hypothermia. The other sequences place later priorities before airway and breathing.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Empyema requiring antibiotics and effective drainage of the infected pleural space): Frank pus in a loculated collection after pneumonia defines empyema and needs infection treatment plus drainage, sometimes surgical. One wrong option describes a noninfected transudate. Another wrong option concerns pleural air under pressure. Several other wrong options do not account for the pus or the post-pneumonia inflammatory collection.
  2. The correct answer is A (Protease-mediated alveolar wall destruction with reduced elastic recoil): Smoke exposure increases protease activity and inactivates protective antiproteases, leading to alveolar destruction in centriacinar emphysema. Loss of alveolar attachments reduces recoil and allows air trapping and hyperinflation. Mucus gland hypertrophy and airway inflammation describe chronic bronchitis rather than alveolar destruction. Fibrosis thickens walls, while smooth muscle constriction causes bronchospasm without destroying alveoli.
  3. The correct answer is D (Level VI in the central compartment beside the trachea): Level VI is the anterior central compartment bounded by the carotid sheaths and extends from the hyoid to the suprasternal notch. It contains pretracheal and paratracheal nodes that commonly drain thyroid cancer. Level IV is the lower jugular chain lateral to the central compartment. Levels II, III, and IV form the lateral jugular chain rather than the midline visceral space.
  4. The correct answer is B (Sestamibi imaging to identify an overactive parathyroid gland): Primary hyperparathyroidism is supported by high calcium, low phosphate and an inappropriately high parathyroid hormone level. Sestamibi imaging can localise an overactive parathyroid gland before focused neck surgery. Radioiodine scanning is used to assess functional thyroid tissue rather than parathyroid adenomas. Computed tomography and magnetic resonance imaging may have selected roles, but a bone density scan measures bone strength and cannot localise a gland.
  5. The correct answer is A (Superficial partial thickness injury affecting the papillary dermis): Pain, blistering, blanching, and a moist red surface after a scald fit this depth. The papillary dermis remains perfused, so pressure temporarily empties the surface colour and the tissue can heal from dermal appendages. One wrong option would look drier and less blanching, while another wrong option would be leathery, nonblanching, and often painless. Several other wrong options contradict the intact blistering, pain, and red blanching surface described.
  6. The correct answer is E (Physiologic postoperative ileus from temporary coordinated motility failure): Inflammation and anaesthetic effects reduce coordinated motility, producing diffuse distension without a mechanical transition point. One wrong option usually produces colicky pain and a transition zone. Another wrong option would show focal changes or strangulation features. Several other wrong options do not match the diffuse nonischaemic pattern or the expected site of vomiting.
  7. The correct answer is E (Conservative advice with cold avoidance and review for red flag features): Long standing bilateral colour change without ulceration, absent pulses, rash, or weakness fits common benign Raynaud phenomenon. One wrong option treats a local vasospastic pattern as carotid embolism. Another wrong option has no evidence of thrombosis. Several other wrong options expose the patient to major treatment without features of outlet compression or systemic inflammation.
  8. The correct answer is D (Severe ongoing hypokalaemia with hypochloraemia and metabolic alkalosis): Gastric and nasogastric losses remove chloride and hydrogen ions while kidney losses of potassium continue, producing a hypochloraemic hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis. Treatment requires replacing losses and correcting potassium and chloride safely. Several other wrong options predict high chloride and acidosis. Several other wrong options choose the wrong primary electrolyte and acid base pattern.
  9. The correct answer is B (Phlegmasia cerulea dolens causing severe venous obstruction and reduced inflow): Massive iliofemoral thrombosis raises tissue pressure, produces a tense blue leg, and can reduce arterial flow. One wrong option does not fit the imaging evidence of extensive venous clot. Another wrong option is chronic and does not cause an acutely cold foot. A further wrong option lacks the expected skin and systemic infection pattern, while yet another wrong option has no muscle injury described.
  10. The correct answer is A (Urgent surgical exploration rather than waiting for imaging): Sudden pain, high testis, horizontal lie, vomiting, and an absent cremasteric reflex strongly suggest torsion, and testicular salvage falls with delay. Surgery should not wait when imaging is delayed. Several other wrong options postpone time-critical care, while another wrong option wrongly treats a possible vascular emergency as infection. A further wrong option adds an unsuitable delay before senior surgical review.
  11. The correct answer is A (Catheter associated bacteriuria with no clinical evidence of systemic infection): A catheter and cloudy urine can produce signs of colonisation without invasive urinary infection. The absence of flank pain, rigors, hypotension, and local abdominal findings supports a less severe interpretation. Pyelonephritis usually causes flank pain and systemic illness, while an abscess produces deeper inflammatory signs. Retention and urethral injury would produce different voiding or bleeding findings.
  12. The correct answer is C (Beta blockade can mask tachycardia while perfusion signs still reveal bleeding): Beta blockers can blunt the expected heart rate response, but poor perfusion and rising lactate remain important signals. One wrong option ignores that haemoglobin falls later as fluids shift into the circulation. Another wrong option treats one normal pressure reading as proof of adequate volume. Several other wrong options conflict with the clinical evidence of shock.
  13. The correct answer is E (The superior thyroid artery descending toward the upper pole): The superior thyroid artery is typically the first anterior branch of the external carotid artery. It descends toward the upper thyroid pole and is a common feeding vessel for upper neck lesions. The lingual and facial arteries arise at higher levels from the anterior surface. The occipital and posterior auricular branches arise from the posterior aspect of the external carotid artery.
  14. The correct answer is D (Posterior hip dislocation after axial loading through the knee): Shortening, adduction, and internal rotation with the knee crossing the midline are characteristic of a posterior dislocation. A dashboard mechanism can drive force through the knee and push the femoral head backward out of the socket. One wrong option describes the opposite rotational pattern, while another wrong option usually causes thigh deformity rather than this fixed hip posture. Several other wrong options do not reproduce the classic displaced hip position.
  15. The correct answer is C (Offer smoking cessation support and continue routine preoperative preparation): Stopping smoking before elective surgery can reduce respiratory and wound complications. The healed scar needs no repeated postponement when it shows no active infection. Stable treated hypertension does not justify stopping every medicine. An echocardiogram needs a clinical reason rather than routine use.
  16. The correct answer is D (Coarctation of the aorta causing upper body hypertension and weak femoral pulses): Narrowing opposite the ductus produces high arm pressure with delayed femoral flow after the left subclavian origin. One wrong option usually has acute tearing pain and unequal upper limb pulses. Another wrong option does not cause bilateral arm hypertension with weak femorals. Several other wrong options do not explain the arm to leg pulse delay.
  17. The correct answer is A (The brachial plexus trunks posterior to the subclavian artery): The brachial plexus lies lateral and posterior to the subclavian artery in the lower neck. Needle injury can cause radiating arm pain and upper limb weakness. Phrenic injury would affect the diaphragm and often cause respiratory symptoms. Recurrent laryngeal or sympathetic injury would produce voice or Horner findings rather than the observed limb deficit.
  18. The correct answer is B (Impaired absorption at arachnoid granulations beside the superior sagittal sinus): Blood products in the subarachnoid space can impair cerebrospinal fluid absorption at arachnoid granulations. The fluid pathways remain open, so the ventricles and subarachnoid space communicate. Aqueduct or foramen of Monro obstruction would produce noncommunicating hydrocephalus. The fourth ventricle outlets and lateral ventricle foramina are not the usual cause of this posthemorrhagic pattern.
  19. The correct answer is D (Pericardial tamponade causing impaired ventricular filling after cardiac surgery): Falling drain output with rising venous pressure, muffled sounds, and hypotension suggests clot compressing the heart. One wrong option would usually cause unequal breath sounds and respiratory distress. Another wrong option does not explain the muffled sounds or narrowed pulse pressure. Several other wrong options do not fit the classic pressure and heart sound pattern.
  20. The correct answer is B (Dense connective tissue of the scalp holds divided vessels open after laceration): The second scalp layer contains dense connective tissue that tethers vessel walls and prevents them from retracting effectively. This anatomy explains why scalp wounds can bleed heavily and require firm pressure or vessel control. The loose areolar layer lies deeper and permits scalp movement rather than direct hemostasis. Aponeurotic fibers do not reliably compress torn vessels, making that explanation incomplete.
  21. The correct answer is E (Rhabdomyolysis with acute kidney injury and cardiac rhythm disturbance): High-voltage current can pass through deep muscle and release large amounts of myoglobin and potassium. Dark urine with an irregular pulse therefore raises concern for renal injury and dangerous cardiac effects. One wrong option underestimates the unseen deep injury, while another wrong option does not explain the electrical current pathway. Several other wrong options focus on surface tissue although electrical damage commonly extends far beyond visible burns.
  22. The correct answer is B (Splanchnic vasoconstriction that may impair mucosal barrier function): Sympathetic and hormonal responses redirect blood to the heart and brain, but the gut mucosa has high metabolic demand and becomes vulnerable. One wrong option reverses the usual compensatory constriction. Another wrong option does not describe a rapid shock response. Several other wrong options conflict with the sympathetic state and the known fall in splanchnic perfusion.
  23. The correct answer is A (Deep peroneal nerve beside the anterior tibial vessels): The deep peroneal nerve runs in the anterior leg compartment beside the anterior tibial vessels and supplies the dorsiflexors. Compartment pressure can impair its function before distal pulses disappear. Common peroneal nerve injury is tempting because it also causes foot drop, but the common nerve lies near the fibular neck before dividing. Complete anterior and lateral decompression helps protect the deep and superficial peroneal territories.
  24. The correct answer is A (Carbon monoxide poisoning after inhaling smoke and combustion gases): Closed-space smoke exposure with headache, nausea, and confusion is a classic presentation, and pulse oximetry can remain falsely reassuring. Carbon monoxide binds haemoglobin and reduces oxygen delivery despite a normal oxygen saturation reading. One wrong option is possible with synthetic material smoke but the exposure history does not make it the first consideration. Several other wrong options do not link the neurological symptoms to combustion gas exposure.
  25. The correct answer is D (The cribriform plate traversed by fine olfactory nerve filaments): The cribriform plate separates the anterior cranial fossa from the nasal cavity and contains small openings for olfactory fibers. Injury creates a direct path for cerebrospinal fluid to enter the nose. The optic canal and orbital roof lie farther lateral and do not form the nasal roof. The sella and foramen lacerum are deeper skull base structures and less compatible with this endoscopic leak.
  26. The correct answer is B (Low albumin and clotting factors with low platelets from splenic pooling): The liver makes albumin and most clotting factors, and portal hypertension enlarges the spleen so more platelets are pooled there. One wrong option attributes the findings to the kidney and marrow. Another wrong option reverses the low albumin and clotting abnormality. Several other wrong options do not explain the combined synthetic and portal findings.
  27. The correct answer is D (Broad exposure disrupts bowel flora and permits toxin-producing C difficile disease): Prolonged broad antibiotics alter normal bowel flora and create an environment where C difficile can multiply and release toxins. Infection control, hydration, stopping unnecessary antibiotics, and severity-based treatment are central. This illness can begin during or after the precipitating course and may spread between patients. Several other wrong options misunderstand the mechanism and risk profile of antibiotic-associated colitis.
  28. The correct answer is D (The superior sagittal sinus adjacent to the arachnoid granulations): Arachnoid granulations protrude into the superior sagittal sinus and transfer cerebrospinal fluid into venous blood. A parasagittal fracture can tear this sinus and cause serious hemorrhage. The sigmoid sinus lies at the skull base behind the mastoid. The cavernous and petrosal sinuses occupy other cranial base compartments.
  29. The correct answer is B (Cirrhosis causes coagulopathy, portal hypertension and impaired drug clearance): Fibrosis and regenerative nodules alter blood flow and liver function. Reduced clotting factor synthesis and low platelets increase bleeding risk, while portal hypertension can cause varices and ascites. Poor hepatic metabolism can prolong drug effects. Cirrhosis does not improve clotting, increase platelet production or remove systemic surgical risk.
  30. The correct answer is B (Superior gluteal nerve supplying gluteus medius and minimus): The superior gluteal nerve runs above piriformis and supplies gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. Injury weakens hip abduction and produces a contralateral pelvic dip during single-leg stance. Inferior gluteal nerve injury is tempting after posterior hip approaches, but it weakens gluteus maximus and causes difficulty rising or climbing. Protecting the superior gluteal branches during lateral exposure preserves gait stability.
  31. The correct answer is D (Brachial artery directly anterior to the distal humerus): The brachial artery lies anterior to the distal humerus near the cubital fossa and can be injured or compressed by a displaced supracondylar fracture. Ischaemia increases the risk of forearm compartment syndrome and long-term contracture. Radial artery injury is tempting because the pulse is weak, but the proximal brachial vessel is the primary fracture hazard. Urgent fracture reduction and repeated vascular assessment are required when perfusion is compromised.
  32. The correct answer is E (Remove the cast, keep the limb level, and arrange urgent fasciotomy): The cast adds external pressure to a compartment that already shows severe stretch pain and sensory change. Immediate release of constriction plus urgent surgical decompression addresses the cause before muscle and nerve death occur. One wrong option raises the limb, which can reduce arterial inflow, while another wrong option waits for a late sign. Several other wrong options delay pressure release without improving the threatened compartment.
  33. The correct answer is A (Stop transfusion, support circulation, and treat a suspected haemolytic reaction): Fever, loin pain, hypotension, and dark urine during transfusion suggest an acute haemolytic reaction. The transfusion should stop immediately while the line is kept patent with saline and urgent help and reporting are arranged. One wrong option permits more incompatible blood. Several other wrong options delay recognition and worsen exposure.
  34. The correct answer is D (Begin sepsis protocol, obtain cultures, and give prompt antimicrobial treatment): Sepsis treatment must start promptly, with cultures taken when they do not delay antibiotics and broad empirical therapy chosen under local policy. Oxygen, fluids, lactate measurement, senior review, and a search for source control proceed at the same time. Waiting for cultures delays treatment in a patient with organ dysfunction. A fluid bolus alone ignores antibiotics and inadequate perfusion.
  35. The correct answer is C (Obstructive shock from impaired ventricular filling and high filling pressures): Pericardial pressure restricts filling, so stroke volume and blood pressure fall while venous pressure remains high. One wrong option predicts warm vasodilated peripheries rather than elevated filling pressure. Another wrong option predicts collapsed neck veins. Several other wrong options do not explain the muffled heart sounds and pulsus paradoxus.
  36. The correct answer is B (Toe pressure measurement with detailed Doppler waveform assessment): Hardened ankle arteries can create a falsely high index, while toe pressure and waveform shape often reflect distal perfusion better. One wrong option repeats the same unreliable measurement. Another wrong option examines veins rather than the arterial supply. Several other wrong options cannot measure perfusion or detect the severity of calcified arterial disease.
  37. The correct answer is C (Obturator nerve along pelvic side wall beside the quadrilateral plate): The obturator nerve passes close to the quadrilateral plate and can be injured by pelvic fractures, retractors, or fixation. Injury weakens the adductors and reduces medial thigh sensation while hip flexion and knee extension remain largely intact. Femoral nerve injury is tempting after anterior pelvic surgery, but it weakens knee extension and hip flexion. Recognising the pattern guides rehabilitation of hip adduction and avoids confusion with a more proximal plexus injury.
  38. The correct answer is D (Ventilation perfusion mismatch from small airway closure and atelectasis): Pain, opioid effects, and supine posture reduce lung expansion and leave poorly ventilated units that remain perfused. One wrong option lacks evidence of vascular obstruction. Another wrong option implies a chronic interstitial process that would not appear this quickly. Several other wrong options ignore the imaging and the common postoperative mechanism.
  39. The correct answer is C (Local invasion, entry into vessels, survival in circulation and growth elsewhere): Metastasis begins with local invasion, which allows tumour cells to enter vessels or lymphatics. Cells must survive travel, arrest in a distant capillary bed, leave the vessel and grow in the new site. Reduced adhesion and enzyme activity support the early invasive step. The other sequences place distant growth before entry or movement through the circulation.
  40. The correct answer is D (Simple fluid with low T1 and high T2 signal): Simple fluid is usually dark on T1-weighted images and bright on T2-weighted images. A well-defined cystic structure without fever or restricted diffusion supports that interpretation. One wrong option would be low in signal on both sequences. Several other wrong options do not match the stated low T1 and high T2 combination.
  41. The correct answer is C (Femoral artery, femoral vein, and saphenous nerve within the adductor canal): The adductor canal contains the femoral artery, femoral vein, saphenous nerve, and nerve to vastus medialis as they pass through the medial thigh. The femoral artery continues toward the popliteal region after leaving the canal. The femoral nerve is tempting because its name appears in the same region, but it has already divided before this level and lies outside the canal. Recognising these contents helps protect the vein and saphenous nerve during medial exposure.
  42. The correct answer is D (Urgent specialist assessment for anticoagulation and possible junction surgery): Superficial thrombosis close to the saphenofemoral junction can propagate into deep veins and needs urgent vascular review. One wrong option ignores that extension risk. Another wrong option uses excessive treatment for a superficial clot without embolic shock. A further wrong option omits treatment planning and risk assessment, while yet another wrong option treats a thrombosed vein as an abscess.
  43. The correct answer is A (An expanding cervical haematoma causing progressive airway compromise): A rapidly enlarging neck after thyroid surgery can compress the airway, and oozing plus falling oxygen makes haematoma the leading emergency. The wound may need urgent opening while senior airway and surgical help are called. Anaphylaxis could cause swelling but usually appears around induction with other allergic features. Atelectasis, seroma, and simple vocal cord palsy do not explain the rapidly expanding neck and wound ooze.
  44. The correct answer is C (Collect multiple blood culture sets before antibiotics without delaying urgent treatment): Blood cultures should be obtained before antibiotics in a stable patient to identify the organism and guide treatment. One wrong option uses absolute wording and moves to surgery without evidence. Another wrong option lowers culture yield, while a further wrong option omits the key microbiological investigation. Yet another wrong option ignores persistent fever with a new murmur.
  45. The correct answer is B (The middle meningeal artery torn beneath the pterion): The pterion overlies the anterior branch of the middle meningeal artery and is vulnerable to lateral trauma. Arterial bleeding produces the classic biconvex extradural collection with a lucid interval. A torn bridging vein more often causes a crescentic subdural collection and does not match the temporal arterial anatomy. The superficial temporal artery supplies the scalp and lies outside the cranial cavity.
  46. The correct answer is A (Femoral nerve lateral to the femoral artery outside the sheath): The femoral sheath contains the femoral artery, femoral vein, and femoral canal lymphatics but does not enclose the femoral nerve. The nerve lies lateral to the sheath outside its compartments. The femoral branch of the genitofemoral nerve is tempting because it lies nearby, but the femoral nerve itself is the main structure excluded from the sheath. Remembering this anatomy helps prevent nerve injury during vascular exposure or groin dissection.
  47. The correct answer is A (Give measured glucose, recheck promptly, and find the precipitating cause): Sweating, tremor, confusion, and a low capillary glucose define hypoglycaemia and require immediate measured glucose replacement. Glucose should be rechecked, with later review of insulin, fasting, and other causes. One wrong option worsens low glucose. Several other wrong options delay correction or use the wrong treatment.
  48. The correct answer is E (Systemic thrombolysis because shock indicates massive pulmonary embolism): Postoperative collapse with right ventricular strain and hypoxaemia suggests a high risk clot causing obstructive shock. One wrong option is specialist treatment but not the immediate first line for most unstable cases. Another wrong option is too slow, and a further wrong option does not relieve an existing obstructing clot. Yet another wrong option may reduce preload when the failing right ventricle needs careful volume and perfusion support.
  49. The correct answer is C (Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone with water retention): After pituitary surgery, inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion can cause concentrated urine, water retention, and hyponatraemia without obvious dehydration. Careful sodium correction is needed. One wrong option predicts low circulating volume, while another wrong option predicts dilute urine and high sodium. Several other wrong options have different metabolic profiles.
  50. The correct answer is A (Distributive shock with low vascular resistance and warm peripheries): Sepsis lowers vascular tone and maldistributes flow, so pressure falls even when the heart is hyperdynamic. One wrong option is excluded by warm skin and good ventricular filling. Another wrong option lacks an obstructive finding on echocardiography. Several other wrong options conflict with rapid capillary refill and a hyperdynamic circulation.
  51. The correct answer is A (Oesophageal atresia with airway protection and specialist referral): Feeding difficulties, cyanosis, excess saliva, and a tube that stops above the stomach suggest an interrupted oesophagus, often with a fistula to the airway. Suction, nil by mouth, and urgent neonatal surgical care protect the airway. One wrong option has a different age and vomiting pattern. Several other wrong options do not explain the feeding tube position or first-feed choking.
  52. The correct answer is E (Chloride excess causing normal gap dilutional metabolic acidosis): Large saline loads provide more chloride than plasma and reduce the bicarbonate buffer without creating a high anion gap. One wrong option predicts low sodium, which is not present. Another wrong option describes alkalosis rather than acidosis. Several other wrong options are excluded by normal lactate, normal glucose, and the clinical context.
  53. The correct answer is C (Acute right ventricular infarction producing impaired filling and reduced cardiac output): Inferior infarction can injure the right ventricle, which then fails to deliver blood through the pulmonary circulation. Raised jugular venous pressure with clear lungs and echocardiographic right ventricular dilation fits this mechanism. Tamponade after rupture would usually show a pericardial effusion and diastolic pressure equalisation. Pulmonary embolism can strain the right ventricle, but the inferior infarct pattern makes infarction the better explanation.
  54. The correct answer is A (Plan compression and endovenous or surgical treatment guided by venous mapping): Symptomatic superficial reflux with patent deep veins is treated by compression and targeted closure or surgery based on mapping. One wrong option treats a venous problem as arterial occlusion. Another wrong option has no evidence of infection. A further wrong option opposes appropriate compression, while yet another wrong option incorrectly assumes that deep thrombosis caused the mapped superficial reflux.
  55. The correct answer is C (Anastomotic leak with intra-abdominal contamination and generalised peritonitis): Fever, tachycardia, peritonitis, rising markers, and fluid near the anastomosis after bowel surgery strongly suggest a leak. This is an urgent source-control problem rather than a condition treated by changing antibiotics alone. Several other wrong options do not explain generalised peritonitis or the deep collection. Several other wrong options are less consistent with the focal anastomotic findings.
  56. The correct answer is E (Diabetic ketoacidosis triggered by surgery and acute illness): High glucose, acidosis, strong ketones, dehydration, vomiting, and deep breathing fit diabetic ketoacidosis. Surgery and acute illness are common triggers, and treatment requires a protocol led approach with fluids, insulin, potassium monitoring, and cause review. One wrong option usually has less ketosis. Several other wrong options conflict with the glucose and ketone findings.
  57. The correct answer is A (Call vascular surgery immediately and transfer for urgent aneurysm repair): Collapse, back pain, hypotension, and a tender pulsatile mass strongly suggest a ruptured aneurysm requiring urgent haemorrhage control. One wrong option allows imaging to delay definitive specialist care in an unstable patient. Another wrong option is far too slow for active bleeding. Several other wrong options delay the operative priority without providing definitive treatment.
  58. The correct answer is E (Debride again and use delayed primary closure after several days): The wound still contains contamination and nonviable tissue, so further debridement is needed before closure can be considered. Delayed primary closure allows serial review and closure once infection is controlled. One wrong option and another wrong option seal material that has not been cleared, while a further wrong option hides contamination beneath a drain. Yet another wrong option leaves no plan for closure after the wound has been prepared.
  59. The correct answer is D (Hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis from sustained upper gastric hydrogen and chloride losses): Vomiting removes hydrogen and chloride, while compensatory hypoventilation raises carbon dioxide. One wrong option does not fit the high bicarbonate or the low chloride. Another wrong option is excluded by the alkaline pH and raised carbon dioxide. Several other wrong options produce acidosis rather than the alkalosis demonstrated in the sample.
  60. The correct answer is E (It treats many anaerobes but may interact with warfarin and requires review): Metronidazole is useful for many anaerobic infections and can potentiate warfarin, so anticoagulation monitoring and drug review matter. Its role should match the source and confirmed sensitivities. Several other wrong options overstate its spectrum or preventive duration. Another wrong option confuses antimicrobial action with surgical source control.
  61. The correct answer is E (Sympathetic blockade causing vasodilation and reduced venous return): A spinal block removes sympathetic tone, lowering systemic resistance and venous return, which can also provoke bradycardia. One wrong option predicts increased return rather than the observed hypotension. Another wrong option overstates direct myocardial depression from a typical spinal dose. Several other wrong options conflict with the controlled field, warm skin, bradycardia, and absent volume loss.
  62. The correct answer is B (Thyroid storm triggered by surgery in uncontrolled thyrotoxicosis): Fever, marked tachycardia, agitation, vomiting, and altered consciousness after surgery in a thyrotoxic patient suggest thyroid storm. Treatment should occur alongside evaluation for infection and other triggers. One wrong option describes low thyroid activity. Several other wrong options do not align as closely with the thyrotoxic history.
  63. The correct answer is B (Pheochromocytoma causing episodic catecholamine release during tumour handling): Catecholamine surges produce severe hypertension, tachycardia, sweating and pallor, and tumour manipulation is a classic trigger. The earlier episodic palpitations fit this diagnosis, while the fall after handling can follow reduced catecholamine release or treatment. Thyroid storm usually causes fever and sustained hypermetabolic features, and carcinoid more often causes flushing and hypotension. Adrenal crisis produces hypotension, and aldosterone excess causes sustained hypertension with hypokalaemia rather than paroxysmal attacks.
  64. The correct answer is D (Autonomic dysreflexia from uninhibited sympathetic activity below the lesion): A noxious stimulus below a cord lesion above T6 can trigger a sympathetic surge with dangerous hypertension. Descending inhibitory control is lost, while baroreceptor reflexes can cause bradycardia and flushing above the lesion. Removing the bladder stimulus is the immediate priority. Simple anxiety or vasovagal syncope does not fit the severe pressure rise and segmental sweating.
  65. The correct answer is C (Proceed promptly with intubation before swelling obstructs the airway): Enclosed-space fire exposure plus soot, hoarseness, drooling, and stridor indicate progressive upper airway injury. Early intubation secures the airway while anatomy remains manageable and before oedema makes laryngoscopy difficult. One wrong option delays definitive control because oxygen does not reverse swelling, while another wrong option waits for a test despite an obvious clinical emergency. Several other wrong options are premature because nebulised adrenaline is not a substitute for airway control and a surgical airway is reserved for failed access.
  66. The correct answer is B (Use enteral feeding while treating nausea and encouraging oral intake): The gut is the preferred route when safe because it supports the bowel lining and lowers infection risk. Enteral support can bridge reduced oral intake while nausea is treated. One wrong option uses intravenous nutrition before the gut has failed. Several other wrong options either starve the patient or provide inadequate nutrition.
  67. The correct answer is B (Hold nephrotoxic and volume reducing medicines while reassessing perfusion): The combination of low blood pressure, possible dehydration, and these medicines can reduce kidney blood flow and worsen acute kidney injury. Holding the harmful drugs while assessing volume and perfusion allows treatment of the cause. Continuing nonsteroidal drugs or increasing diuretic treatment adds further kidney stress. Blind potassium replacement can be unsafe when kidney function and blood levels are changing.
  68. The correct answer is E (Postoperative sepsis with a possible infected intra-abdominal collection): Confusion, hypotension, tachypnoea, oliguria, guarding, and raised lactate indicate sepsis with organ dysfunction and a likely abdominal source. These findings are more severe than a benign inflammatory response or superficial colonisation. Pulmonary embolism can cause shock but does not explain the focal abdominal guarding. An uncomplicated urinary infection would not normally produce generalised peritonitis.
  69. The correct answer is D (Potassium release from injured cells with reduced renal excretion): Muscle crush injury releases intracellular potassium, and hypoperfusion limits its removal by the kidney. One wrong option describes treatment mechanisms that lower plasma potassium rather than its cause here. Another wrong option predicts a fall in potassium instead of the measured rise. Several other wrong options move the potassium in the opposite direction from the clinical evidence.
  70. The correct answer is B (Metastatic adenocarcinoma involving the pleura from the breast primary): Cohesive glandular cells invading tissue and epithelial marker positivity favour metastatic carcinoma. Breast cancer is a common source of malignant pleural effusion and can produce blood-stained fluid. Reactive mesothelial cells do not invade as glandular structures, and mesothelioma usually has mesothelial rather than glandular marker features. Tuberculosis and empyema produce inflammation or pus rather than invasive epithelial glands.
  71. The correct answer is A (Use the susceptible narrow agent when the site and clinical course support it): A confirmed susceptible organism allows targeted treatment, provided the drug reaches the infected site and the patient is improving after source control. Narrowing limits toxicity, resistance pressure, and unnecessary broad cover. One wrong option misses the benefit of sensitivity data. Several other wrong options either widen care or stop prematurely.
  72. The correct answer is E (Provide supportive care and arrange urgent surgical assessment): A tense irreducible groin hernia with vomiting, tachycardia, and skin change may be incarcerated and progressing toward strangulation.   73. The correct answer is C (Use the lowest-risk agent and follow current renal and local guidance): Gadolinium safety depends on kidney function, the specific agent, the indication, and current policy. A specialist plan can use the lowest-risk option when the result may change urgent care. Several other wrong options overstate safety or general unsuitability.   74. The correct answer is E (Alpha one adrenergic receptors on vascular smooth muscle): Alpha one receptors are the main postjunctional adrenergic receptors that constrict many peripheral blood vessels. Their activation increases peripheral resistance. Beta one receptors mainly increase heart rate and contractility, while beta two receptors promote smooth muscle relaxation in several tissues. Muscarinic and nicotinic receptors belong to cholinergic pathways rather than this norepinephrine response.
  75. The correct answer is C (Popliteal artery tethered near the posterior knee capsule): The popliteal artery is deep in the popliteal fossa and lies close to the posterior capsule, making it vulnerable to traction or laceration during knee dislocation. An absent posterior tibial pulse with a pale foot suggests impaired distal flow. Anterior tibial artery injury is tempting because it supplies the leg, but it branches below the popliteal artery and is less likely to explain the full clinical picture. Immediate vascular assessment and reduction are essential because prolonged ischaemia threatens limb viability.
  76. The correct answer is B (Flattened T waves with visible U waves and increased ectopic activity): Hypokalaemia delays ventricular repolarisation and commonly produces T flattening, U waves, and ectopic beats. One wrong option describes hyperkalaemia, which tends to produce peaked T waves and QRS widening. Another wrong option describes injury currents rather than the usual potassium disturbance. Several other wrong options describe pre-excitation or ischaemic repolarisation patterns that do not match potassium 2.6 mmol/L.
  77. The correct answer is E (Giant cell arteritis involving a medium-sized cranial artery): The age, cranial symptoms and biopsy findings are typical of giant cell arteritis. Granulomas near a fragmented elastic lamina are characteristic, and prompt steroid treatment reduces visual loss risk. Takayasu arteritis also causes giant cell arteritis but usually affects younger patients and the aortic arch. Polyarteritis, microscopic polyangiitis and thromboangiitis have different vessel sizes and clinical patterns.
  78. The correct answer is E (Deep vein thrombosis with possible pulmonary embolism causing symptoms): Calf swelling and tenderness after knee replacement raise concern for deep vein thrombosis, while new breathlessness raises concern for pulmonary embolism. Urgent assessment and treatment are needed without massaging the leg. Several other wrong options understate venous thrombosis risk. Several other wrong options do not explain the new respiratory symptom.
  79. The correct answer is E (Hypovolaemia causing prerenal kidney injury and reduced urine flow): Prolonged vomiting has caused volume depletion, and dry membranes with a postural pulse rise point to reduced circulating volume. The low urine output is therefore likely prerenal rather than a primary kidney or drainage problem. Urinary obstruction usually causes a painful distended bladder, while sepsis would show inflammatory or haemodynamic features. Recent stable contrast exposure does not explain the clear fluid deficit.
  80. The correct answer is B (Hyaline arteriolosclerosis causing uniform thickening of small vessel walls): Chronic hypertension and ageing produce a glassy proteinaceous deposit in arteriole walls that narrows the lumen. The absence of inflammation and fibrinoid injury supports this chronic change. Intimal fibroplasia affects larger arteries, while fibrinoid necrosis and arteritis are active destructive lesions. Medial calcification can stiffen arteries but does not explain uniform arteriolar hyaline thickening.
  81. The correct answer is C (The vestibulocochlear nerve within the internal acoustic meatus): The internal acoustic meatus transmits the facial and vestibulocochlear nerves. The hypoglossal nerve exits through its own canal lower in the skull base.
  82. The correct answer is A (Theatre washout, joint assessment, antibiotics, and formal surgical closure): Leakage of air and blood shows that the wound communicates with the knee joint.   84. The correct answer is B (A carotid body paraganglioma at the carotid bifurcation): A carotid body tumor characteristically splays the internal and external carotid arteries.   85. The correct answer is E (Hounsfield units with water assigned a value near zero): Computed tomography density is expressed in Hounsfield units, with water as the reference. Air and fat are lower, while bone and metal are much higher. One wrong option describes a signal quality measure, not tissue density. Several other wrong options belong to other imaging methods or measurement questions and do not define computed tomography brightness.

  87.   88. The correct answer is D (Small cell carcinoma with neuroendocrine differentiation and early nodal spread): Small cell carcinoma commonly presents as a central mass with bulky nodes and early metastasis. It may secrete antidiuretic hormone or related peptides and cause hyponatraemia. Adenocarcinoma is more often peripheral, while squamous carcinoma can be central but is less associated with this paraneoplastic syndrome. Large cell carcinoma and typical carcinoid tumours do not fit the aggressive nodal pattern as well.
  89. The correct answer is C (Stop further opioid, call for help, and support breathing): Sedation and slow shallow breathing after opioids indicate respiratory depression and require immediate airway and breathing support. The opioid supply should stop while help and monitoring are organised. More opioid would deepen the problem, and deep breathing instructions are not enough for a patient who is difficult to rouse. A sedative can worsen respiratory depression and delay essential treatment.
  90. The correct answer is C (The pathologist may be unable to identify and assess the important margin): Margins are assessed by linking the tumour's position to the anatomical edges of the specimen. Sutures, ink and a diagram preserve that orientation during transfer. Without these clues, the pathologist may not know which edge is closest to the tumour or which surface is critical. Fixation does not make a tumour benign, and loss of orientation does not automatically alter nodal status or grade.
  91. The correct answer is A (Right lateral medullary infarction from posterior inferior cerebellar artery occlusion): The lateral medulla carries vestibular, bulbar, trigeminal, spinothalamic, and sympathetic pathways. Middle cerebral and anterior cerebral lesions mainly produce cortical hemisphere deficits. A lateral medullary syndrome does not fit the option describing diffuse bilateral occipital injury.
  92. The correct answer is A (Biofilm and dead tissue protect bacteria from reliable antibiotic penetration): Biofilm is an adherent microbial layer that prevents reliable drug access, and necrotic tissue further shields organisms. Debridement and source control are essential alongside appropriate antimicrobials. One wrong option assumes universal resistance without evidence.   96. The correct answer is C (Alpha blockade first followed by beta blockade only if needed): A pheochromocytoma releases catecholamines that cause severe hypertension and dangerous operative pressure swings. Preoperative alpha blockade is established first to control vasoconstriction and allow volume expansion. Beta blockade is added later for persistent tachycardia because giving it first can leave unopposed alpha mediated vasoconstriction. Diuretics may worsen volume depletion, and proceeding without blockade makes surgery extremely hazardous.
  97. The correct answer is D (The third pharyngeal pouch, which forms the thymus and inferior parathyroids): The third pouch gives rise to inferior parathyroid tissue and the thymus. Their shared descent explains why the inferior parathyroid glands may be found lower than expected. The fourth pouch forms the superior parathyroid glands.   98. The correct answer is B (Clear cell renal cell carcinoma associated with VHL pathway loss): Golden yellow tissue reflects lipid-rich clear cells, and the delicate vascular network is characteristic.   101.   102.   107.   108.   122.   123.   125. The correct answer is D (Adrenal insufficiency causing poor vascular tone and metabolic disturbance): Long term steroid use can suppress the adrenal response to surgical stress, producing hypotension, hypoglycaemia and hyperkalaemia. Hydrocortisone and fluid treatment are required when the diagnosis is suspected in this setting.   126. The correct answer is E (Stage recording the anatomical extent of nodes and distant metastases): Stage describes the anatomical spread of cancer and strongly predicts outcome. Nodal and distant disease identifies a tumour that has already passed local boundaries. Grade describes differentiation and growth potential, while histological type identifies the tissue lineage. These are useful, but they do not capture the extent of spread that separates these two patients.

  129.   131.   135. The correct answer is C (Carcinoma in situ with dysplasia confined by the basement membrane): Severe dysplasia that occupies the epithelium but does not cross the basement membrane is carcinoma in situ. It has malignant cellular features but no invasive capacity because the boundary remains intact. Invasive carcinoma requires cells to enter underlying tissue. Metaplasia and hyperplasia alter mature tissue without this degree of disordered cellular growth.
  136.   137.   138. The correct answer is E (Vasoactive tumour products reaching circulation through liver metastases): Carcinoid syndrome occurs when vasoactive substances from a neuroendocrine tumour reach the systemic circulation in sufficient amounts. Liver metastases bypass the usual hepatic clearance of these substances and allow flushing, diarrhoea and right sided cardiac valve damage. Insulin and calcitonin cause different clinical syndromes, and liver tumour bulk alone does not explain the biochemical and cardiac findings.

  147. The correct answer is A (Medial circumflex femoral artery through retinacular branches near the femoral neck): Most adult blood supply to the femoral head comes from retinacular branches of the medial circumflex femoral artery. A displaced intracapsular fracture can disrupt these vessels and lead to avascular necrosis. This anatomy explains why timely fixation or arthroplasty decisions depend on fracture type and patient factors.
  148. The correct answer is E (Increased cortisol and catecholamines causing insulin resistance and glucose release): Surgery activates stress hormones that promote glucose production and reduce peripheral insulin sensitivity, so glucose rises even without diabetes. Lower stress hormones would improve sensitivity rather than raise glucose, and increased insulin uptake would lower it. Aldosterone regulates sodium and potassium rather than causing glucose absorption from urine.

Copious irrigation then dilutes residual alkali and limits ongoing injury.   152. The correct answer is C (Flexor carpi ulnaris in the medial forearm near the ulnar artery): Flexor carpi ulnaris is supplied by the ulnar nerve and flexes and adducts the wrist. The medial half of flexor digitorum profundus also receives ulnar innervation, while most other forearm flexors are median supplied. The alternatives “Application of a cast with the elbow fully extended” and “Delayed transfer after oral analgesia and repeated pulse checks” do not correct the displaced fracture and may worsen vascular compromise.
  221. The correct answer is E (Urgent reduction and splinting with repeated neurovascular assessment): Pale tented skin signals severe pressure from the displaced fracture and a high risk of skin breakdown and neurovascular injury. Prompt reduction restores alignment and reduces pressure before the skin fails. The alternative “Wait for definitive surgery because the skin has not broken” waits for skin to break, while the alternative “Apply a compression bandage and elevate the limb overnight before fracture clinic review” uses a bandage that does not correct displacement. The alternatives “Obtain magnetic resonance imaging before manipulating the ankle” and “Discharge with a walking boot and fracture clinic appointment” delay urgent realignment and place the limb at avoidable risk.
  222. The correct answer is D (Increased physiological dead space from ventilated but poorly perfused alveoli): Obstruction removes perfusion from ventilated lung units, so more of each breath fails to exchange carbon dioxide. The alternative “Reduced physiological dead space from excluded nonperfused alveoli” reverses the expected change in physiological dead space. The alternative “Pure hypoventilation causing low alveolar oxygen and low carbon dioxide” predicts carbon dioxide retention from shallow breathing rather than embolism. The alternatives “Diffusion impairment alone while alveolar ventilation remains fully preserved” and “Complete shunt through collapsed alveoli without pulmonary vascular obstruction” describe diffusion or shunt physiology without the embolic vascular defect.
  223. The correct answer is D (Amyloid deposition between myocardial fibres causing restrictive cardiomyopathy): Amyloid protein infiltrates the myocardium, stiffens the ventricle and produces heart failure with preserved filling. Deposition can thicken the wall while low voltage reflects the electrical effect of the abnormal protein. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy usually has high voltages and asymmetric septal hypertrophy. Dilated cardiomyopathy, iron overload and ischaemic fibrosis do not fit the plasma cell disorder with thickened granular myocardium.
  224. The correct answer is B (A duty arises at presentation, so assess, resuscitate, and escalate the emergency without delay): A clinician owes a duty of care once a patient is accepted into the emergency care pathway, including assessment and urgent escalation. Delay while waiting for a bed can cause avoidable harm when the patient may have a ruptured aneurysm. The alternatives “No duty exists until an inpatient bed has been formally allocated to the patient”, “Only the referring doctor holds a duty because the patient has not yet been admitted”, “The patient must arrange private review before the surgical team has any professional duty”, and “A duty begins only after written consent for assessment has been signed by the patient” invent requirements that are not needed to create a clinical duty. The response should focus on resuscitation and rapid senior, anaesthetic, imaging, and surgical assessment.
  225. The correct answer is B (Urgent escharotomy to release pressure from the constricting burn): A circumferential full thickness burn forms a rigid band as the limb swells, which can impair arterial inflow. The fading pulse and worsening pain mark a limb-threatening pressure effect that needs release before further deterioration. The alternative “Elevate the arm above shoulder level and repeat pulse checks hourly” does not cut the constricting tissue, and the alternative “Apply a compression bandage and delay specialist review overnight” worsens pressure while delaying care. The alternatives “Arrange routine physiotherapy because the arm movement remains normal” and “Give stronger analgesia and wait for the radial pulse to disappear” treat symptoms without relieving the mechanical cause of reduced perfusion.
  226. The correct answer is B (The greater petrosal nerve carrying fibers toward the pterygopalatine ganglion): Parasympathetic fibers from the facial nerve travel through the greater petrosal nerve to the pterygopalatine ganglion. Postganglionic fibers then reach the lacrimal gland and stimulate tear production. The chorda tympani carries taste and salivary fibers, while the stapedius branch modulates sound transmission. The marginal and temporal branches are motor branches to facial muscles.
  227. The correct answer is D (Seven litres per minute when heart rate is multiplied by stroke volume): Multiplying 100 beats per minute by 70 millilitres per beat gives 7000 millilitres, or seven litres per minute. The alternative “Seven litres per minute when stroke volume is divided by heart rate” uses division instead of the multiplication in the cardiac output equation. The alternative “Three and a half litres per minute because filling pressure is omitted” halves the result without a physiologic basis. The alternatives “Fourteen litres per minute when heart rate is multiplied twice” and “Seven hundred millilitres per minute after an incorrect unit conversion” use incorrect arithmetic or unit conversion.
  228. The correct answer is B (Pancoast tumour invading the brachial plexus and sympathetic chain): An apical lung mass can invade lower brachial plexus fibers and the sympathetic pathway, causing arm weakness and a drooping eyelid. The alternative “Superior vena cava obstruction causing facial swelling and dilated chest veins” primarily causes facial and arm swelling. The alternative “Pneumothorax causing pleuritic shoulder pain and reduced breath sounds” does not explain nerve or eyelid findings. The alternatives “Rotator cuff injury causing shoulder pain with preserved pupil and eyelid function” and “Myasthenia gravis causing fatigable weakness without a lung mass” are unrelated to the apical mass and do not fit the combined deficits.
  229. The correct answer is C (Cholesterol embolisation from an eroded atherosclerotic plaque after catheter manipulation): Catheter manipulation can dislodge cholesterol crystals from a diseased artery. Small vessels become blocked, causing blue toes, livedo and organ injury such as renal impairment. Retinal cholesterol spots support the diagnosis. Fat embolism follows bone trauma, air embolism causes a different acute event, and large clot emboli usually obstruct a major vessel with pulse loss.
  230. The correct answer is E (Pulmonary infarction caused by obstruction of a distal pulmonary artery): Immobility after pelvic surgery predisposes to venous thromboembolism, and a distal embolus can cause a wedge-shaped infarct. Pleuritic pain and haemoptysis occur when the infarct reaches the pleura. Pneumonia and aspiration produce airspace disease but do not explain the dilated right ventricle. Fat embolism follows bone trauma, and simple pleuritis does not produce the vascular opacity.
  231. The correct answer is E (Seek senior and legal advice, then disclose the minimum necessary only if a lawful justification exists): Confidentiality can be outweighed when there is a serious risk to another person, but disclosure requires a lawful basis, senior advice, and the minimum information needed. The surgeon should also offer support and address the patient's health needs without acting on assumption alone. The alternative “Tell the partner immediately because a surgeon must disclose every serious infection to relatives” treats disclosure as automatic, while the alternative “Inform the entire clinical team and local services without seeking consent or assessing necessity” overrides privacy without assessing necessity. The alternatives “Record nothing because confidentiality prevents any discussion or internal advice about the risk” and “Disclose to the partner's employer because workplace contact may create some possible community risk” either omit a serious risk or reveal information to someone with no clear need.
  232. The correct answer is A (Adenoma carcinoma sequence with APC loss followed by additional mutations): Loss of APC disturbs Wnt signalling and initiates adenoma growth, after which KRAS and p53 changes promote progression. The adjacent adenomatous areas support a stepwise precursor pathway. Microsatellite unstable tumours commonly begin with mismatch repair loss, and BRAF is more typical of the serrated route. Hamartomatous syndromes and cancers without a precursor follow different sequences.
  233. The correct answer is E (Tibial nerve just beneath the popliteal fascia near the roof): The tibial nerve is the most superficial major neurovascular structure in the popliteal fossa and lies near the roof of the space. An expanding haematoma can compress it before deeper structures are affected. Common peroneal nerve injury is tempting after knee trauma, but it lies along the lateral margin near the fibular head. Repeated tibial nerve and compartment examinations help detect compression or ischaemia before permanent deficits develop.
  234. The correct answer is A (Ionised hypocalcaemia caused by transient parathyroid gland dysfunction): Parathyroid manipulation or removal after thyroid surgery lowers calcium and increases neuromuscular excitability. The alternative “Hyperkalaemia caused by surgical tissue damage and metabolic acidosis” does not fit the tingling, spasm, or QT prolongation. The alternative “Hypomagnesaemia caused by chronic preoperative nutritional deficiency and starvation” may coexist but does not explain this classic postoperative timing as directly. The alternatives “Respiratory alkalosis caused by anxiety and rapid hyperventilation” and “Hypernatraemia caused by excessive postoperative administration of isotonic saline” do not account for the combination of perioral tingling, carpopedal spasm, and long QT.
  235. The correct answer is E (Septic cavernous sinus thrombosis after spreading facial infection): Facial infection can spread through valveless venous channels into the cavernous sinus. Thrombosis compresses the oculomotor, trochlear, abducens, and ophthalmic trigeminal nerves, producing ophthalmoplegia and corneal sensory loss. Preseptal cellulitis does not usually cause multiple cranial nerve palsies or proptosis. A posterior communicating aneurysm can affect the oculomotor nerve but does not explain the broader septic picture.
  236. The correct answer is E (Hirschsprung enterocolitis requiring urgent resuscitation and specialist care): Delayed meconium, abdominal distension, poor feeding, fever, foul stool, and tenderness suggest complicated Hirschsprung disease. Enterocolitis is life threatening and needs urgent fluids, antibiotics, decompression, and specialist input under local policy. The alternative “Simple constipation requiring no investigation because meconium is passed” ignores a sick neonate, and the alternative “Pyloric stenosis causing non-bilious vomiting and metabolic alkalosis” lacks the typical vomiting history. The alternatives “Testicular torsion causing abdominal signs through referred visceral pain and vomiting” and “Viral gastroenteritis with reassurance and routine outpatient review” do not fit the bowel presentation and systemic illness.
  237. The correct answer is C (Optimise circulation, use low-dose contrast, and review nephrotoxins): The possible benefit of urgent diagnosis can outweigh kidney risk, but avoidable factors should be addressed first. Circulation, contrast volume, and interacting medicines are practical targets. The alternatives “Withhold contrast and transfer him without further assessment” and “Give the largest contrast dose to finish the scan quickly” either omit a useful diagnosis or increase exposure without a safety plan. The alternatives “Ignore kidney function because urgent imaging poses only minor risk” and “Delay the scan until all current medicines have been stopped for several days” understate kidney risk or postpone useful urgent imaging.
  238. The correct answer is E (Fat saponification binding calcium in areas of peripancreatic necrosis): Pancreatic lipases release fatty acids from peripancreatic fat, and these acids bind calcium to form insoluble soaps. This consumes biologically available calcium and can produce true hypocalcaemia. Vitamin D deficiency is too slow to explain this acute change, while kidney injury alone is incomplete. Low albumin changes total calcium, but extensive fat necrosis can lower ionised calcium directly.
  239. The correct answer is E (Sciatic nerve behind the acetabulum and posterior column): The sciatic nerve passes behind the acetabulum and posterior column, where it can be injured by a posterior hip dislocation or retraction. Posterior displacement is suggested by internal rotation and shortening of the leg. Femoral nerve injury is tempting after hip trauma, but it lies anteriorly and causes weak knee extension rather than this distribution of posterior limb symptoms. Urgent reduction and careful nerve documentation are essential when sciatic weakness or numbness is present.
  240. The correct answer is A (Haemorrhage into a pituitary macroadenoma causing mass effect): Sudden bleeding into a pituitary tumour causes pituitary apoplexy, with headache, visual compression and cranial nerve palsy. Expanding sellar contents can also reduce hormone secretion. A berry aneurysm causes subarachnoid blood rather than blood confined to a pre-existing sellar mass.   241. The correct answer is E (Retention of free water diluting extracellular sodium concentration): Metabolised 5% dextrose behaves like free water, so antidiuretic hormone can retain the excess water and lower sodium concentration. The alternative “Reduced total body sodium causing water shift from cells into plasma” reverses the usual finding in this dilutional problem because total body sodium is not the main deficit. The alternative “Excess aldosterone causing sodium retention with free water diuresis” describes aldosterone excess, which causes sodium retention rather than dilutional hyponatraemia. The alternatives “Acute hyperglycaemia pulling water out of cells into plasma” and “Increased plasma oncotic pressure retaining water inside vessels” describe osmotic forces moving water in the opposite direction from the observed low sodium state.
  242. The correct answer is A (Suction the airway, position the patient laterally, and call for help): The airway must be cleared and protected immediately after aspiration to improve oxygen and prevent further contamination. Lateral positioning helps drainage while suction and senior support are arranged. Waiting for imaging delays the basic airway intervention that matters first. Fluids, antiemetics alone, flat positioning, or pharyngeal anaesthesia can worsen obstruction and aspiration.
  243. The correct answer is C (A deep intra-abdominal collection requiring image-guided drainage and antibiotics): Fever, focal tenderness, rising inflammatory markers, and a collection beside the anastomosis identify a deep fluid collection. Source control through drainage is central because antibiotics penetrate collections poorly. A superficial wound infection would not explain the deep imaging finding.   244. The correct answer is C (Use guideline based medicine and consider extended prophylaxis after discharge): Major cancer surgery can leave a high thrombosis risk after discharge, and local guidance should determine whether prophylaxis continues and for how long. Bleeding risk and kidney function still need review. The alternative “Stop all prophylaxis as soon as the patient first walks” ends protection too early, while the alternatives “Use compression stockings alone despite a strong clotting risk” and “Give aspirin only because cancer surgery causes venous stasis” are inadequate for high risk.   245. The correct answer is E (Microvascular ischemic oculomotor nerve palsy with pupil sparing): Diabetes and hypertension can impair the interior of the oculomotor nerve while sparing superficially placed pupillary fibers.   246. The correct answer is B (Common peroneal nerve near the fibular neck at the lateral knee): The common peroneal nerve winds around the fibular neck and can be stretched by fracture, cast pressure, or knee injury. Injury weakens ankle dorsiflexion and foot eversion while causing dorsal foot numbness. Tibial nerve injury is tempting because both nerves cross the knee area, but it weakens plantar flexion and causes plantar sensory loss.   247. The correct answer is A (Median nerve within the carpal tunnel beneath transverse carpal ligament): A volar wrist laceration can divide the median nerve as it passes through the carpal tunnel and supplies thenar muscles and lateral palmar digits. Loss of thumb opposition with this sensory pattern strongly localises the injury to the median nerve.   248. The correct answer is C (Bacterial infection of ascitic fluid without a surgical source): A high ascitic neutrophil count in a febrile patient with cirrhosis supports spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, often without a perforated organ. The alternatives “Perforated viscus causing free gas and polymicrobial peritonitis” and “Nephrotic syndrome causing low albumin ascites without cells” are excluded by absent free gas or the pattern of the fluid.
  249. The alternative “Superficial wound infection arising from contamination during the original injury” lacks a wound, while the alternative “Deep vein thrombosis because the calf is swollen and painful” does not account for the tense limb. The alternatives “Fat embolism syndrome causing the immediate calf swelling” and “Simple haematoma without any pressure or neurovascular concern” do not fit the immediate mechanical pressure from this fracture.
  250. The correct answer is C (High gap acidosis with an anion gap of 24 mmol/L): The gap is 138 minus the sum of chloride and bicarbonate, which gives 24 mmol/L, and the lactate explains the added unmeasured acid. The alternative “Normal gap metabolic acidosis because chloride is within reference” ignores the calculated gap and the elevated lactate. The alternative “High gap acidosis with an anion gap of 14 mmol/L” uses an incorrect arithmetic result for this sample. The alternatives “Normal gap acidosis caused only by respiratory carbon dioxide retention” and “Mixed alkalosis with compensation from reduced carbon dioxide” do not match either the low bicarbonate or the raised anion gap.
  251. The correct answer is B (Acute pulmonary embolism requiring urgent assessment and anticoagulant treatment): Sudden pleuritic pain, breathlessness, tachycardia, low oxygen, and calf swelling after major lower limb surgery raise strong concern for pulmonary embolism. Urgent assessment and treatment are required while the diagnosis is confirmed. Clear lungs without sputum make pneumonia less likely, and atelectasis does not fit the sudden pleuritic syndrome.   252. The correct answer is E (Refer for urgent tissue diagnosis and specialist staging assessment): A new spiculated nodule that has doubled in volume raises high concern for lung cancer despite its small size.   253. The correct answer is C (Usual interstitial pneumonia with patchy fibrosis and fibroblastic foci): The chronic course, basal crackles, honeycomb change and subpleural distribution strongly suggest usual interstitial pneumonia. Histology shows temporally patchy fibrosis with active fibroblastic foci and microscopic honeycombing. Organising pneumonia forms fibroblastic plugs inside airspaces, while acute diffuse alveolar damage has hyaline membranes. Alveolar proteinosis fills spaces with material, and sarcoidosis produces granulomas with a different distribution.
  254. The correct answer is C (Temporary sequestration of extracellular fluid after major operation): Inflammation and capillary leak can move extracellular fluid into tissues and reduce the effective circulating volume despite normal measured filling pressure.   255.   256.   257.
