  281. The correct answer is C (Prostaglandin loss reduces afferent dilation while blocked angiotensin leaves efferent tone low): Dehydration makes both protective mechanisms important, and the two drugs impair them at the same time. The alternative “The nonsteroidal drug dilates afferent vessels while the enzyme inhibitor preserves efferent tone” reverses the actions of the medicines. The alternative “Both drugs increase prostaglandin synthesis and improve renal blood flow” claims a beneficial increase in prostaglandins that does not occur. The alternatives “Increased antidiuretic hormone causes selective renal potassium wasting” and “The drug interaction causes immediate glomerular membrane rupture” describe unrelated or implausible mechanisms.
  282. The correct answer is A (Bicarbonate rich small bowel loss causing hypokalaemic metabolic acidosis): Proximal intestinal drainage removes bicarbonate and potassium, producing acidosis and low potassium. The alternative “Chloride rich gastric loss causing hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis” describes gastric rather than small bowel fluid. The alternative “Pure water loss producing hypernatraemic hyperosmolar dehydration without major solute loss” predicts sodium concentration to rise without the measured bicarbonate and potassium deficits. The alternatives “Aldosterone excess causing sodium retention and potassium excretion” and “Predominant colonic water loss causing hyperchloraemic metabolic alkalosis” do not fit the high volume small bowel fistula.
  283. The correct answer is C (Prostatic adenocarcinoma with loss of basal cells and nuclear atypia): Peripheral location, crowded small acini, prominent nucleoli and absent basal cells are key features of invasive prostatic adenocarcinoma. These findings explain the raised prostate specific antigen and suspicious imaging. Benign hyperplasia preserves basal cells, and prostatitis causes inflammation rather than a neoplastic acinar pattern. High-grade prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia retains ducts and a basal layer, while granulomatous prostatitis is inflammatory.
  284. The correct answer is B (Warm the patient and replace clotting factors guided by bleeding and testing): Hypothermia, dilution, and consumption can create a coagulopathy with diffuse oozing. Warming and balanced replacement of plasma, platelets, calcium, and other factors as indicated target the cause. The alternative “Give more red cells alone because the haemoglobin remains low” replaces oxygen carrying capacity alone. The alternatives “Stop all blood products until the platelet count is above normal”, “Give a large crystalloid bolus to dilute the remaining clotting factors”, and “Wait for visible clot to form before correcting temperature” worsen coagulation or delay treatment.
  285. The correct answer is D (Anterior cruciate ligament limiting anterior tibial translation inside the knee): The anterior cruciate ligament checks anterior movement of the tibia and contributes to rotational stability. A positive anterior draw with a soft endpoint after a twisting injury fits an anterior cruciate ligament tear. Posterior cruciate injury is tempting because it is also an intra-articular knee ligament, but it allows excessive posterior tibial translation. Collateral ligament testing remains useful because combined injuries change instability and treatment planning.
  286. The correct answer is B (Plateau pressure reflects alveolar distending pressure at end inspiration): With flow briefly stopped, pressure equilibrates across the circuit and approximates the pressure distending alveoli. The alternative “Plateau pressure estimates airway resistance when gas flow is zero” uses the pressure difference from peak to plateau for resistance. The alternative “Plateau pressure equals mean arterial pressure during positive pressure” mixes airway and circulatory pressures. The alternatives “Plateau pressure measures total compliance only during spontaneous breathing” and “Plateau pressure is unaffected by chest wall compliance or abdominal pressure” overlook the measurement conditions and the influence of chest wall mechanics.
  287. The correct answer is B (Axillary nerve affected by an anterior shoulder dislocation): The axillary nerve runs close to the inferior shoulder capsule and supplies the deltoid and lateral shoulder skin. Anterior dislocation can therefore cause lateral deltoid numbness, weak abduction, and a reduced shoulder sensation patch. The alternatives “Musculocutaneous nerve affected by a humeral shaft fracture” and “Radial nerve affected by a mid-shaft humeral fracture” describe upper limb nerve injuries linked more often to humeral shaft fractures. The alternatives “Median nerve affected by a supracondylar elbow fracture” and “Ulnar nerve affected by a medial epicondylar elbow injury” involve the elbow and do not explain the lateral shoulder sensory loss after dislocation.
  288. The correct answer is D (Inferior rectus muscle and infraorbital nerve near the fracture): Orbital floor fractures can trap orbital fat and the inferior rectus, restricting elevation of the globe. The infraorbital nerve travels in the orbital floor and explains reduced cheek and upper lip sensation. The optic canal and superior orbital fissure are not part of this floor injury pattern. The superior oblique may contribute to vertical diplopia, but its tendon is not the structure caught in a typical blowout fracture.
  289. The correct answer is D (The abducens nerve stretched along the clivus by raised pressure): The abducens nerve has a long intracranial course and can be stretched by downward brain displacement. It supplies lateral rectus, so injury impairs abduction of the eye. Oculomotor palsy usually adds ptosis and limited adduction or elevation. Trochlear palsy mainly disturbs downward and inward gaze, while optic nerve injury affects vision.
  290. The correct answer is D (Obtain urgent specialist assessment, secure the airway, and plan focused imaging): Malignant superior vena cava obstruction with positional stridor can worsen quickly and needs coordinated airway and cancer care. The alternative “Arrange routine outpatient review after three months of observation and repeat examination” ignores the airway warning. The alternative “Start antibiotics because facial swelling confirms a facial cellulitis” misdiagnoses facial swelling, and the alternative “Perform immediate thoracotomy before obtaining tissue diagnosis or staging” operates before diagnosis or staging. The alternative “Give long-term diuretics alone because venous obstruction cannot be treated” provides no effective plan for the obstructed vein or underlying mass.
  291. The correct answer is C (Perform immediate needle or finger decompression followed by chest drainage): Tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis, and decompression must occur before imaging in a rapidly deteriorating patient. The alternative “Obtain chest radiography before considering any pleural intervention” delays a life saving step. The alternative “Insert a chest drain only after computed tomography confirms the diagnosis” waits for unnecessary confirmation. The alternative “Give intravenous diuretics because distended neck veins indicate heart failure” misreads obstructive shock as heart failure, while the alternative “Arrange bronchoscopy to remove presumed blood from the airway” does not relieve pleural pressure.
  292. The correct answer is C (The lingual nerve after it has entered the floor of the mouth): The lingual nerve carries general sensation from the anterior two thirds of the tongue. It lies close to the oral mucosa and floor of the mouth along the planned resection. The chorda tympani contributes taste fibers but does not carry all general sensation. The hypoglossal nerve supplies tongue movement, while the glossopharyngeal nerve mainly serves the posterior third.
  293. The correct answer is D (C6 nerve root contributing to biceps and thumb sensation): C6 radiculopathy often causes pain into the lateral forearm and thumb, reduced biceps reflex, and weakness of elbow flexion. The C5 to C6 foramen is the expected level of compression. C5 is tempting because it also contributes to biceps function, but C6 involvement better explains thumb sensory symptoms and the common C5 to C6 level. Correlating the myotome, dermatome, and reflex changes supports a targeted diagnosis.
  294. The correct answer is A (A lesion proximal to the geniculate ganglion in the facial canal): A lesion before the geniculate ganglion can affect tear production through the greater petrosal nerve. More proximal injury also interrupts fibers to stapedius and chorda tympani, producing hyperacusis and anterior tongue taste loss. A mastoid segment lesion spares lacrimation and often stapedius function. An isolated chorda tympani lesion causes taste change without facial weakness, reduced tears, or hyperacusis.
  295. The correct answer is E (Catheter obstruction with bladder retention rather than low urine production): The palpable bladder, high bladder volume, and kinked tubing localise the problem to drainage rather than filtration. The alternative “Prerenal hypovolaemia with an expected low bladder volume and empty suprapubic region” predicts a small bladder volume. The alternative “Intrinsic tubular injury causing high urine output and progressive bladder distension” does not explain the large retained volume. The alternatives “Postrenal obstruction at the ureter despite an empty and nonpalpable bladder” and “Antidiuretic hormone excess producing a palpable bladder and high urine osmolality” place the cause at the ureter or name a hormonal mechanism while missing the tube obstruction.
  296. The correct answer is D (Angiotensin two induced efferent constriction with prostaglandin induced afferent dilation): Constricting the outlet and dilating the inlet supports glomerular capillary pressure when renal perfusion falls. The alternative “Afferent constriction with efferent dilation and reduced filtration pressure” reverses both effects. The alternative “Angiotensin two induced afferent dilation and prostaglandin induced efferent constriction” assigns angiotensin and prostaglandin to the wrong vessels. The alternative “Prostacyclin induced efferent constriction and angiotensin induced afferent dilation” reverses their sites, and the alternative “Equal constriction of both arterioles preserving renal blood flow” does not preserve filtration pressure as effectively.
  297. The correct answer is D (Urgent senior review with fluid resuscitation and urgent computed tomography): The combination of tachycardia, abdominal tenderness, oliguria, and rising inflammatory markers raises concern for an anastomotic leak or collection. Prompt senior assessment, resuscitation, and early imaging allow source control without delay. Oral antibiotics and simple observation are too weak for a potentially leaking anastomosis. The drain is not providing reassuring output and should not be removed as the main plan.
  298. The correct answer is A (The posterior cerebral artery encircling the midbrain and supplying occipital cortex): The basilar artery divides into the posterior cerebral arteries near the midbrain. These vessels pass around the cerebral peduncles and supply the occipital lobe and inferior temporal surfaces. The superior cerebellar artery supplies the upper cerebellum rather than the occipital cortex. The anterior inferior cerebellar artery relates mainly to the lower pons and internal ear.
  299. The correct answer is A (It provides the tension-bearing layer that supports the overlying skin): The galea is the strong aponeurotic sheet between the subcutaneous tissue and loose areolar layer. Restoring it distributes wound tension and improves alignment of the overlying skin. The pericranium is the outer periosteum and is not formed by the galea. The loose areolar layer permits scalp movement, which makes the firm attachment option tempting but incorrect.
  300. The correct answer is B (Medial collateral ligament attached to the medial meniscus): The medial collateral ligament restrains valgus stress and is attached to the medial meniscus, so valgus injury can open the medial joint line and sometimes involve the meniscus. The lateral collateral ligament is tempting because collateral injuries often produce joint line opening, but it restrains varus stress on the lateral side. Testing stress in extension and flexion helps separate isolated ligament injury from a more complex multiligament knee injury. The lateral side remains stable because varus stress did not load the injured medial restraint.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (Acknowledge the concern promptly, review the records, and seek senior advice about the clinical allegation): A complaint should be acknowledged, investigated through the local process, and answered with clear information while the patient remains informed. A serious clinical allegation should prompt early senior, clinical governance, and defence advice so the response is accurate and coordinated. The alternative “Tell the patient that nerve injury proves negligence and discuss settlement before clinical review” wrongly equates harm with negligence and makes premature promises. The alternatives “Delay acknowledgement until the consultant returns because team members must not respond independently”, “Ask the patient to withdraw the complaint before records can be reviewed by the clinical team”, and “Discuss the case in the public staff group to help colleagues understand what happened” delay the response, discourage the complaint, or breach confidentiality.
  2. The correct answer is C (Acute mesenteric ischaemia causing severe pain and later mucosal injury): Severe pain that seems greater than the examination suggests in a patient with atrial fibrillation and vascular disease can indicate arterial gut ischaemia. The alternative “Acute appendicitis causing localised right iliac fossa pain and guarding” usually localises to the right lower abdomen with guarding. The alternative “Uncomplicated constipation causing diffuse abdominal discomfort and reduced stool” lacks objective inflammatory or metabolic change. The alternatives “Cholecystitis causing right upper quadrant pain with positive Murphy sign” and “Urinary tract infection causing dysuria and suprapubic tenderness” have different localising symptoms.
  3. The correct answer is B (Adrenaline slows absorption but does not make an excessive dose safe): Adrenaline can reduce local blood flow, slow drug uptake, and prolong a block, but systemic toxicity remains possible. Dose limits vary by drug, route, adrenaline content, patient, and local policy. The alternatives “Adding adrenaline removes the need to follow product and site dose limits” and “Adrenaline permits unlimited repeated dosing during long procedures” ignore approved limits or imply unsafe flexibility, while “Adrenaline removes the need to aspirate before incremental injection” and “Adrenaline prevents seizures after accidental intravascular administration of local anaesthetic” overlook injection technique and accidental vascular delivery. This distinction preserves useful vasoconstriction without treating adrenaline as a safeguard against excess.
  4. The correct answer is A (It rapidly answers a focused intraoperative question about margin involvement): Frozen section is suited to a narrow question that changes the operation immediately, such as margin status. The alternative “It replaces permanent assessment for tumour grade and receptor status” overstates its role because permanent sections remain essential for full diagnosis and biomarkers. The alternative “It gives reliable receptor results even when the biopsy tissue sample is very small” assigns receptor testing to a method poorly suited to extensive marker work. The alternatives “It preserves cell detail better than formalin-fixed permanent sections” and “It determines distant stage by sampling several draining node basins” confuse frozen section with permanent tissue quality or distant staging.
  5. The correct answer is A (Osteosarcoma with malignant osteoid production by atypical bone cells): Osteosarcoma commonly affects the metaphysis in adolescents and produces malignant osteoid. A sunburst periosteal reaction and soft tissue extension reflect aggressive growth. Ewing sarcoma consists of small round cells without osteoid, while chondrosarcoma forms malignant cartilage. Giant cell tumour has many osteoclast-like cells, and metastatic carcinoma is uncommon in this age group and lacks neoplastic osteoid.
  6. The correct answer is A (Assess and secure the airway before managing the wound): Airway obstruction can cause rapid deterioration and must be managed before less immediately threatening problems. The noisy breathing and falling oxygen indicate that breathing and airway patency need urgent senior support. Wound pressure will also be needed, but it should not replace the first airway steps. Routine review or movement without assessment delays treatment of a hypoxic patient.
  7. The correct answer is C (The left brachiocephalic vein, which crosses transversely to the right.): This vein passes behind the thymus and in front of the major arterial branches. The superior vena cava is lower and mainly on the right. The azygos vein lies posteriorly over the right bronchus. The internal thoracic and inferior thyroid veins do not occupy this central retrothymic corridor.
  8. The correct answer is A (The temporal lobe compresses the right oculomotor nerve at the tentorial edge.): Uncal herniation stretches the oculomotor nerve and produces a dilated pupil that does not react to light. The abducens nerve supplies lateral rectus and does not control the pupil. Facial, hypoglossal, and optic nerve compression produce different signs. The contralateral arm weakness reflects pressure on the cerebral peduncle as herniation advances.
  9. The correct answer is C (Pathological stage because it records nodal and distant spread): Stage records the anatomical extent of disease and usually predicts outcome more strongly than grade. The alternative “Histological grade alone because it measures cellular resemblance” is useful but cannot distinguish local from metastatic disease. The alternatives “Largest tumour diameter because this records local disease volume” and “Mitotic count because it estimates the speed of cell division” describe growth features that do not replace staging information. The alternative “Patient age because it changes the expected survival for each patient” may influence treatment decisions, yet it does not describe the tumour's anatomical spread.
  10. The correct answer is A (Review dose and timing with renal function and local guidance): Low molecular weight heparin is cleared partly by the kidney, so reduced clearance can increase bleeding exposure. Dose and timing should follow renal function, operation risk, and local policy. Standard treatment dosing without review can be unsafe, while withholding prevention ignores high surgical risk. Aspirin is not an equivalent substitute, and stockings alone may not meet the full risk reduction need.
  11. The correct answer is E (The sciatic nerve, which passes behind the acetabulum and posterior column.): This nerve travels through the posterior hip region and can be injured during posterior approaches. Its injury can affect hamstrings and all muscles below the knee, producing foot drop and sensory loss. The femoral nerve is anterior in the groin. The obturator, superior gluteal, and lateral femoral cutaneous nerves produce different patterns without the full sciatic deficit.
  12. The correct answer is A (The left gonadal vein enters the renal vein, while the right enters the inferior vena cava.): The left renal vein crosses in front of the aorta and receives the left gonadal vein. The right gonadal vein usually drains directly into the inferior vena cava. The other alternatives reverse these sides or send the gonadal veins into the portal system. This asymmetry matters during left retroperitoneal dissection because the renal vein is a nearby landmark.
  13. The correct answer is A (Assess bone health, fall risk and treatment for osteoporosis): Repeated low-trauma fractures identify a high risk of osteoporosis and further injury. Assessment should address bone density, medicines, nutrition and fall causes, followed by appropriate bone treatment and safety measures. Repeated wrist radiographs do not reduce fracture risk, and bed rest weakens bone and muscle. Myeloma can cause fractures but is not the first conclusion without supporting features, and waiting for another break misses a clear prevention opportunity.
  14. The correct answer is C (Suspected bowel or mesenteric injury requiring urgent operative assessment): Extraluminal gas and unexplained free fluid after blunt trauma suggest perforation or serious mesenteric injury. Worsening pain with borderline haemodynamic status supports urgent exploration rather than prolonged observation. Simple bruising does not produce free gas, and a renal contusion does not explain the imaging pattern. Pancreatitis and an uncomplicated retroperitoneal haematoma would not normally create free intraperitoneal gas.
  15. The correct answer is A (Hydrogen and chloride losses produce a hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis): Gastric fluid is rich in hydrogen and chloride, so prolonged vomiting raises bicarbonate and lowers chloride. Low potassium reflects gastric and renal losses and can worsen the alkalosis by promoting renal hydrogen secretion. The alternative “Bicarbonate rich small bowel loss produces a normal gap metabolic acidosis” describes the opposite acid base effect of small bowel loss. The alternatives “Lactic acid accumulation from dehydration raises the measured bicarbonate level”, “Excess aldosterone produces metabolic acidosis with renal potassium wasting”, and “Hyperventilation lowers carbon dioxide and creates a primary metabolic alkalosis” misclassify bicarbonate, aldosterone or ventilation as the primary cause of the alkaline picture.
  16. The correct answer is B (Secure the airway and arrange urgent specialist control of the bleeding source): Large volume haemoptysis with confusion and poor airway protection can rapidly cause asphyxiation, so airway and bleeding control must happen urgently. The alternative “Arrange bronchoscopy only after bleeding has stopped spontaneously” waits while the patient deteriorates. The alternative “Give oral tranexamic acid and arrange routine respiratory clinic review” is too slow and indirect. The alternative “Perform immediate chest drain insertion before assessing airway protection” targets the pleural space, while the alternative “Start systemic anticoagulation because clot within the cavity is the likely source” could worsen active bleeding.
  17. The correct answer is E (Subarachnoid haemorrhage caused by a ruptured intracranial aneurysm): A thunderclap headache that reaches maximum intensity suddenly and occurs during exertion raises immediate concern for subarachnoid haemorrhage. Vomiting and meningeal irritation support bleeding into the subarachnoid space. Early computed tomography is the first test, and further investigation follows the local pathway if it is negative. Migraine and tension headache do not usually begin with this abrupt maximal intensity, and bacterial meningitis commonly causes fever.
  18. The correct answer is A (Escalate immediately for sepsis and surgical review while providing safe analgesia): Pain with guarding, fever, tachycardia, and falling urine output after surgery suggests a serious intra-abdominal complication. The alternative “Give more opioid alone and wait to see whether pain settles overnight” masks deterioration without treating the cause. The alternative “Discharge with oral analgesia because postoperative pain commonly lasts several days” is unsafe for an acute abdomen. The alternatives “Arrange routine physiotherapy before considering examination or blood tests” and “Start long-term neuropathic medicine without assessing the acute abdomen” delay assessment of possible sepsis or organ injury.
  19. The correct answer is C (Right tension pneumothorax causing obstructive shock and respiratory failure): Tracheal shift away from the affected side, absent breath sounds, shock, and distended neck veins form the expected pattern. Pressure in the right chest impairs venous return and rapidly reduces cardiac output. The alternative “Right massive haemothorax causing shock without pressure effect” can cause shock but usually produces dullness without the tension shift described, while the alternative “Cardiac tamponade causing distended neck veins and quiet heart sounds” would not explain unilateral absent breath sounds. The alternatives “Left-sided flail chest causing paradoxical chest wall movement” and “Pulmonary contusion causing focal crackles, low oxygen, and worsening breathlessness” do not account for tracheal deviation with obstructive shock.
  20. The correct answer is E (The superior mesenteric vein forms the portal vein directly behind the neck.): The splenic vein joins the superior mesenteric vein behind the pancreatic neck to create the portal vein. The neck therefore overlies a major venous confluence that must be protected during transection. The pancreatic tail approaches the splenic hilum, while the inferior vena cava lies further posterior and to the right. The superior mesenteric artery is nearby but usually lies to the left of the vein.
  21. The correct answer is C (An indirect inguinal hernia entering the deep ring lateral to the vessels.): An indirect hernia follows the inguinal canal after entering the deep ring lateral to the inferior epigastric vessels. A direct hernia passes through the posterior wall medial to those vessels. A femoral hernia lies below the inguinal ligament, while Spigelian and obturator hernias occur at different anatomical sites. The observed position of the deep ring makes the indirect route most likely.
  22. The correct answer is B (Resolution of ketones leaves a hyperchloraemic normal gap metabolic acidosis): During ketoacidosis treatment, ketones are metabolised and bicarbonate can be regenerated, while chloride rich fluids expand the extracellular space and maintain a normal gap acidosis. Negative ketones, high chloride, low bicarbonate and a normal gap fit this recovery pattern. The alternative “Ongoing ketone production maintains a high gap despite negative ketone testing” contradicts the negative ketones, while the alternative “Bicarbonate loss from vomiting explains a normal gap acidosis after treatment” lacks supporting gastrointestinal loss. The alternatives “Excess lactate from insulin therapy causes the persistent low bicarbonate level” and “Respiratory alkalosis alone reduces bicarbonate through renal compensation” do not explain a normal gap acidosis with a stable clinical state.
  23. The correct answer is A (Acute haemarthrosis from an anterior cruciate ligament rupture): Rapid tense swelling within hours of a twisting injury usually represents blood in the knee. A pop, inability to extend fully and no fracture support an anterior cruciate ligament tear, though examination and imaging confirm the diagnosis. Degenerative tears cause more gradual swelling, and patellar tendinopathy causes localised tendon pain. Septic arthritis usually has fever and systemic illness, while osteoarthritis develops over years rather than minutes.
  24. The correct answer is A (Carbon monoxide binds haemoglobin and pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhaemoglobin): Standard pulse oximetry treats carboxyhaemoglobin as if it were oxyhaemoglobin, so the displayed saturation can remain falsely reassuring. Carbon monoxide also reduces oxygen content and shifts the dissociation curve, which explains confusion and acidosis. The alternative “High inspired oxygen has corrected tissue oxygen delivery despite persistent confusion” ignores ongoing tissue hypoxia, while the alternative “A normal arterial oxygen tension confirms adequate haemoglobin oxygen content” confuses dissolved oxygen tension with total content. The alternatives “Carbon dioxide retention has displaced oxygen from haemoglobin binding sites” and “Peripheral vasoconstriction produces a falsely low reading rather than a false normal” name the wrong gas and the wrong direction of the oximetry error.
  25. The correct answer is C (Prescribe risk appropriate prophylaxis while checking bleeding contraindications): Obesity, cancer, pelvic surgery, reduced mobility, and prior venous thromboembolism produce high risk that calls for guideline based prevention. The bleeding risk, kidney function, operation, and timing guide the method and dose. Mechanical prevention does not replace medicines in every high risk patient, and prophylaxis should be planned rather than delayed. A treatment dose is not automatically correct for prevention.
  26. The correct answer is C (Urgent pericardial drainage with simultaneous surgical and blood product support): Penetrating chest trauma with pericardial fluid and impaired cardiac filling indicates likely cardiac tamponade. The patient needs urgent drainage, usually in theatre with surgical repair and ongoing resuscitation for associated cardiac injury. A chest drain does not relieve pericardial pressure, and large fluid boluses cannot overcome mechanical restriction indefinitely. Needle drainage may be a temporary measure, but it does not replace urgent surgical control.
  27. The correct answer is A (The left renal vein crosses anterior to the aorta to reach the inferior vena cava.): The longer left renal vein passes in front of the aorta and often receives the left gonadal and adrenal veins. This broad, low-pressure vessel can bleed heavily if avulsed. The right renal vein does not cross the aorta, and the right gonadal vein drains directly into the vena cava. These sided differences are important during renal and retroperitoneal dissection.
  28. The correct answer is B (Urgent computed tomography of the head according to local head injury guidance): Anticoagulation and amnesia after head injury are recognised features that lower the threshold for urgent head imaging. Computed tomography can detect intracranial bleeding and guide reversal of anticoagulation and neurosurgical referral. A normal Glasgow Coma Scale score does not exclude dangerous bleeding in this context. Skull radiographs miss intracranial injury, lumbar puncture is unsafe before excluding raised pressure, and delayed magnetic resonance imaging risks late diagnosis.
  29. The correct answer is E (Right extradural haematoma with mass effect and impending herniation): A lucid interval followed by deterioration, ipsilateral pupil dilation, and contralateral weakness is typical of an expanding extradural collection. It can compress the temporal lobe and cerebral peduncle as herniation develops. The alternative “Diffuse axonal injury causing immediate prolonged coma without focal signs” usually causes immediate severe impairment without a lucid interval, while the alternative “Chronic subdural haematoma causing gradual confusion over several weeks” evolves over weeks. The alternatives “Traumatic subarachnoid haemorrhage causing isolated neck stiffness and headache” and “Simple concussion causing transient symptoms without focal deficit” do not explain focal weakness and pupillary change.
  30. The correct answer is E (The facial nerve, which divides within the parotid gland.): Its branches supply the muscles of facial expression and are the main danger during parotid surgery. Injury causes asymmetric smile, incomplete eye closure, and loss of forehead wrinkling. The trigeminal motor branch controls chewing. Hypoglossal injury affects the tongue, spinal accessory injury affects shoulder and neck muscles, and lingual injury alters tongue sensation.
  31. The correct answer is E (The thoracic duct, which carries intestinal lymph into the venous system.): Injury causes a chylothorax, with milky lymphatic fluid collecting in the pleural cavity. Bleeding from the azygos vein or bronchial arteries produces blood, not milky fluid. Recurrent laryngeal nerve injury causes voice or airway problems. Phrenic nerve injury affects diaphragm movement but does not produce chyle.
  32. The correct answer is B (Anterior cruciate ligament after a noncontact twisting injury): A pivot with a pop, rapid swelling and giving way is a common anterior cruciate ligament injury pattern. Blood in the joint causes early tense swelling, and instability appears with rotational movement. A posterior cruciate injury usually follows direct impact to the front of the knee, while a medial collateral injury follows a valgus force. Patellar tendon rupture prevents active extension, and a degenerative meniscal tear produces gradual mechanical symptoms.
  33. The correct answer is D (Decompress the stomach and assess for ileus or obstruction): Persistent vomiting and distension with absent flatus suggest ileus or obstruction, and gastric decompression reduces vomiting and aspiration risk. Fluid and electrolyte status, medicines, and the abdomen should be reviewed while the cause is investigated. Immediate surgery is not supported without peritonitis or another emergency feature. Laxatives and extra opioids may worsen bowel inactivity.
  34. The correct answer is B (Burkitt lymphoma with high proliferation and t(8;14) translocation): A rapidly growing childhood jaw mass with starry sky histology and a high mitotic rate strongly suggests Burkitt lymphoma. The translocation places MYC beside an immunoglobulin locus and drives proliferation. Mantle cell lymphoma has cyclin D1 overexpression but usually affects older adults, and marginal zone lymphoma is slower. Lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma and Langerhans histiocytosis lack this genetic and histological pattern.
  35. The correct answer is A (Confounding because wound contamination affects both treatment choice and infection): More contaminated wounds can make clinicians select the new therapy and can independently raise infection risk. This third factor distorts the apparent therapy result. The alternative “Random error because observational studies lack reliable comparison groups” wrongly treats all observational studies as lacking valid comparisons, and the alternative “Measurement bias because infection was diagnosed in both groups” does not describe bias by itself. The alternatives “Publication bias because only positive results reached the journal” and “Lead-time bias because treatment changed the date of infection” concern publication or timing rather than confounding by wound severity.
  36. The correct answer is E (Give early antibiotics, cover the wound, splint and arrange urgent debridement): Visible bone through a wound defines an open fracture, which needs early antibiotic cover and prompt surgical cleaning. A sterile dressing protects the wound, a splint reduces movement and pain, and urgent orthopaedic care guides fixation. Closing the skin in the emergency department traps contamination and risks deep infection. Delayed antibiotics, unprotected transfer and oral treatment alone all increase the chance of serious bone infection.
  37. The correct answer is A (Squamous cell carcinoma associated with chronic immunosuppression and sunlight): Long-term immunosuppression and ultraviolet exposure strongly increase squamous cell carcinoma risk, especially on the lip. Invasive keratinocytes and keratin pearls confirm squamous differentiation. Basal cell carcinoma forms basaloid nests, while Merkel cell carcinoma consists of small blue neuroendocrine cells. Kaposi sarcoma and cutaneous lymphoma produce vascular or lymphoid infiltrates rather than keratinising epithelial invasion.
  38. The correct answer is C (The intercostobrachial nerve, which supplies sensation to the axilla and medial upper arm.): It crosses the axilla and is often divided during lymph node clearance. Its injury produces the localized sensory patch without major weakness. The musculocutaneous and median nerves have motor roles that are preserved here. Long thoracic and thoracodorsal injuries would affect scapular movement or shoulder strength.
  39. The correct answer is A (The femoral nerve, femoral artery, femoral vein, and lymphatics.): This is the standard lateral-to-medial sequence in the femoral triangle. The femoral nerve lies outside the femoral sheath, while the artery and vein lie within it. The lymphatics occupy the most medial area near the femoral canal. These landmarks reduce confusion during groin puncture and dissection.
  40. The correct answer is E (Fournier gangrene requiring resuscitation, antibiotics and immediate debridement): Rapidly spreading scrotal and perineal pain with dusky skin, crepitus and shock indicates necrotising infection. Fournier gangrene needs broad-spectrum antibiotics and urgent surgical removal of dead tissue, alongside resuscitation. Cellulitis does not cause crepitus or such rapid systemic collapse, and torsion causes testicular pain rather than spreading perineal necrosis. Epididymitis and hydrocele do not produce fever with crepitus or dusky infected skin.
  41. The correct answer is B (An indirect inguinal hernia passing through the lateral deep ring beside the epigastric vessels): An indirect hernia follows the inguinal canal after entering the deep ring lateral to the inferior epigastric vessels. A direct hernia passes through the posterior wall medial to those vessels. A femoral hernia lies below the inguinal ligament near the femoral vein. Spigelian and Richter hernias do not follow this deep ring route.
  42. The correct answer is E (Injury to the pancreatic tail during ligation at the splenic hilum.): The pancreatic tail reaches the splenic hilum and is vulnerable when adhesions obscure the plane. A pancreatic leak produces clear enzyme-rich fluid rather than blood from a named vessel. Gastric or renal injury would cause different findings. The inferior mesenteric vein is not the main structure beside this upper left hilar dissection.
  43. The correct answer is D (Ward observation, repeated examination, and immediate access to intervention): A stable patient without active bleeding may be managed without immediate surgery, but splenic injury can evolve. Serial examination and rapid access to imaging, angioembolisation, or theatre make nonoperative care safe. The alternative “Immediate splenectomy before instability or active bleeding is documented” is too aggressive for current stability, while the alternative “Discharge home with advice to return if pain becomes severe” removes essential monitoring. The alternatives “Nonoperative care without serial examination or hospital monitoring” and “Treat the injury as infected solely because solid organ trauma occurred” either omit monitoring or replace proven injury management with unnecessary antibiotics.
  44. The correct answer is C (Low-dose noncontrast computed tomography of the kidneys, ureters and bladder): Low-dose noncontrast computed tomography detects most stones and shows their size, position and obstructive effect. It is the standard adult test when pregnancy is excluded, and the low-dose technique reduces radiation. Ultrasound can miss small ureteric stones and may not define obstruction fully, while a plain radiograph cannot assess obstruction. Magnetic resonance imaging is less reliable for stones, and laparoscopy is not a diagnostic pathway for renal colic.
  45. The correct answer is E (Reduced red cell two, three diphosphoglycerate shifts the dissociation curve left): Stored red cells lose two, three diphosphoglycerate, which normally reduces haemoglobin oxygen affinity and supports unloading. A left shifted curve holds oxygen more tightly and can temporarily reduce delivery. The alternative “A rightward shift in the oxygen dissociation curve improves tissue unloading” describes the opposite shift, while the alternative “Higher haemoglobin affinity increases oxygen release at low tissue oxygen tension” confuses higher affinity with easier release. The alternatives “Carbon dioxide retention immediately increases red cell oxygen affinity” and “A fall in body temperature shifts the oxygen curve to the right” also reverse the known effects of carbon dioxide and cooling on the curve.
  46. The correct answer is D (Low cardiac output reduces oxygen delivery despite adequate arterial saturation): Oxygen delivery depends on both arterial oxygen content and the volume of blood pumped each minute. A normal saturation and acceptable haemoglobin cannot compensate when cardiac output is markedly reduced. The alternative “A saturation of 97 percent excludes a clinically important oxygen delivery problem” treats saturation as a complete measure of delivery, while the alternative “Haemoglobin concentration alone determines oxygen delivery to peripheral tissues” omits blood flow. The alternatives “Oxygen extraction falls when cardiac output is low and venous oxygen rises” and “High haemoglobin affinity causes failure of oxygen loading within the lungs” describe extraction or loading problems rather than the measured pump failure.
  47. The correct answer is B (QT prolongation and tendon injury risks may make another agent safer): Fluoroquinolones can prolong the QT interval and are associated with tendon injury, especially in older or frail patients. Electrolyte correction and review of other medicines help reduce avoidable risk, and an alternative may be preferable. The alternatives “Fluoroquinolones reliably shorten the QT interval and protect against falls”, “Low potassium removes any concern about drug-induced rhythm disturbance”, and “Fluoroquinolones have no interaction with other rhythm-affecting medicines” deny established safety concerns. The alternative “Tendon rupture is limited to topical exposure and does not follow systemic treatment” incorrectly limits tendon injury to topical use.
  48. The correct answer is C (Perform bladder scan and relieve retention with catheterisation): Lower abdominal pressure and inability to void after spinal anaesthesia suggest urinary retention, and a bladder scan can confirm it quickly. Relief prevents bladder overdistension and pain while the cause is reviewed. Reassurance alone can allow dangerous bladder distension, while a fluid bolus without assessment may worsen it. Infection is not supported without fever or urinary infective symptoms.
  49. The correct answer is D (Metastatic prostatic adenocarcinoma causing extradural spinal cord compression): Prostate cancer commonly spreads to bone and can extend into the extradural space, producing cord compression. The destructive vertebral lesion explains pain, weakness and bladder dysfunction. Haematoma and abscess require trauma or infection, while an astrocytoma is intramedullary. Degenerative disc disease causes foraminal narrowing but not a destructive vertebral mass with extradural compression.
  50. The correct answer is A (Citrate in stored blood binds ionised calcium and impairs cardiac contractility): Blood products contain citrate that chelates calcium, and rapid large volume transfusion can overwhelm hepatic citrate metabolism. Low ionised calcium causes tingling, tetany, QT prolongation, weak contraction and reduced vasopressor response. The alternative “Hyperkalaemia from stored red cells causes calcium entry into cardiac muscle” describes hyperkalaemia rather than calcium binding, while the alternative “Metabolic alkalosis from plasma infusion lowers ionised calcium concentration” lacks evidence of alkalosis. The alternative “Citrate toxicity raises ionised calcium and reduces vascular smooth muscle tone” reverses the calcium change and the alternative “Hypomagnesaemia from crystalloid dilution increases calcium binding to albumin” proposes an unsupported magnesium mechanism.
  51. The correct answer is A (Overshoot may cause osmotic demyelination despite improvement in the seizure): Chronic hyponatraemia allows brain adaptation, so a rapid sodium rise can shrink brain cells and injure myelin. Treatment of the seizure is urgent, but the rate and total rise still need careful limits once the immediate danger is controlled. The alternatives “The rise is safe because seizures justify any speed of sodium correction” and “Further rapid correction is required to restore sodium to the normal range” treat the initial emergency as permission for uncontrolled correction and ignore osmotic demyelination. The alternatives “The sodium change is independent of the seizure and does not need review” and “Hypertonic saline should continue until full neurological recovery is observed” underestimate the risk from the large sodium change and give unsafe stopping rules.
  52. The correct answer is D (Pulmonary oedema creates diffusion impairment and marked ventilation perfusion mismatch): Poor left ventricular function raises pulmonary venous pressure, and fluid in the alveoli and interstitium impairs oxygen transfer and creates regional mismatch. Bilateral shadowing and an enlarged heart fit this mechanism. The alternative “Pure hypoventilation has reduced alveolar oxygen without ventilation perfusion mismatch” describes hypoventilation without explaining pulmonary oedema, while the alternative “A fixed intracardiac shunt is present and responds fully to high oxygen” proposes a fixed shunt that would not improve with oxygen. The alternatives “Dead space from pulmonary embolism has caused alveolar flooding and cardiac enlargement” and “Reduced inspired oxygen concentration is the primary cause of the bilateral shadowing” do not match the cardiac and radiographic findings.
  53. The correct answer is B (Type II error because the study lacked power to detect benefit): A false negative occurs when a real difference exists but the study does not detect it, often because the sample or expected effect is too small. A wide interval also shows meaningful uncertainty. The alternative “Type I error because the study produced a false positive conclusion” describes a false positive, while the alternatives “Observer bias because surgeons knew which operation was used” and “Lead-time bias because follow-up began before treatment started” describe sources of systematic error. The alternative “Immortal time bias because survival was measured before entry” uses survival timing that is not needed to explain a power problem.
  54. The correct answer is B (Excessive minute ventilation is producing hypocapnia and cerebral vasoconstriction): A raised ventilator rate has driven carbon dioxide down, and the alkaline pH with near normal bicarbonate identifies respiratory alkalosis. Carbon dioxide is a cerebral vasodilator, so hypocapnia reduces cerebral blood flow and may impair perfusion after neurosurgery. The alternative “Metabolic acidosis is causing compensatory alveolar hyperventilation after the ventilator change” is wrong because there is no metabolic acidosis. The alternatives “Respiratory acidosis is lowering cerebral blood flow through carbon dioxide retention” and “Metabolic alkalosis is producing hypoventilation and reduced cerebral perfusion” reverse the carbon dioxide and pH changes, while the alternative “A normal acid base state is present because bicarbonate remains near normal” ignores the abnormal arterial values.
  55. The correct answer is A (The portal vein lies anterior, with the inferior vena cava posterior.): These vessels form the main anterior and posterior boundaries of the epiploic foramen. The caudate lobe lies superiorly and the first part of the duodenum inferiorly. Reversing the caval and portal positions misrepresents the safe anatomy. The gastroduodenal artery and common bile duct are nearby, but they do not form these main front and back relations.
  56. The correct answer is A (The right main bronchus is wider, shorter, and more vertical than the left.): Its vertical course provides a straighter path for aspirated material. The left bronchus is longer, narrower, and more horizontal, so it receives less aspirated material. A bronchus above the carina is not normal anatomy and does not explain the pattern. Equal bronchial angles contradict the usual difference that favors right-sided aspiration.
  57. The correct answer is A (Take blood cultures, give antibiotics, and remove the infected line promptly): Rigors during infusion, exit site inflammation, and falling pressure suggest a catheter related bloodstream infection and possible sepsis. Cultures should be obtained promptly, empirical antibiotics started under local policy, and the source device removed. Forceful flushing can spread infection, while antipyretics and routine review delay urgent treatment. Removing the line before cultures can lose useful diagnostic information.
  58. The correct answer is E (Compartment syndrome can occur with a palpable pulse and needs urgent fasciotomy assessment.): Small vessel and nerve compression can cause severe pain and sensory loss while a large foot pulse remains present. A pulse does not exclude the diagnosis. Deep vein thrombosis and isolated nerve injury do not explain the tense swollen compartment with severe stretch pain. Waiting for pulse loss could permit permanent muscle and nerve damage.
  59. The correct answer is D (Keep him calm and urgently gather senior airway expertise): Fever, drooling, muffled voice, and a child sitting forward suggest a threatened upper airway. Disturbance can precipitate complete obstruction, so calm handling and early anaesthetic, ear, nose, and throat support are priorities. The alternatives “Inspect the throat with a tongue depressor before calling for help” and “Ask the child to lie flat so the airway can be examined carefully” risk airway collapse through distress. The alternatives “Send him alone to radiology for a lateral neck radiograph first” and “Give oral fluids and observe for improvement over several hours” separate the child from needed expertise or delay definitive airway planning.
  60. The correct answer is E (The common peroneal nerve, which winds around the fibular neck.): Compression at the fibular neck weakens ankle dorsiflexion and eversion and reduces dorsal foot sensation. Tibial nerve injury would affect plantar flexion and sensation on the sole. Femoral and obturator injuries affect the thigh. The saphenous nerve is sensory and supplies the medial leg rather than the dorsum of the foot.
  61. The correct answer is C (Posterior urethral valves causing congenital obstruction of the male urethra): Congenital obstructive valves in the posterior urethra cause high-pressure bladder emptying, bilateral kidney swelling and poor urine flow in boys. Voiding cystourethrography showing a dilated posterior urethra supports the diagnosis. Reflux alone does not explain the thick-walled bladder and urethral obstruction, while hypospadias affects the opening position. A neurogenic bladder requires a neurologic cause, and ureteropelvic junction obstruction usually affects one kidney.
  62. The correct answer is B (Asymmetric growth with atypical melanocytes and dermal invasion): Melanoma shows disordered architecture, cytological atypia and often dermal spread beyond the epidermal compartment. These features reflect loss of normal melanocyte control. Uniform confined nests can occur in benign naevi, while pigment in keratinocytes is not a reliable sign of malignancy. Symmetry and stable margins favour a benign lesion, and extra collagen alone does not establish a melanocytic tumour.
  63. The correct answer is D (Fat globules enter veins, lodge in pulmonary capillaries, and trigger systemic inflammation): Pulmonary vascular obstruction causes hypoxia, while platelet activation and endothelial injury contribute to rash and neurological features. The alternative “Direct systemic arterial fat emboli cause all features without pulmonary involvement” skips the lung phase and incorrectly claims arterial emboli explain every sign. The alternative “Bone marrow emboli lodge only in the liver and produce jaundice alone” places the first filter only in the liver. The alternatives “Calcium depletion from bone injury causes fever and petechiae independently” and “Excessive crystalloid alone causes fat embolism after femoral shaft fracture” propose unsupported causes for this syndrome.
  64. The correct answer is A (Insulin has shifted potassium into cells despite a large total body deficit): Diabetic ketoacidosis commonly causes major total body potassium loss even when admission potassium appears normal because acidosis and insulin deficiency keep potassium outside cells. Insulin then drives potassium intracellularly and can expose dangerous hypokalaemia. The alternative “The initial measurement overestimated potassium because of haemolysis” invents a sampling error, and the alternative “Fluid resuscitation has increased renal potassium excretion without insulin effect” ignores the strong insulin effect. The alternatives “Metabolic alkalosis is driving potassium into the intracellular compartment” and “Potassium stores were normal before treatment and have now become depleted” reverse the acid base shift or deny the pre-treatment potassium deficit.
  65. The correct answer is B (Use an individualised insulin and glucose monitoring plan during fasting): Patients with type 1 diabetes need basal insulin even while fasting to prevent ketosis, with meal insulin adjusted to intake and glucose. Frequent glucose checks and a written perioperative plan allow prompt treatment of high or low levels. Omitting all insulin risks diabetic ketoacidosis, while giving only rapid insulin removes essential background cover. Starting an SGLT2 inhibitor on the day adds an inappropriate perioperative risk.
  66. The correct answer is C (Catheter-associated bacteriuria without urinary symptoms often represents benign colonisation alone): A catheter can acquire organisms and produce cloudy urine without tissue invasion or symptoms. Treating asymptomatic colonisation exposes patients to resistance, C difficile, and drug toxicity without clear benefit. The alternatives “Cloudy urine alone proves invasive urinary infection requiring treatment”, “Any positive catheter urine culture requires a ten-day antibiotic course”, and “Treat every positive catheter culture before considering catheter removal” treat the result alone. The alternative “Urine testing should be repeated daily until the specimen becomes sterile” creates repeated testing without a clinical purpose.
  67. The correct answer is D (Use standard resuscitation and give lipid emulsion according to the protocol): Serious local anaesthetic toxicity can cause seizures and difficult ventricular arrhythmias, especially with bupivacaine. Treatment combines standard airway, breathing, circulation, and cardiac arrest care with lipid emulsion as specified by the current rescue protocol. The alternatives “Give more bupivacaine to test whether conduction improves with repeat exposure” and “Stop resuscitation after one short cycle because bupivacaine effects may persist” give unsafe or premature responses. The alternatives “Delay chest compressions until an antihistamine has fully taken effect” and “Use lipid emulsion while omitting standard cardiac arrest treatment during resuscitation” replace proven resuscitation components with ineffective or incomplete actions.
  68. The correct answer is D (Chronic subdural haematoma causing gradual decline and crescentic bleeding): Older age and anticoagulation increase the risk of bleeding from bridging veins onto the brain surface. A crescent-shaped collection with headache, confusion and gradual weakness is typical of a chronic subdural haematoma. Symptomatic or enlarging collections may need surgical drainage. Extradural bleeding is biconvex and often acute, subarachnoid bleeding causes sudden severe headache, and stroke does not create this extra-axial collection.
  69. The correct answer is C (Call for urgent senior review and assess for drain related complications): Acute deterioration after drain insertion requires urgent evaluation for obstruction, malposition, bleeding, lung injury, or another cause of shock. The alternative “Clamp the drain indefinitely and arrange routine chest radiography next day” waits despite instability. The alternative “Give oral analgesia and observe because bubbling confirms correct drainage” wrongly treats bubbling as sufficient reassurance. The alternatives “Remove the drain immediately at the bedside without specialist assessment” and “Start diuretics because rapid breathing indicates acute pulmonary oedema” make major assumptions before the complication is assessed.
  70. The correct answer is D (Colonic absorption of urine chloride produces a normal gap metabolic acidosis): Bowel mucosa exposed to urine can absorb chloride and ammonium while losing bicarbonate, creating a hyperchloraemic normal gap acidosis. The high chloride, low bicarbonate and normal gap fit this mechanism. The alternatives “Lactic acid accumulation from hypoperfusion creates an elevated anion gap” and “Ketone production from prolonged starvation elevates the measured anion gap” would widen the anion gap through lactate or ketones. The alternatives “Mineralocorticoid excess increases bicarbonate generation and produces alkalosis” and “Renal tubular bicarbonate loss causes a normal gap acidosis after diversion” propose alkalosis or renal bicarbonate loss rather than the characteristic diversion related physiology.
  71. The correct answer is B (Hollow viscus perforation with abdominal contamination requiring emergency laparotomy): Free air near the colon is strong evidence of bowel perforation, and abdominal fluid with guarding supports contamination and peritonitis. Prompt surgery controls the leak and cleans the peritoneal cavity. The alternative “Isolated splenic contusion without active bleeding or free fluid” describes a stable solid organ injury without free air, while the alternative “Renal parenchymal laceration with stable haematoma and no extravasation” lacks perforation. The alternatives “Mesenteric haematoma without bowel ischaemia or any associated perforation” and “Retroperitoneal haematoma arising from a stable pelvic ring fracture” may need observation but do not explain free air and spreading abdominal contamination.
  72. The correct answer is C (The white line of Toldt along the lateral reflection of the colon.): Incising this line allows the colon to be reflected while the ureter and gonadal vessels stay in the retroperitoneum. The epiploic foramen and lesser omentum belong to the upper abdominal dissection. The linea semilunaris and linea alba are abdominal wall landmarks and do not guide lateral colonic mobilisation. Staying along the correct reflection reduces the risk of retroperitoneal injury.
  73. The correct answer is C (The spleen, which lies beneath the left hemidiaphragm near low left ports.): A low left lateral port can cross the diaphragm and enter the splenic area. The liver and right kidney lie on the opposite side from a low left port. The stomach is more central and is not the immediate organ named for this lateral danger zone. The pancreas lies retroperitoneally and is not the first structure beneath this low lateral entry.
  74. The correct answer is C (Aspirate the joint before antibiotics and arrange urgent drainage and treatment): A hot joint with fever, severe pain and immune suppression requires urgent assessment for septic arthritis. Joint fluid should be obtained before antibiotics when this will not delay care, then sent for cell count, crystals, Gram stain and culture. Drainage and intravenous antibiotics are central treatments, while the nearby ulcer is a possible entry point.   75. The correct answer is B (The right main bronchus is wider, shorter, and more vertical than the left bronchus.): Its nearly straight downward course gives aspirated material a more direct route into the right lung.   76. The correct answer is C (Severe anaemia lowers arterial oxygen content despite a normal saturation): Most arterial oxygen is bound to haemoglobin, so a very low haemoglobin concentration reduces oxygen content even when every available binding site is saturated. Confusion, cold extremities and rising lactate show that compensatory output is no longer sufficient. The alternative “Low saturation has directly limited the amount of oxygen bound to haemoglobin” contradicts the measured saturation, while the alternative “High cardiac output has reduced the time available for alveolar gas exchange” misstates the effect of high output on gas exchange. The alternatives “The lactate is unrelated to oxygen delivery because blood pressure remains adequate” and “Oxygen therapy alone can restore oxygen carrying capacity in severe anaemia” overlook the central role of oxygen carrying capacity.
  77. The correct answer is A (Insulinoma causing endogenous hyperinsulinism and recurrent fasting hypoglycaemia): A pancreatic neuroendocrine tumour that secretes insulin causes high insulin with high C-peptide during hypoglycaemia. Fasting attacks that improve after glucose intake are typical. Exogenous insulin lowers C-peptide because the patient's own pancreatic secretion is suppressed, while sulfonylurea use also lacks a pancreatic mass. Adrenal insufficiency and IGF-2 secreting tumours reduce insulin action or production rather than producing this endogenous insulin pattern.
  78. The correct answer is E (Homologous recombination repair failure from a germline BRCA1 mutation): BRCA1 helps repair double-strand DNA breaks by homologous recombination. Loss of this repair allows mutations to accumulate and strongly increases breast and ovarian cancer risk. Mismatch repair and APC defects belong to colorectal cancer pathways, while p53 gain of function is not the BRCA1 mechanism. HER2 amplification drives growth in a subset of breast cancers but does not explain this germline repair defect.
  79. The correct answer is B (Escalate for urgent review and assess for active bleeding): Rapid fresh blood loss with a rising pulse and falling pressure suggests active bleeding even when the wound dressing looks dry. Urgent senior review, resuscitation, blood tests, and assessment for operative control are required. Clamping or removing the drain can hide ongoing loss and delay treatment. Oral iron and routine review are inadequate for acute haemorrhage.
  80. The correct answer is C (Urgent angiographic embolisation with continued blood product support): Ongoing pelvic haemorrhage without intraperitoneal fluid can arise from pelvic arterial injury and may be treated by urgent embolisation when that service is available. Removing the binder can destabilise clot, and immediate laparotomy may not reach the bleeding source. Observation and magnetic resonance imaging would delay lifesaving haemorrhage control.
  81. The correct answer is E (The median nerve is compressed in the carpal tunnel and supplies the thenar muscles.): This produces numbness in the median nerve digits and weakness of thumb abduction while sparing little finger sensation.   82. The correct answer is C (Primary hyperparathyroidism from a solitary parathyroid adenoma causing excess calcium release): A single enlarged gland with high calcium, low phosphate and raised parathyroid hormone fits a parathyroid adenoma. Excess hormone increases bone resorption and renal calcium retention while lowering phosphate. Secondary disease follows chronic kidney failure and usually enlarges multiple glands, while tertiary disease develops after long-standing secondary stimulation. PTH-related peptide is low or undetectable in primary hyperparathyroidism, and familial hypocalciuric hypercalcaemia has low urinary calcium.
  83. The correct answer is C (The superior gluteal nerve, which supplies gluteus medius and minimus.): Weak hip abductors allow the pelvis to drop on the opposite side during single-leg support, producing a positive Trendelenburg sign.   84. The correct answer is E (Serial lactate clearance with mental status and urine output trends): Lactate trends, mentation, and urine output reflect whether tissue perfusion is improving over time. These measures help detect ongoing shock even when blood pressure appears better. The alternative “A single haemoglobin value taken at the time of arrival” is a single laboratory value that changes slowly, while the alternative “One systolic blood pressure reading after the first blood unit” uses one snapshot of pressure. The alternatives “Repeated abdominal springing to judge whether bleeding has stopped” and “A chest radiograph alone to estimate whole-body perfusion” either risk more bleeding or cannot provide a broad bedside measure of perfusion response.
  85. The alternatives “Tension pneumothorax with tracheal shift and absent breath sounds” and “Massive haemothorax with dullness, absent breath sounds, and severe shock” primarily affect the pleural space and would not directly explain pericardial fluid.   86. The trigger must be removed at once while help comes, dantrolene is given, cooling starts, and complications are treated. The alternative “Continue anaesthesia while giving dantrolene and external cooling measures” wrongly continues anaesthesia with a trigger agent.   87. The correct answer is C (Redose according to protocol during prolonged operation or major blood loss): Prophylactic drug levels can fall during a long operation and after substantial bleeding, leaving tissue exposed.   88. The correct answer is B (Ischaemic muscle necrosis from raised compartment pressure and impaired tissue perfusion): Fracture swelling within a closed fascial compartment raises pressure, reduces capillary flow and causes ischaemic necrosis.   89.   99. The correct answer is A (Neurogenic shock from loss of sympathetic vascular tone after cord injury): Cervical cord injury can interrupt sympathetic outflow and remove normal vascular tone.   100. The correct answer is E (Anastomotic leak with established intra-abdominal sepsis and peritonitis): Fever, organ dysfunction, guarding, and raised lactate several days after colectomy fit an abdominal source such as an anastomotic leak.   101.   102. The correct answer is E (Delayed splenic rupture after a subcapsular haematoma expands and breaks): Trauma can cause bleeding beneath the splenic capsule, which may tamponade initially and then rupture days later. The interval of temporary improvement followed by shock fits this sequence.   103. The correct answer is C (Initial compensation displaces cerebrospinal fluid and venous blood): The fixed skull allows a small mass to enter while cerebrospinal fluid and venous blood are displaced, but the reserve is limited. The alternative “Brain volume can increase freely because cerebrospinal fluid leaves the skull” ignores the rigid skull and finite capacity.   104. The correct answer is A (Anaphylaxis causing vasodilation and bronchospasm after drug exposure): Hypotension, difficult ventilation, urticaria, and rising airway pressure soon after possible antibiotic or antiseptic exposure fit anaphylaxis. The treatment priority is to stop the trigger, call for help, support the airway and circulation, and give adrenaline according to the emergency protocol. The alternatives “Vasovagal response causing pallor, bradycardia, and temporary hypotension” and “High spinal block causing bradycardia, hypotension, and inadequate muscle tone” usually cause bradycardia and do not explain urticaria and bronchospasm. The alternative “Malignant hyperthermia causing rigidity and progressive carbon dioxide retention” causes rigidity, while the alternative “Pulmonary embolism causing sudden chest pain and reduced carbon dioxide return” does not explain the rash and acute allergic pattern.
  105. The correct answer is B (Give it within protocol alongside blood products and source control): Tranexamic acid reduces clot breakdown and should be integrated into the major haemorrhage pathway at the recommended time. It supports resuscitation but cannot replace mechanical control of the bleeding vessel or pelvic source. Waiting for all laboratory results or an arbitrary number of blood units can miss the useful treatment window. Planned surgery is often part of source control rather than a reason to withhold the drug.
  106. The correct answer is C (Check the approved dose, aspirate, inject incrementally, and monitor closely): Prevention requires an appropriate weight-based dose for the drug, route, patient, and local protocol. Aspiration, incremental injection, and ongoing observation reduce the chance of major intravascular exposure and allow early rescue. The alternatives “Choose the largest remembered dose and inject all of it rapidly” and “Skip product information because adrenaline makes all doses universally safe” use unverified limits or mistaken assumptions about adrenaline. The alternatives “Avoid aspiration because it delays treatment and rarely changes risk” and “Observe only after symptoms develop rather than monitoring during injection” remove practices that protect against toxicity.
  107. The correct answer is E (Use multimodal analgesia and positive pressure support with close monitoring): A flail chest is managed mainly by relieving pain, supporting oxygenation and ventilation, and treating associated injuries. Positive pressure support can stabilise the chest wall indirectly and reduce the work of breathing while the patient recovers. Tight strapping restricts ventilation, and withholding analgesia reduces deep breathing and secretion clearance. Immediate thoracotomy is not routinely required for the flail segment itself.
  108. The correct answer is B (Pass the tube just above the upper border of the rib below.): This route avoids the neurovascular bundle lying in the subcostal groove. Entering below the upper rib risks laceration of the intercostal vein, artery, or nerve. A midspace approach is less controlled because the bundle can be hard to identify. Cartilage division unnecessarily increases tissue injury without protecting the bundle.
  109. The correct answer is D (Add a dated, signed correction that preserves the original and states what was omitted and why): A correction must preserve the original entry and add a dated, signed statement explaining the omission and the corrected facts. This maintains continuity and lets another clinician see how the record was amended. The alternatives “Delete the entry and replace it with a clean note containing no mention of the error” and “Edit the original note without comment because the omission occurred only two hours earlier” remove that audit trail, while the alternative “Ask the consultant to sign a new note that replaces the junior clinician's earlier documentation” creates an unsupported replacement record. The alternative “Leave the record unchanged because the theatre record already documents the unexpected events” relies on another document when the operation note itself remains inaccurate and incomplete.
  110. The correct answer is C (Increased physiological dead space raises minute ventilation and lowers carbon dioxide): Pulmonary embolism obstructs perfusion to ventilated alveoli and increases dead space. Chemoreceptor stimulation raises total ventilation, but much of each breath reaches unperfused lung, so carbon dioxide falls. The alternative “Increased alveolar ventilation from pain has caused a primary metabolic alkalosis” mislabels the respiratory effect as metabolic, while the alternative “Hypercapnia from dead space has stimulated ventilation and lowered carbon dioxide” calls the carbon dioxide change hypercapnia. The alternatives “A large shunt has reduced carbon dioxide transfer from the pulmonary circulation” and “Low cardiac output has decreased carbon dioxide delivery to the lungs” highlight shunt or low delivery but do not match the dead space physiology of embolism.
  111. Cystoscopy visualises the bladder lining, and imaging assesses the kidneys and ureters. Reassurance, yearly dipsticks or a short repeat interval could miss a urinary cancer. Empirical antibiotics are inappropriate without infection, especially when the urine samples were clean.
  112. The correct answer is E (Neurogenic shock from loss of sympathetic tone and unopposed vagal activity): A high cervical injury removes sympathetic vascular tone and leaves vagal bradycardia unopposed. The alternative “Obstructive shock from tension pneumothorax and high venous pressure” conflicts with the clear chest and normal filling on ultrasound. The alternative “Hypovolaemic shock from concealed bleeding and narrow pulse pressure” lacks a bleeding source and would usually produce tachycardia. The alternatives “Cardiogenic shock from myocardial contusion and pulmonary oedema” and “Septic shock from aspiration and fever with warm extremities” do not explain the warm dry limbs or simple cervical injury pattern.
  113. Standard resuscitation continues because recovery can be prolonged and rhythm disturbances may recur. The alternatives “Delay all airway support until the seizure has fully resolved” and “Withhold treatment because recovery from local anaesthetic toxicity is immediate” delay essential care.   114.   115. The correct answer is D (Midgut visceral pain begins near the umbilicus and becomes lateral when parietal peritoneum is involved.): The appendix is a midgut structure, so early visceral pain follows its referral to the umbilical region. When inflammation reaches the parietal peritoneum, the pain becomes sharper and localizes to the right iliac fossa. Foregut pain is referred to the epigastrium, and hindgut pain is referred lower. Reversing visceral and somatic pain also fails to explain the migration.
  116. The correct answer is D (The lethal triad of hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy): Major bleeding, transfusion, cold exposure, and poor tissue perfusion can combine to produce these three linked problems. Each worsens the others by impairing enzyme activity, clot formation, and oxygen delivery. The alternative “Early septic shock with fever and normal clotting function” requires infection and fever, while the alternative “Neurogenic shock causing bradycardia and unusually warm extremities” features bradycardia and warm skin without this clotting failure. The alternatives “Cardiogenic shock with raised filling pressures and pulmonary oedema” and “Anaphylactic shock causing urticaria, wheeze, and airway swelling” do not match the temperature, pH, and coagulation findings.
  117. The correct answer is B (Activate the major haemorrhage protocol and arrange urgent bleeding control): Continuing shock after initial transfusion requires early balanced blood component replacement and rapid control of the bleeding source. The major haemorrhage protocol provides coordinated products, monitoring and senior support while definitive treatment is organised. Further crystalloid dilutes clotting factors and worsens cooling, and red cells alone do not replace plasma or platelets. Waiting for tests or imaging delays treatment of an actively bleeding patient.
  118. The correct answer is D (Review the steroid dose and arrange stress dose guidance with specialists): Long term steroids can suppress the normal stress response, and major surgery may require additional coverage under a clear plan. The dose should be reviewed with the relevant specialist and local guidance. Stopping abruptly can precipitate adrenal crisis, while continuing an unchanged dose may not cover major stress. A single blanket dose or fludrocortisone alone does not provide a complete individual plan.
  119. The correct answer is B (T10, carrying the oesophagus and both vagal trunks.): The oesophageal opening usually lies near T10 and transmits the oesophagus with its vagal trunks.   120. The correct answer is C (Ductal carcinoma in situ without invasion through basement membrane): Clustered pleomorphic calcifications often accompany ductal carcinoma in situ, which consists of neoplastic cells confined within ducts. An intact basement membrane distinguishes it from invasive carcinoma. Invasive ductal and lobular cancers breach the duct or lobule boundary, while atypical ductal hyperplasia has less severe architectural and cytological change. Fibroadenoma is a benign mixed stromal and epithelial lesion.
  121. The correct answer is B (Cerebral perfusion pressure of 70 mmHg is mean arterial pressure minus intracranial pressure): Subtracting 20 from 90 gives the pressure difference that drives cerebral blood flow.   130.   165. The correct answer is C (Coexisting magnesium depletion is preventing effective potassium correction): Magnesium deficiency opens potassium channels and increases renal potassium loss, so potassium remains low until magnesium is replaced. The combination of a high output fistula, ventricular ectopy and documented low magnesium fits that mechanism. The alternative “Potassium has shifted rapidly into cells because of metabolic acidosis” has the shift direction wrong for acidosis and would not explain the low magnesium. The alternatives “Unmeasured stool losses are clearly the only cause of ongoing deficit”, “Aldosterone deficiency is increasing renal potassium loss despite replacement”, and “The arrhythmia reflects calcium deficiency rather than potassium imbalance” overlook the measured magnesium deficit or propose hormone and electrolyte explanations that are inconsistent with the results.
  166. The correct answer is C (Continue aspirin when bleeding risk is acceptable and discuss changes): Low dose aspirin is often continued for secondary prevention when the operation carries little bleeding risk. The decision should consider the stroke history, planned procedure, and ability to control bleeding. Routine stopping can increase thrombotic risk, while vitamin K does not reverse aspirin. A direct oral anticoagulant is more potent and is not an appropriate replacement.
  167. The correct answer is A (Lactate can rise from reduced hepatic clearance despite adequate tissue perfusion): The liver clears lactate, so chronic liver disease can produce a higher value even when perfusion appears satisfactory. The alternative “Lactate is specific for anaerobic metabolism and may indicate septic shock” overstates specificity and universal sepsis causation. The alternatives “A falling lactate may reflect worsening liver failure rather than recovery” and “Lactate should be ignored because it cannot guide resuscitation” misread a falling trend and discard a useful warning signal.

  170. The correct answer is C (Permissive hypotension with a lower pressure while organ perfusion remains adequate): Before bleeding is controlled, a moderately lower pressure can avoid diluting clotting factors and displacing clot while the brain remains protected. The alternative “Rapid crystalloid infusion to a normal pressure before bleeding control” may worsen bleeding through pressure and dilution, while the alternative “Deliberate severe hypotension while mental status and urine output remain unassessed” is dangerously extreme.   171.   176. The correct answer is E (Positive predictive value falls when disease prevalence is low): Positive predictive value is the chance that a positive result is true, and it depends on how common the disease is in the tested group. A rare disease produces more false positives even when sensitivity and specificity are fixed.
