Isotonic saline does not raise sodium concentration, and aldosterone deficiency or free water retention produces a different water balance.
  234. The correct answer is D (Clear cell renal cell carcinoma arising within the kidney cortex): Clear cell renal cell carcinoma is the commonest kidney cancer and often produces haematuria, flank pain or a mass. Many tumours are found on imaging, but this presentation shows several recognised effects of an advanced renal cortical lesion. Transitional cell carcinoma usually arises in the collecting system rather than the cortex, and oncocytoma is less likely with this clinical picture. Wilms tumour mostly affects young children, while a metastasis would not usually be the first renal diagnosis.
  235. The correct answer is A (Specificity, calculated as true negatives among all people without disease): Specificity is the proportion of disease-free people who correctly test negative. It describes test performance in people without the condition. Sensitivity uses people with disease rather than disease-free people. Predictive values estimate post-test probability among test-positive or test-negative groups, and prevalence describes disease frequency in the population.
  236. The correct answer is D (Early tranexamic acid given according to the major haemorrhage protocol): Tranexamic acid reduces clot breakdown and is most useful when given early after major traumatic bleeding. It supports the haemorrhage protocol rather than replacing blood products or urgent source control. Plasma alone omits the requested antifibrinolytic, and routine vitamin K lacks a bleeding-disorder indication. Protamine does not reverse an unidentified anticoagulant, and delayed tranexamic acid misses the period of active clot breakdown.
  237. The correct answer is E (High gap lactic acidosis with an additional respiratory acidotic component): The anion gap is twenty five, and the elevated lactate identifies lactic acidosis as the main metabolic process. A depleted bicarbonate with raised lactate does not fit a normal-gap process from diarrhoea. The carbon dioxide of thirty is higher than expected for a bicarbonate of twelve, so ventilation contributes an acidotic component. Neither alkalosis nor chronic respiratory acidosis fits the direction of the measured changes.
  238. The correct answer is B (The cricothyroid membrane, which lies between the thyroid and cricoid cartilages.): The thyroid cartilage forms the laryngeal prominence, and the cricoid is the next complete ring below it, so the membrane between them is the emergency access point. The thyrohyoid membrane lies above the thyroid cartilage. The cricotracheal ligament lies below the cricoid. The quadrangular membrane and thyroglossal duct are not front-of-neck access membranes.
  239. The correct answer is C (Stop unnecessary antibiotics, isolate, replace fluids, and follow severity guidance): C difficile disease management begins by stopping precipitating drugs when safe, preventing spread and restoring fluids. A treatment agent is selected according to severity and local policy rather than colonisation alone. Loperamide or another broad antibiotic can retain toxins or continue the trigger. Delayed testing or vancomycin for a colonised patient misses the clinical target.
  240. The correct answer is E (Check the drain system and escalate a persistent large air leak): Increased bubbling with breathlessness may reflect a large air leak or a system problem, so the tubing and connections need prompt checking. A kink can obstruct drainage, and persistent leakage requires senior cardiothoracic review. Clamping without specialist guidance can cause a tension problem, while removing the drain may worsen pneumothorax. Forceful milking and delayed oxygen are unsafe.
  241. The correct answer is B (Use analgesia, antibiotics, and prompt surgical care by local policy): Appendicitis can be harder to localise in children, but guarding, pain on movement, fever and a confirmed inflamed appendix need prompt treatment. Analgesia can be provided safely, and local pathways guide antibiotics and operation. Discharge because appendicitis is uncommon misjudges the confirmed diagnosis and peritonism. Waiting for rupture, using oral antibiotics alone or prolonged observation would undertreat established disease.
  242. The correct answer is E (Urgent angiographic embolisation with monitored nonoperative care and repeat review): A contrast collection within a splenic injury can represent a pseudoaneurysm or active vascular lesion with a risk of delayed rupture. Angiography with embolisation can control that lesion while preserving splenic tissue, followed by monitored nonoperative care. Immediate splenectomy may be needed if the patient becomes unstable, but it is not automatic for every contained lesion. Discharge or limited ultrasound follow-up may miss a dangerous delayed bleed.
  243. The correct answer is E (Knee osteoarthritis causing mechanical pain, crepitus and bony swelling): Use-related pain, brief morning stiffness, bony swelling and joint space narrowing form a typical osteoarthritis picture. Treatment begins with exercise, weight management and pain control, with replacement considered for severe disability. Rheumatoid arthritis usually causes longer morning stiffness and inflammatory synovitis, while septic arthritis and gout cause acute warmth and systemic or crystal-related features. A cruciate rupture follows injury and causes instability rather than years of mechanical pain.
  244. The correct answer is D (Carbon dioxide embolism passing through a vascular opening into circulation): The abrupt fall in end-tidal carbon dioxide with hypotension and high airway pressure after trocar insertion typifies gas entering the venous system. Anaphylaxis causes rash, bronchospasm or swelling without the characteristic carbon dioxide fall. Malignant hyperthermia raises end-tidal carbon dioxide and produces rigidity. Tension pneumothorax causes unilateral findings and tracheal shift, while vagal traction causes bradycardia without high airway pressure.
  245. The correct answer is E (The left brachiocephalic vein crossing transversely behind the thymus.): This large vein lies immediately behind the thymus and in front of the major arterial branches. Traction near the upper pole can tear it during thymectomy. The superior vena cava is lower and mainly right-sided, while the azygos vein lies posteriorly. The internal thoracic and inferior thyroid veins do not occupy the described central retrothymic plane.
  246. The correct answer is E (Oxygen, multimodal analgesia, physiotherapy, and breathing support as needed): Flail chest impairs ventilation through pain, underlying lung injury and abnormal chest wall movement. Initial care focuses on oxygen, effective analgesia, lung expansion and escalation to ventilatory support when required. Thoracotomy without a cardiac indication treats a chest wall finding as a cardiac emergency. Strapping restricts breathing, and an unnecessary drain or unmonitored observation omits essential respiratory support.
  247. The correct answer is B (Optically clear nuclei with nuclear grooves and cytoplasmic pseudoinclusions): These nuclear changes are characteristic of papillary thyroid carcinoma and help separate it from benign follicular lesions. Papillary architecture alone can be seen in other thyroid conditions. Parafollicular cells with amyloid indicate medullary carcinoma, while Hurthle cells are a separate cytological finding. Lymphoid follicles with fibrosis suggest thyroiditis rather than papillary carcinoma.
  248. The correct answer is C (A Spigelian hernia through the aponeurosis near the linea semilunaris.): A Spigelian hernia occurs near the lateral edge of the rectus sheath, usually below the umbilicus. The intact external oblique can hide the swelling and make examination difficult. Inguinal, femoral, and obturator hernias occur in different locations and have different relations to the epigastric vessels and pubic landmarks. The imaging location strongly supports the Spigelian route.
  249. The correct answer is E (Acute epididymitis associated with urinary or sexually transmitted infection): Gradual scrotal pain with urinary symptoms and relief on elevation is typical of epididymitis. Infection can follow urinary pathogens or sexually transmitted organisms, and treatment should reflect the likely source. Torsion usually begins abruptly and may leave the testis high with an absent cremasteric reflex. Cancer is usually a painless mass, Fournier gangrene causes rapid necrosis and severe systemic illness, and appendix testis torsion commonly has a localised tender nodule.
  250. The correct answer is C (Acute extradural haematoma causing a lucid interval and focal signs): A temporal impact can tear the middle meningeal artery and produce an extradural collection shaped like a biconvex lens. A brief lucid interval followed by falling consciousness, pupil dilation and opposite-sided weakness is the classic pattern. Rising blood pressure with a slowing pulse suggests raised intracranial pressure and is not reassuring. Chronic subdural bleeding is crescentic, while diffuse axonal injury causes immediate severe impairment without a focal collection.
  251. The correct answer is C (The middle cerebral artery, which supplies lateral cortex and lenticulostriate perforators.): This territory includes the lateral motor and sensory cortex and deep pathways affected by perforator branches. The combination of face and arm weakness with expressive difficulty fits a left hemisphere middle cerebral lesion. Anterior cerebral lesions favor leg weakness, and posterior cerebral lesions favor vision. The cerebellar arteries produce brainstem or cerebellar signs rather than this cortical pattern.
  252. The correct answer is B (The dorsalis pedis artery, which runs lateral to extensor hallucis longus.): This surface landmark identifies the dorsalis pedis pulse, with the deep peroneal nerve beside the artery. The posterior tibial artery is palpated behind the medial malleolus. The anterior tibial artery lies in the anterior leg and does not pass behind the lateral malleolus. The peroneal and superficial circumflex iliac arteries are not located at this foot landmark.
  253. The correct answer is B (Potassium leaks from stored red cells during rapid transfusion in kidney injury): Potassium moves from red cells into the preservative solution during storage, and rapid transfusion can deliver a substantial potassium load. Reduced renal potassium clearance makes hyperkalaemia more likely. The stated calcium and potassium shift is reversed, and citrate metabolism does not release potassium from skeletal muscle. Hypothermia or delayed haemolysis does not explain the immediate transfusion-related potassium load.
  254. The correct answer is E (Use multimodal analgesia and monitor sedation, breathing, and device use): Combining analgesics with different actions can reduce opioid requirements, and sedation can precede obvious deterioration. Monitoring sedation, respiratory effort, and pump use is essential when an opioid is combined with a benzodiazepine. Options A and B create avoidable overdose risk. Options C and D rely on incomplete or unsafe measures.
  255. The correct answer is B (Reduce the dislocation urgently and reassess pulses, sensation and movement): A deformed ankle with absent pulses and reduced sensation indicates threatened blood flow and nerve function. Urgent reduction relieves pressure and may restore perfusion, after which pulses, sensation and movement must be checked again. Imaging can follow once the limb is safe, and surgery may still be needed for instability or fracture. A cast over a malaligned joint, delayed imaging and morning review all risk permanent ischaemic injury.
  256. The correct answer is D (The recurrent median branch, which supplies the thenar muscles near the carpal tunnel.): Injury can weaken thumb opposition while leaving thumb sensation and long flexor function intact. The deep ulnar branch supplies intrinsic muscles but not most thenar muscles. The anterior interosseous nerve affects deep flexors and pronation. Superficial radial and palmar cutaneous branches are sensory and do not explain the motor deficit.
  257. The correct answer is B (Oxygen consumption equals cardiac output multiplied by the arteriovenous oxygen difference): The Fick principle compares arterial and mixed venous oxygen contents and multiplies that difference by cardiac output. This gives whole-body oxygen uptake per minute under steady conditions. Dividing by the content difference or subtracting the two values changes the physical relationship. Saturation alone omits both blood flow and oxygen carrying capacity.
  258. The correct answer is D (Paget disease of nipple associated with ductal carcinoma in situ): Paget disease spreads malignant glandular cells through the nipple epidermis and can mimic eczema. An underlying ductal carcinoma in situ is common, and the biopsy findings link the two components. Simple eczema lacks atypical malignant cells, and melanoma has melanocytic rather than glandular spread. Basal and squamous cell carcinomas produce different epithelial tumour patterns.
  259. The correct answer is A (The radial nerve, which spirals around the humerus between the triceps heads.): Midshaft fracture can injure the radial nerve and cause wrist drop with reduced dorsal hand sensation. The median nerve injury does not typically produce wrist drop. The ulnar nerve lies near the elbow and affects intrinsic hand muscles. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies elbow flexors, and the axillary nerve affects deltoid near the shoulder.
  260. The correct answer is E (The transverse pericardial sinus, which allows great vessel control.): It lies behind the ascending aorta and pulmonary trunk and in front of the superior vena cava. The oblique sinus lies behind the left atrium and has a different surgical use. The coronary sinus is a venous channel, not this transverse passage. The fibrous pericardium is the outer sac and does not provide the described plane.
  261. The correct answer is C (The long thoracic nerve, which supplies serratus anterior and stabilizes the scapula.): Its injury weakens serratus anterior and produces winging of the medial scapular border. The axillary nerve affects deltoid and shoulder abduction without scapular winging. The intercostobrachial nerve causes medial upper arm numbness. The thoracodorsal nerve weakens latissimus dorsi, and spinal accessory nerve injury affects trapezius rather than the isolated pattern described.
  262. The correct answer is C (The phrenic nerve lies anterior to the root and the vagus nerve lies posterior.): The phrenic nerve descends over the pericardium toward the diaphragm, while the vagus passes behind the lung root. This relationship helps the surgeon avoid dividing the phrenic nerve during hilar dissection. Reversing the nerves or placing both on one side misrepresents the operative anatomy. Both nerves are found on each side, although their recurrent branches follow different arterial loops.
  263. The correct answer is C (Tumour handling can release vasoactive mediators causing hypotension): Mechanical stimulation can release serotonin and kinins that produce flushing, bronchospasm and sudden vasodilation. These mediators cause profound hypotension during tumour handling. Serotonin excess does not usually cause a dominant severe hypertensive response in this setting. Octreotide does not directly block adrenal cortisol, beta blockade cannot prevent every mediator response, and most carcinoid tumours arise in the small bowel.
  264. The correct answer is A (Multiple myeloma with clonal plasma cells and osteolytic bone lesions): Clonal marrow plasma cells with lytic lesions, anaemia, hypercalcaemia and renal injury satisfy myeloma related organ damage. Malignant plasma cells secrete factors that activate osteoclasts and suppress normal marrow function. Monoclonal gammopathy lacks these organ effects, and reactive plasmacytosis is polyclonal. Waldenstrom macroglobulinaemia and primary amyloidosis have different dominant proteins and clinical patterns.
  265. The correct answer is D (The spleen lying beneath the left hemidiaphragm near low left ports.): A port placed too far inferiorly on the left can cross the diaphragm into the spleen. The liver and right kidney lie on the opposite side from this port. The stomach is more central and the pancreas is retroperitoneal, so neither has the immediate low lateral relation described. Early recognition of diaphragmatic injury and abdominal bleeding is essential.
  266. The correct answer is D (Basal cell carcinoma with peripheral palisading and stromal retraction): A pearly rolled edge with telangiectasia on sun-damaged skin is typical of basal cell carcinoma. Peripheral palisading and retraction spaces between tumour nests and stroma support the diagnosis. Squamous carcinoma usually shows keratin pearls, while actinic keratosis remains confined to the epidermis. Melanoma and sebaceous carcinoma have different cell populations and architectural patterns.
  267. The correct answer is E (The superior mesenteric vein and splenic vein join behind the neck to form the portal vein.): The pancreatic neck overlies the portal venous confluence and the superior mesenteric vessels. The inferior vena cava and right renal vein are more posterior and right-sided. The aorta and left renal artery are arterial structures rather than the main venous confluence. The splenic vein does not drain into the inferior vena cava, and the portal vein lies behind rather than in front of the neck.
  268. The correct answer is D (Fluid excess is contributing to congestion and ileus despite oliguria): Five litres of crystalloid, raised venous pressure and basal crackles point to venous congestion and extravascular fluid accumulation. The distended abdomen with absent bowel sounds fits a fluid-related ileus. Further blind boluses would risk worsening lung and gut oedema. Pneumonia or mechanical obstruction does not explain the combined jugular, lung and gut findings, and urine output alone does not prove hypovolaemia.
  269. The correct answer is B (Attempt gentle urethral catheterisation and monitor the drained urine): Painful acute urinary retention with a distended bladder needs prompt decompression, and gentle urethral catheterisation is the preferred route when passage appears safe. The drained volume, urine output and electrolytes should then be monitored, especially after prolonged obstruction. Suprapubic diversion is reserved for failed or unsafe urethral passage. Alpha blockade or delayed observation does not provide urgent drainage, and transurethral resection is not an emergency first step.
  270. The correct answer is A (Normal saturation can coexist with low oxygen delivery from reduced haemoglobin): Most arterial oxygen is carried by haemoglobin, so severe anaemia lowers content and delivery despite almost full saturation. Cardiac output increased as compensation, but oxygen delivery still became inadequate enough to cause confusion. Dissolved oxygen is a small fraction of total content, and normal saturation does not prove adequate tissue oxygen use. Supplemental oxygen alone does not restore lost haemoglobin, and low cardiac output does not describe this circulation.
  271. The correct answer is C (Cohesive atypical epithelial cells within lymphatic sinus and stroma): Metastatic carcinoma forms cohesive nests of atypical epithelial cells that can enter nodal sinuses and invade stroma. This differs from the mixed lymphoid populations of reactive nodes. Follicular hyperplasia and sinus histiocytosis preserve an orderly reactive architecture. Granulomas indicate inflammation rather than carcinoma, and paracortical hyperplasia is a lymphoid response.
  272. The correct answer is E (Barlow manoeuvre, which attempts to dislocate a located hip): The Barlow test applies posterior pressure while the flexed hip is adducted to detect instability. The Ortolani test starts from a dislocated position and brings the femoral head forward into the socket. Thomas and Trendelenburg tests assess hip flexion deformity and standing abductor function rather than neonatal instability. The Galeazzi test compares knee heights and does not apply posterior pressure to the femoral head.
  273. The correct answer is E (Low ionised calcium contributing to impaired clotting and circulation): Stored blood contains citrate, which binds calcium and can lower the ionised fraction during large transfusions. Calcium is needed for effective clotting and vascular tone, so worsening hypotension and weaker clotting can follow. Hypercalcaemia reverses the expected change, and hypernatraemia does not fit massive transfusion physiology. Polycythaemia and hyperkalaemia do not explain deterioration despite component replacement.
  274. The correct answer is E (Obtain emergency specialist removal because oesophageal injury is likely): A button battery in the oesophagus can cause rapid electrical burns, perforation and major bleeding. Removal is urgent even when the child initially seems stable. Observation at home ignores the high risk of impaction, and blind finger sweeps can injure the airway or throat. Food or delayed endoscopy prolongs electrical injury and may worsen tissue damage.
  275. The correct answer is D (Compensatory cardiac output can maintain oxygen delivery until reserves are exhausted): Anaemia lowers arterial oxygen content, but a rise in cardiac output can preserve delivery while the patient remains otherwise stable. Reassuring mental state, urine output, perfusion and lactate show that compensation is currently effective. Haemoglobin concentration does not directly measure blood volume. Normal saturation does not prove adequate delivery, and cardiac output does not fall during this compensated response.
  276. The correct answer is E (The aortic opening near T12, carrying the aorta, thoracic duct, and azygos vein.): This opening lies posteriorly between the crura and contains the named major vessels. A caval injury would occur nearer T8, while oesophageal structures pass near T10. The central tendon and sternocostal triangle do not represent this posterior crural track. The relationship explains the combined vascular and lymphatic risk from this penetrating injury.
  277. The correct answer is A (Invasive lobular carcinoma with loss of E-cadherin expression): Loss of E-cadherin allows cells to separate and invade as single files through breast stroma. This pattern is characteristic of invasive lobular carcinoma. Ductal carcinoma usually forms tubules or solid nests with maintained adhesion, and lobular carcinoma in situ remains confined rather than infiltrating stroma. Medullary and mucinous carcinomas have different architectural appearances.
  278. The correct answer is B (Discuss timing with cardiology before changing dual antiplatelet treatment): Stopping clopidogrel soon after coronary stenting can cause stent thrombosis, so the surgical and cardiac risks need shared review. The operation can often be timed safely, but that decision requires cardiology input. Continuing both drugs without considering bleeding risk is also incomplete. Warfarin is not a substitute, and an arbitrary ten year delay is not an appropriate plan.
  279. The correct answer is B (Plan margin control and reconstruction together after completing staging): Limb-sparing sarcoma surgery requires a clear oncological plan plus reconstruction of the resulting defect. Staging and multidisciplinary planning define the margin and the available reconstruction options. Immediate amputation before staging is premature, and fine needle aspiration cannot reliably replace core biopsy for grade or architecture. Simple enucleation risks inadequate clearance, while delayed staging risks operating without essential information.
  280. The correct answer is C (Unusual taste, tinnitus, perioral numbness, and increasing agitation): Local anaesthetic systemic toxicity commonly begins with neurologic warning symptoms before seizures or cardiovascular collapse. These features develop soon after injection and call for immediate supportive treatment and local protocol escalation. Malignant hyperthermia produces hypercapnia and rigidity, while anaphylaxis produces airway swelling and hypotension. Delayed weakness or urticaria does not match this early toxic sequence.
  281. The correct answer is D (Stop unnecessary intravenous fluids once oral intake is adequate): Fixed intravenous fluid without a current need can cause overload and slow recovery, so it should stop when drinking is safe and adequate. The patient should be reassessed for losses and symptoms instead of continuing a bag by habit. A diuretic does not correct the reason for unnecessary fluid, and prolonged fasting or extra fluid delays normal recovery. Early oral intake and mobility are central enhanced recovery principles.
  282. The correct answer is A (Acute ureteric colic caused by a stone obstructing the ureter): Severe colicky loin to groin pain with restlessness and haematuria is the classic pattern of ureteric colic. The absence of fever and systemic illness makes infection unlikely, while the pain is too episodic for a painless tumour. Pyelonephritis usually causes fever and loin tenderness, and rupture of an aortic aneurysm commonly causes shock or a pulsatile mass. Appendicitis normally produces localized right lower abdominal pain rather than isolated loin to groin colic.
  283. The correct answer is A (It protects the presacral venous plexus and autonomic pelvic nerves.): The correct plane keeps the mesorectal envelope intact while sparing the presacral veins and pelvic autonomic nerves. The levator ani and external sphincter are not removed by the standard mesorectal plane. Division of the pudendal nerve would harm continence and perineal sensation. Leaving mesorectal fat behind and opening the ischioanal fossa are not goals of total mesorectal excision.
  284. The correct answer is D (Acute bacterial arthritis with a markedly neutrophil-rich inflammatory joint fluid): Fever, an acute monoarthritis and a very high neutrophil-rich synovial count suggest septic arthritis. Gram-positive cocci provide direct evidence of bacterial infection. Gout and pseudogout cause crystal inflammation without bacteria, and reactive arthritis occurs after infection without organisms in the joint. Rheumatoid arthritis is usually chronic and polyarticular rather than an acute febrile monoarthritis with positive Gram stain.
  285. The correct answer is C (The brachial artery is at risk because it lies close to the distal humerus.): A displaced supracondylar fracture can injure or compress the brachial artery and cause acute forearm ischaemia. The radial artery is more distal and is not the vessel directly related to this fracture. The axillary artery is far proximal. The ulnar and common interosseous arteries are not the main vessels at the supracondylar fracture site.
  286. The correct answer is A (Testicular germ cell tumour presenting as a painless solid testicular mass): A solid intratesticular mass in a young man is cancer until proven otherwise, and a painless hard lump is a common presentation. Ultrasound and specialist assessment guide tumour marker testing and treatment. Epididymitis causes inflammatory pain and urinary features, while torsion produces abrupt severe pain. A hydrocele transilluminates and surrounds the testis, and a varicocele lies above the testis with a soft engorged feel.
  287. The correct answer is D (The white line of Toldt beside the ascending colon and retroperitoneum.): Opening this peritoneal reflection mobilises the colon while keeping the ureter and gonadal vessels in the retroperitoneal plane. Dissection anterior to these structures avoids urinary and vascular injury. The epiploic foramen and lesser omentum belong to the upper foregut region. The transverse mesocolon and pelvic brim do not provide this lateral colonic plane.
  288. The correct answer is E (The perineal body, which joins pelvic floor and anal sphincter structures.): It is the central support between the vaginal opening and anal canal, and its injury can reduce pelvic floor support. The perineal membrane is a deeper sheet in the urogenital triangle. Ischioanal fat provides padding but is not the central supporting junction. The sacrospinous ligament and puborectalis muscle are important, yet neither matches the described central perineal gap.
  289. The correct answer is B (Peripancreatic fat saponification binds calcium and lowers ionised levels): Severe pancreatitis releases pancreatic enzymes that digest peripancreatic fat, and the resulting fatty acids bind calcium as soap complexes. This lowers ionised calcium and produces neuromuscular irritability and QT prolongation. Renal phosphate retention is a slower mechanism, and respiratory alkalosis requires an alkalotic trigger that is not present. Rhabdomyolysis is absent, and a normal albumin concentration does not explain a low ionised calcium.
  290. The correct answer is C (Remove the catheter and monitor voiding with a bladder scan): An unnecessary catheter increases infection and discomfort, so it should be removed once the patient is mobile and no longer needs accurate output measurement. Monitoring voiding allows early detection of retention without keeping the device in place. Routine replacement or prolonged use adds risk, and prophylactic antibiotics do not make a needless catheter safe. Fluid restriction does not address the device risk.
  291. The correct answer is D (Sympathetic fibres in the cervical chain are interrupted within the neck.): Loss of sympathetic tone produces ptosis, miosis, enophthalmos, and reduced sweating on the same side, which is Horner syndrome. Parasympathetic injury affects pupil constriction and gland secretion in different patterns. Pelvic splanchnic fibres control pelvic organs rather than the eye. Trigeminal injury causes facial sensory loss rather than this autonomic combination.
  292. The correct answer is D (Median nerve, brachial artery, biceps tendon, radial nerve.): This is the usual medial-to-lateral arrangement in the cubital fossa. The radial nerve lies most laterally, while the median nerve lies most medially. Moving the biceps tendon medial to the artery or placing the radial nerve centrally reverses the order. These relations guide safe dissection around the elbow.
  293. The correct answer is E (Increasing Breslow thickness and ulceration raising metastatic risk): Greater tumour depth gives malignant cells more opportunity to reach dermal lymphatics, and ulceration independently worsens prognosis. These features help identify patients who may benefit from sentinel node assessment. Growth pattern alone does not remove the risk created by depth and ulceration. A lower Clark level or absent dermal mitoses would not outweigh a thick ulcerated melanoma.
  294. The correct answer is B (Microcirculatory shunting and impaired cellular oxygen use can raise venous saturation): In sepsis, blood may pass through shunts without exchanging oxygen, and mitochondria may fail to use delivered oxygen, so venous blood remains highly saturated despite tissue distress. Rising lactate and organ dysfunction show that high venous saturation does not equal normal cellular respiration. High saturation does not prove excessive oxygen delivery. Normal oxidative phosphorylation would not explain the metabolic failure, and low cardiac output or anaemia does not account for the reduced extraction.
  295. The correct answer is D (The medial circumflex femoral artery through retinacular branches, which can tear with displacement.): Retinacular branches provide most adult femoral head blood and are vulnerable in a displaced intracapsular fracture. The artery in the ligament of the head is a small contributor in adults. The superior gluteal and lateral circumflex femoral arteries do not provide the main retinacular supply. The deep femoral artery is a parent vessel but not the direct route described.
  296. The correct answer is D (Early operative fixation or arthroplasty with rehabilitation and medical review): A displaced hip fracture in an older patient is best treated with early surgery chosen according to fracture pattern and mobility. Early operation reduces pain and complications from prolonged bed rest, and rehabilitation supports recovery. Medical review should address falls, bone health and perioperative risks. Traction, discharge, bed rest and cast treatment all delay mobilisation and increase pressure injury, clot and infection risks.
  297. The correct answer is E (The anterior border of latissimus dorsi, lateral border of pectoralis major, and line above the nipple.): The classic safe triangle uses these three borders and has its apex below the axilla. The long thoracic nerve lies along the chest wall outside this protected region when the drain is placed correctly. A paramedian or scapular boundary would place the tube near the heart, mediastinum, or posterior chest structures. A more medial or posterior choice would not match the standard surface landmark.
  298. The correct answer is C (Activate major haemorrhage response and return urgently to theatre): Rapid drain loss, abdominal distension, worsening haemoglobin and haemodynamic instability indicate continuing postoperative haemorrhage. Blood products and simultaneous surgical control are needed because transfusion alone cannot repair the bleeding source. Another crystalloid bolus delays recognition while large-volume loss continues. Observation, computed tomography or stopping the drain all delay the source control that the patient needs.
  299. The correct answer is A (The arcuate line marks the lower end of the posterior rectus sheath.): Below this line, the aponeuroses pass in front of rectus abdominis, leaving the posterior surface covered by transversalis fascia and peritoneum. The inferior epigastric vessels run on the posterior surface of rectus and are not a midline content of the linea alba. The inguinal ligament does not bound the rectus sheath posteriorly. The conjoint tendon relates to the lower inguinal region and does not explain the sheath change.
  300. The correct answer is A (The popliteal artery, which lies deep and close to the posterior capsule.): Posterior knee dislocation can tear or compress this artery and threaten the lower limb. The femoral artery is more proximal and protected in the adductor canal. The great saphenous vein is superficial and venous. The anterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries are distal branches rather than the vessel directly at the posterior capsule.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (The radial nerve, which passes within the spiral groove of the humerus.): A midshaft humeral fracture can injure the radial nerve and cause wrist drop and weak finger extension. Its posterior interosseous continuation also supplies many forearm extensors, while the superficial branch supplies dorsal hand sensation. Median and ulnar injuries produce different sensory and intrinsic hand patterns. Musculocutaneous and axillary injuries affect elbow flexion or shoulder abduction.
  2. The correct answer is B (The patient with a tension pneumothorax and falling oxygen saturation): Immediate priority goes to the patient with a rapidly lethal but treatable airway or breathing problem. Tension pneumothorax can cause cardiac arrest and needs urgent decompression before routine injuries are assessed. Stable fractures, minor scalp wounds and controlled hand injuries can wait while ABCDE threats are treated. Wound size alone is not a safe triage measure because hidden airway and circulation problems cause earlier death.
  3. The correct answer is D (The approved stop interval must be met before neuraxial needle placement): Direct oral anticoagulants increase the risk of spinal or epidural haematoma after neuraxial procedures. Timing must follow the specific drug, kidney function, procedure and current local guidance, with thrombosis and bleeding risk balanced. Proceeding without an interval or continuing the anticoagulant ignores a serious bleeding risk. A normal clotting screen does not exclude spinal bleeding, and reviewing timing after the block reverses the required safety sequence.
  4. The correct answer is C (The popliteal artery because it lies close to the posterior knee capsule.): Posterior knee dislocation can tear or occlude this artery and threaten the whole lower leg. The common peroneal nerve may also be injured, but it does not explain absent pulses and a cool foot. The anterior tibial artery is distal to the popliteal segment, while the saphenous vein is superficial. Femoral artery injury occurs more proximally in the thigh.
  5. The correct answer is C (The middle cerebral artery, which supplies lateral cortex and lenticulostriate perforators.): This territory includes the lateral motor, sensory, and language areas affected in this presentation. The face and arm predominate, and a dominant left hemisphere lesion can impair expression. Anterior cerebral lesions favor leg weakness, while posterior cerebral lesions mainly affect vision. Cerebellar artery lesions produce ataxia and brainstem signs rather than this cortical pattern.
  6. The correct answer is B (Squamous cell carcinoma causing an indurated ulcer with regional nodes): A persistent lower lip ulcer with a raised indurated edge in a sun-exposed smoker raises concern for squamous cell carcinoma. Regional lymph node involvement is possible, so prompt biopsy and head and neck assessment are needed. Basal cell carcinoma is more often pearly and rarely spreads to nodes, while melanoma is usually pigmented. Aphthous and herpes lesions have different appearances and usually follow a shorter recurrent course.
  7. The correct answer is A (Hypothermia impairs coagulation and reduces drug metabolism during surgery): Cooling slows enzymatic clotting reactions, alters platelet function and reduces clearance of anaesthetic drugs. This explains increased bleeding and unexpected drowsiness at low doses. Increased clotting factor activity and accelerated anaesthetic clearance reverse both effects. Fever, hyperthyroidism or malignant hyperthermia does not fit the measured low core temperature and intraoperative pattern.

Neurogenic, allergic, and cardiac causes do not account for this electrolyte pattern as closely.

The timing after feeding and the sharp fall in phosphate are the key diagnostic clues.
  79. The correct answer is A (Tumour mediator release causes vasodilation and profound intraoperative hypotension): Handling a carcinoid tumour can release serotonin and other mediators that produce flushing, bronchospasm and sudden vasodilatory hypotension. The clinical setting and absence of sepsis or bleeding support this response. A catecholamine surge produces hypertension with pallor rather than this vasodilatory pattern. Histamine from a transfusion reaction requires an exposure and fever, pulmonary embolism does not explain flushing, and adrenal insufficiency lacks its electrolyte and glucose pattern.
  80. The correct answer is C (Diabetic ketoacidosis with an increased anion gap metabolic acidosis): Insulin deficiency drives lipolysis and ketone production, producing a high anion gap acidosis with deep breathing and dehydration. High ketones and low bicarbonate are the decisive findings. A hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state has minimal ketones and a smaller anion gap. Lactic acidosis with absent ketones and normal bicarbonate does not fit, while diarrhoea or a primary respiratory alkalosis does not explain the ketotic high-gap acidosis.
  81. The correct answer is C (Spectrum bias because the studied population was unusually severe): Test accuracy can differ when the disease spectrum includes more severe or obvious cases. Results from emergency patients with major disease may not transfer to mild outpatient presentations. Recall bias concerns memory, and allocation bias concerns how treatment groups are formed. Publication bias concerns missing negative studies, and attrition bias concerns participants leaving follow-up rather than the selected severity spectrum.
  82. The correct answer is A (The recurrent laryngeal nerve, which supplies most intrinsic laryngeal muscles.): This nerve runs close to the thyroid gland and inferior thyroid artery, so ligation can injure it. Vocal cord weakness causes hoarseness and can affect airway protection. The external superior laryngeal branch supplies only cricothyroid and mainly changes pitch, while the internal branch is sensory above the cords. Glossopharyngeal and phrenic injuries do not control most laryngeal muscles.
  83. The correct answer is A (In vitro haemolysis has falsely raised potassium and lactate dehydrogenase): Pink plasma and a normal repeat sample support haemolysis during collection or handling as the cause of false hyperkalaemia and raised lactate dehydrogenase. A normal electrocardiogram further argues against true severe hyperkalaemia. Adrenal insufficiency would not be corrected by simply repeating the same stable clinical state. Prehepatic jaundice affects bilirubin rather than potassium, coagulopathy requires platelet and clotting evidence, and prolonged tourniquet use tends to raise rather than lower potassium.
  84. The correct answer is C (Catecholamine release from a phaeochromocytoma causes severe vasoconstriction): An adrenal tumour with episodic headache, palpitations, sweating and severe hypertension fits a catecholamine-secreting phaeochromocytoma. Anaesthetic drugs and tumour handling can trigger abrupt release and extreme vasoconstriction. Cortisol does not cause acute vasoconstriction, and aldosterone does not usually produce the acute pressor response described. Thyroid hormone surge lacks the episodic adrenal syndrome, while carcinoid mediator release causes flushing and hypotension rather than this presentation.
  85. The correct answer is C (The abducens nerve, which lies centrally beside the internal carotid artery.): It supplies lateral rectus, so injury limits abduction of the eye. The oculomotor, trochlear, and trigeminal branches lie in the lateral wall rather than this central position. The optic nerve is not a cavernous sinus content. Facial and hypoglossal nerves move the face and tongue and do not explain this eye movement deficit.
  86. The correct answer is D (Acute haemolytic reaction with fever, shock, and haemoglobinuria): Fever, rigors, hypotension, breathlessness, abdominal pain, and dark urine soon after transfusion fit an acute haemolytic reaction. Stop the transfusion, maintain the line with saline, call for help, monitor closely, and report the reaction through the approved pathway. A febrile nonhaemolytic reaction does not usually cause shock or dark urine. Allergy, contamination, and simple overload do not explain this combined pattern.
  87. The correct answer is E (The ischial spine, where the nerve lies medial to the lesser sciatic foramen.): The pudendal nerve crosses the ischial spine and then enters the lesser sciatic foramen. This landmark guides transvaginal and perineal approaches to the nerve. The pubic tubercle, coccyx, acetabular rim, and anterior superior iliac spine do not mark this crossing. Blocking near the correct ischial landmark improves anaesthesia of the perineum and external anal sphincter.
  88. The correct answer is A (Keloid scar growing beyond the original wound edge and recurring): Extension beyond the original injury plus recurrence and thick collagen bundles is characteristic of a keloid. The scar grows into surrounding tissue rather than remaining confined to the puncture. A hypertrophic scar usually stays within the original boundary. Scar contracture shortens tissue, a serosal adhesion is internal, and granulation tissue represents an earlier healing stage rather than a mature overgrown scar.
  89. The correct answer is E (Divide appropriately, keep required tissue fresh, and label each portion clearly): Lymphoma diagnosis may require fresh cells for flow cytometry or molecular tests and fixed tissue for architecture and routine stains. Dividing and labelling portions preserves material for both cellular and architectural studies. Fixing every cell prevents live-cell testing. Freezing all tissue can impair morphology, saline without a request lacks a testing plan, and crushing destroys relationships while wasting tissue.
  90. The correct answer is E (Median nerve compression within the carpal tunnel beneath the flexor retinaculum.): The median nerve supplies sensation to the radial three and a half digits and the recurrent branch supplies the thenar muscles. Compression causes night-time numbness and weak thumb abduction while sparing little finger sensation. Ulnar nerve compression affects the little finger and intrinsic hand muscles differently. Radial and posterior interosseous nerve lesions produce extensor rather than flexor or thenar deficits.
  91. The correct answer is A (Retinacular branches of the medial circumflex femoral artery.): These branches travel along the femoral neck and provide most adult blood flow to the femoral head. Displaced intracapsular fractures can tear them and cause avascular necrosis. The artery within the ligament of the head supplies only a small area. Lateral circumflex, perforating, and inferior gluteal branches do not provide the main intraosseous supply described here.
  92. The correct answer is A (Repeat the prophylactic dose at the locally recommended interval): A long operation and substantial blood loss can reduce protective tissue antibiotic levels. The correct action is to follow the locally specified repeat interval and dose rather than assuming one preoperative dose is enough. A full treatment course is unnecessary when no infection is established. Waiting for fever, changing class without advice, or stopping prophylaxis risks inadequate cover.
  93. The correct answer is D (Thyroid storm with excessive hormone action and increased adrenergic sensitivity): Untreated thyrotoxicosis combined with surgery can produce fever, marked tachycardia, agitation, labile pressure and gastrointestinal symptoms. The absence of an infection source supports a thyroid crisis rather than sepsis. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome usually causes rigidity, and malignant hyperthermia typically begins during or soon after volatile anaesthetic exposure with rigidity. Panic lacks the severe metabolic disturbance, while serotonin syndrome does not fit the medication exposure.
  94. The correct answer is A (Check the interaction profile and monitor INR while reviewing his diet and medicines): Warfarin effect can change with antibiotics, infection, reduced intake and altered liver function or vitamin K exposure. INR monitoring and medicine review let the team adjust treatment safely while considering the valve indication. Stopping warfarin for the entire course without review removes necessary anticoagulation. Ignoring INR changes misses the interaction, routine vitamin K lacks an indication, and switching to aspirin does not protect a mechanical valve.
  95. The correct answer is E (Neurogenic shock requiring careful vasopressor and heart rate support): A high spinal injury can interrupt sympathetic outflow, causing vasodilatation with hypotension and an inappropriately slow pulse. Bleeding must still be excluded, but warm skin and a limited fluid response after trauma assessment support neurogenic shock. Tension pneumothorax and tamponade usually produce different respiratory or venous findings, and immediate laparotomy without evidence of abdominal bleeding is unsafe. Adrenal crisis is not the most likely acute cause after cervical trauma.
  96. The correct answer is C (Pain and supine posture reduce functional residual capacity and promote atelectasis): Upper abdominal surgery, obesity, opioid analgesia and lying flat all reduce lung volumes and encourage dependent alveolar collapse. Reduced basal sounds and a low-volume chest radiograph fit this process. Pulmonary embolism can cause hypoxaemia but does not explain the radiographic low volumes. Aspiration contradicts the described film, oxygen therapy does not lower ventilation, and wound infection lacks fever or inflammatory signs.
  97. The correct answer is A (The long thoracic nerve, which supplies serratus anterior.): Injury causes serratus anterior weakness and gives the scapula a winged appearance during forward pressure. The nerve runs along the lateral chest wall and is vulnerable during axillary clearance. Intercostobrachial injury causes sensory loss, while thoracodorsal injury weakens latissimus dorsi. Radial and axillary nerve injuries produce different patterns of arm weakness.
  98. The correct answer is D (The presacral venous plexus lying outside the mesorectal envelope.): These fragile veins lie against the sacrum and can retract into bone, making bleeding difficult to control. Staying within the mesorectal plane protects them during rectal cancer surgery. The superior, middle, and inferior rectal veins have different drainage and are not the main presacral source. The internal iliac vein lies laterally and is not injured by excessive posterior sacral dissection.
  99. The correct answer is B (Opioid induced respiratory depression causing inadequate alveolar ventilation): Drowsiness, very slow shallow breathing, and hypoxia after morphine fit opioid-induced respiratory depression. Give oxygen, call for urgent help, support ventilation, monitor closely, and use the locally approved reversal plan. Pneumonia, embolism, leak, and low glucose do not fit the slow respiratory rate after morphine as closely. The clear temporal relation to an opioid makes respiratory depression the leading diagnosis.
  100. The correct answer is B (The scaphoid has retrograde blood flow entering mainly from its distal side.): A proximal pole fracture can interrupt flow after blood has already entered distally, risking avascular necrosis and nonunion. Tenderness in the anatomical snuffbox over the scaphoid warrants immobilisation even when early films are normal. The scaphoid is not supplied entirely through Guyon canal or by synovial diffusion alone. Equal proximal and distal arterial entry would reduce rather than explain this specific risk.
